FOUND PREPARER’S NOTE

This was typed from a 1907 edition published by Cassell and Company, Limited.

DEDICATION

This faithful but unpretending record of a remarkable adventure is hereby respectfully dedicated by the narrator, ALLAN QUATERMAIN, to all the big and little boys who read it.

AUTHOR’S NOTE

The author takes this quiet moment—as one might step aside from a bright, clattering dining-room into a cool pantry where the scents of pepper, dried herbs, and stored fruit hang in the dark—to thank his readers for the generous welcome they have given to the successive editions of this tale over the last twelve years. He hopes that in its present shape it may pass into still more hands, and that, in years to come, it will keep its savour for those who remain young enough at heart to hunger for a story of treasure, war, and wild adventure.

Ditchingham, 11 March, 1898.

POST SCRIPTUM

Now, in 1907, as this edition is set forth again, I can add only this: it pleases me to know the romance has continued to satisfy so many, not as a gaudy sweet sold at a fair, but as a plain, sustaining dish whose flavour deepens when it is warmed and served a second time—yes, even when it reaches the table of another household altogether.

The curious thing is that imagination has been verified by fact. The King Solomon’s Mines that I once dreamed of have indeed been found, and they are giving up their gold once more, and, by the latest reports, their diamonds also. The Kukuanas—or, more truly, the Matabele—have been subdued by the white man’s bullets; the loud, harsh sound of that mastery has travelled farther than any song, and where it passes, older words and older ways grow faint, like a dialect spoken lower and lower until even the children cannot catch it. Yet still, despite the din of modern doings and the coarse advertising of wonder, there seem to be many who find pleasure in these simple pages.

May they continue to do so—even to the third and fourth generation, or perhaps longer still—would, I am sure, have been the hope of our old and departed friend, Allan Quatermain, who knew better than most that what a man has lived cannot be perfectly translated into what he can tell, and that some part of the taste and sound of it must always remain his alone.

H. Rider Haggard.

Ditchingham, 1907.

Now that these pages have gone from my hands into the press—like a pot set on the coals that must boil to the end, whether the cook be proud of it or not—I feel the weight of what is missing from them, and of what in them is poorly seasoned. A man can taste, even before he serves, where the broth is thin.

As to the matter of it, this writing makes no claim to be the full dish of all we did and saw in Kukuanaland. There were things on that journey I would gladly have let simmer longer, so that their flavour might come out, and yet here they are scarcely more than a breath over the pot. I think first of the old tales I gathered—tales told not in my tongue, but in theirs, with meanings that cling like smoke to the thatch and will not be carried home whole—about the chain armour that turned death from us in the great battle of Loo; and also of the “Silent Ones,” the Colossi that keep their watch at the mouth of the stalactite cave. Those stories were not recited like a printed paragraph. They came at evening, when the cattle were penned and the women moved about their fires, and the night was full of little living sounds—the click of beadwork, the thin cry of a night-bird, the far drum that is not music for sale but a signal, a heartbeat of the place itself. There are meanings in such telling that do not travel well into English, for English is a dominant knife: sharp, useful, and too ready to cut away what it cannot name.

Had I listened only to my own bent, I should also have written at length on the differences between the Zulu and Kukuana dialects—differences that struck me as suggestive, not merely of speech, but of how a people remembers itself. Some words in one tongue lie heavy and old as mealies stored through a dry season; in another tongue, the same notion is spoken lightly, as if it were new-gathered. And there are turns of phrase that cannot be translated without loss, for they do not only mean—they belong. A man’s identity sits inside his native sounds as surely as a child sits inside its mother’s hut; and when those sounds are driven out by louder, imported noise, something goes hungry that no foreign bread quite satisfies.

A few pages, too, might have been profitably given to the flora and fauna of that country: to the bulbs that push up after rain as if the earth were boiling with life, and to the antelope—eight varieties of them I had never before set eyes on—moving with a silence that makes our clumsy boot-noise seem like blasphemy. There are lands where the ear governs the mind. In such places you learn what it is to live without the endless grinding and hammering that modern folk now take for the normal weather of the world. Where there is no iron shriek of machinery and no thin manufactured din poured day and night into the skull, the spirit rests differently; you can hear yourself think, and you can hear another man’s approach long before you see his face. It is a kind of health, and it is being stolen from us in towns, where even what they call “art” is often only noise cleverly packaged and sold.

Then there is the subject that most tempts me, and that in these pages has only been touched as one might touch a hot pot-lid and draw back—the magnificent military organisation of the Kukuanas. In my judgment it is far superior to that established by Chaka in Zululand, for it allows even more rapid mobilisation and does not require that pernicious system of enforced celibacy. But to set that system down properly would mean showing it in its daily life, not only in its parade: how the regiments are fed and housed, how discipline is braided into common routine as neatly as grass into a sleeping-mat, how orders pass not as shouted confusion but as a controlled soundscape—call, answer, silence—so that a nation can move like one body and yet keep its breath.

Lastly, I have scarcely spoken at all of the domestic and family customs of the Kukuanas, many of them exceedingly quaint; nor of their skill in smelting and welding metals. They carry that craft to a remarkable perfection. A good instance is found in their “tollas,” those heavy throwing-knives: the backs made of hammered iron, the edges of beautiful steel welded on to the iron frames with great nicety, as though a man had joined two different natures into one weapon and made the seam vanish. I should have liked to tell more of the household fires where such work is spoken of, and of the ways food and family and labour hold together a people’s memory when written history is not their storehouse. But I did not.

The truth is, I thought, with Sir Henry Curtis and Captain Good, that the best plan would be to set down the story plainly and straightforwardly, and leave these other matters to be handled later, in whatever way may at last seem desirable. In the meantime I shall be glad, of course, to give any information I possess to anyone interested in such things.

And now it only remains for me to offer apologies for my blunt manner of writing. I am more used to the feel of a rifle in my hands than a pen, and cannot pretend to the grand flights and flourishes that I see in novels—for sometimes I like to read a novel. I suppose such flourishes are desired, and I regret I cannot supply them; yet I cannot help thinking that simple things are often the most impressive, and that books are easier to understand when they are written in plain language, though perhaps I have no right to set up an opinion on the matter.

“A sharp spear,” runs the Kukuana saying, “needs no polish”; and on the same principle I venture to hope that a true story, however strange, does not require to be dressed out in fine words.

ALLAN QUATERMAIN.

It is an odd trade for a man of my years—fifty-five last birthday—to set his hand to paper and try to make a history out of what has mostly been dust, sweat, gunpowder, and bad water. A history ought to have the comfort of a hearth in it: a pot that simmers to its conclusion, a table laid, the same faces returning like seasons. My own life has been more like a trek-knife—always out, always cleaned and put away again, never quite done with blood.

And yet here I am in Durban, with my left leg aching as if the lion’s tooth were still in it. I have been trading, hunting, fighting, and mining since I was scarcely old enough to know the taste of my own bread from another man’s. Only eight months ago I made what folks call a “pile”—how large I cannot rightly say, for money has a way of slipping into corners, like mealie-meal in a wagon-crack. But I would not go through the last fifteen or sixteen months again for it; not even if an angel promised me safe passage out the far end. I am a timid man, and I do not relish violence, and I have grown near sick of adventure.

Then why write?

Because Sir Henry Curtis and Captain John Good asked me.

Because my leg keeps me housebound and short-tempered, so that if I do not give my thoughts a road to travel they will trample my mind like oxen in a mealie field. Ever since that confounded lion got hold of me I have had this trouble. Wounds close, and yet, when the weather turns to the very season of the mauling, they wake again and burn. There must be something in a lion’s mouth besides teeth—some poison or memory that will not die. It is a hard thing, too, when one has shot five-and-sixty lions, that the sixty-sixth should take to chewing one’s leg as if it were tobacco. It is disorderly, and I am an orderly man; I like the routine of the world to keep its promises.

And because I want my boy Harry—over there in London, in the hospital, learning to cut and stitch and name what is inside a man—to have something with more life in it than dead flesh and disinfectant. There are smells in a hospital that cling like wet cloth: carbolic, boiled linen, cold metal. A young fellow needs, now and then, the scent of open air and a story that bites back.

Lastly—because this is the strangest tale I know. There is no woman in it to speak of, unless you count Foulata; and Gagaoola—if she was a woman, and not a wicked old spirit with skin stretched over bones—was nearer a hundred than anything decent, so I do not count her either. There is not a petticoat in the whole business. It is mostly men, thirst, old names, and the kind of silence that makes you hear your own blood moving.

That is my yoke, and it sits stiff on the neck. But “sutjes, sutjes,” as the Boers say—softly does it. A strong team will pull through in the end, if the oxen are not poor. You can do nothing with poor oxen, and you can do nothing with a poor tongue either.

I was tempted to begin like a deposition—“I, Allan Quatermain, of Durban, Natal, Gentleman, make oath and say”—the same heading I used before a magistrate about poor Khiva and Ventvögel’s deaths. But a book is not a court, and besides, what is a gentleman? I have known natives who were gentlemen in the only sense that matters, and I have known white men fresh from home, with money in their belts and hardness in their mouths, who were not. The words we use are often the whips we do not notice in our hands. I do not like certain old names people fling about as if they were harmless; they sour the mouth, and once a man learns to speak of another as less than a man, the next step comes too easily. Still, I was born a gentleman, and I have tried—God knows I have tried—to behave like one, though I have lived most of my days as a wandering trader and hunter.

I have killed men, yes. But never for sport, never for pleasure, never for the mere taste of power. Only when there was no other way to keep my own life or another’s. The Almighty gave us breath; I suppose He meant us to defend it. I have never stolen, either—though once I cheated a Kaffir out of cattle, and the thing has sat on my conscience like bad meal. He had done me a dirty turn, true, but a wrong does not season a wrong into right; it only makes a stew you cannot swallow without remembering what went into it.

It was about eighteen months ago that I first met Sir Henry Curtis and Captain Good. I had been beyond Bamangwato elephant-hunting and had the kind of luck that turns a man’s stomach: spoor that vanished like smoke, waterholes that tasted of rot, and then fever—hot as a forge in the bones, cold as river stones in the sweat. When I was fit to sit a wagon without falling off, I trekked down to the Diamond Fields, sold what ivory I had, sold the wagon and oxen too, paid off my hunters, and took the post-cart to the Cape.

Cape Town is a place where a man can be overcharged for a bed and still be told, politely, that he ought to be grateful. I stayed a week, saw the botanical gardens—useful, those, for there is more future in a seed than in a speech—and saw the new Houses of Parliament, which I fancy will grow plenty of talk and little else, like mealies planted in sand.

Then I booked myself on the Dunkeld to Natal. She lay at the docks, waiting for the Edinburgh Castle due in from England. I took my berth, went aboard, and that afternoon the Natal passengers from the Edinburgh Castle transhipped. We weighed and put to sea.

Among those who came aboard were two men who caught my eye at once, not because they sought attention, but because the world itself seemed to make room around them.

One was about thirty, and built as if the Creator had begun with a broad chest and long arms and only afterwards remembered the rest. Yellow hair, thick yellow beard, grey eyes set deep, and a face cut clean as if from a hard-grained wood. He looked like one of those old sea-raiders you see in pictures—men with horn cups in their fists and iron on their shoulders, laughing at storms. I thought of a modern Dane I once met who cheated me of ten pounds, and then of an ancient Dane I’d only ever seen in an illustration—white Zulus, I called them in my mind, for what else are they but a nation of tall fighters, only born under a colder sky? As he stood there by the companion-ladder, I could almost smell tar and salt and hear the hollow boom of a drum in some long-ago hall. Afterwards I learned that his blood did run Danish, which is a curious thing; a man’s past will speak through his bones even when his mouth is shut.

The other man was stout, darker, and so neat that he looked as if he had been shaved and polished like a parade gun. I knew, without knowing why, that he was Navy. Perhaps it was the set of his shoulders—as if he measured space by deck-planks—or the way he took the ship’s movement into his body, instead of fighting it. I have gone shooting with several naval men in my time, and for all their fondness for language that would blister paint, I have found them the best fellows alive. If anyone asks me again what a gentleman is, I shall answer: a Royal Naval officer is, in a general way—though there are black sheep everywhere. I believe it is the wide sea and the wind of God that scours them clean and keeps their minds from turning small.

His name, I found from the passenger list, was Good—Captain John Good. He was medium height and broad, clean-shaven to the last hair, and wore an eye-glass in his right eye that seemed to belong there like an extra organ. There was no string to it; he only removed it to wipe it, as carefully as a man wiping the rim of a good wineglass. Later I discovered he did not sleep in it—as I first suspected—but put it in his trouser pocket at night, along with his false teeth. He had two beautiful sets, which, considering my own are no great affair, has often tempted me into the tenth commandment.

By evening the weather turned dirty. A keen breeze sprang up off land and brought with it one of those Scotch mists that do not fall so much as press themselves into your face and clothes, dampening everything—rope, wood, skin, temper. The Dunkeld is a flat-bottomed punt, and going up light she rolled like a drunken man trying to walk a straight line. It seemed she would go clean over, yet she never did. There is a sort of stubbornness in ships, the way there is in old oxen: they will lurch and groan, but they will keep their feet.

Walking was impossible, so I stood near the engines where it was warm. The thump and hiss there had a steady comfort to it—an honest sound, like a mortar and pestle in a kitchen at dawn. A ship has its own music: the deep breathing of steam, the slap of water, the complaining creak of timber. Out at sea, away from carts and stamping yards and the harsh clatter of town-work, those sounds settle a man. There is less of the modern racket that makes thinking difficult—no endless hammering, no shriek of iron, no cheap music shouted at you by streets. The ocean keeps its own order, and if you listen properly you can hear where you are in the world.

Opposite me a pendulum was fixed, swinging back and forth as the vessel rolled, marking the angle at each lurch.

“That pendulum’s wrong; it is not properly weighted,” said a testy voice at my shoulder.

I turned and saw Captain Good.

“Indeed,” I said, “and what makes you think so?”

“Think so? I don’t think at all,” he snapped. “Why, there—” as she righted herself after a roll—“if the ship had really rolled to the degree that thing pointed to, she would never have rolled again, that’s all. But it’s just like these merchant skippers; they are always so confoundedly careless.”

At that moment the dinner-bell rang, and I was not sorry. It is a dreadful thing to hear a naval man let himself loose on merchant skippers, unless you are equally fond of hearing merchant skippers speak candidly of naval men. Captain Good and I went down together, and found Sir Henry already seated. He and Good were placed together, and I sat opposite.

The saloon smelled of damp wool and varnished wood, with the stew’s heavy warmth trying to do battle against sea-air that had crept in on everyone’s coats. There is a particular sound at a ship’s dinner: cutlery clicking in time with the roll, glasses sliding a hair’s breadth and being steadied, men’s voices rising and falling like waves. It is a small domestic world afloat, pretending it is not surrounded by deep water.

Good and I soon fell into talk—shooting, veldt, beasts—and he asked questions in his quick way, the way a man checks the knots in a rope: tug, tug, tug, to see where it will give. I answered as well as I could. Presently he got onto elephants.

“Ah, sir,” called out somebody near me, “you’ve reached the right man for that. Hunter Quatermain should be able to tell you about elephants if anybody can.”

Sir Henry, who had been listening quietly, started as if the name were a hand laid sudden on his shoulder.

“Excuse me, sir,” he said, leaning forward, his voice low and deep, as suited his great lungs. “Excuse me, but is your name Allan Quatermain?”

I said that it was.

He made no further remark, but I heard him mutter “fortunate” into his beard, as though he had been walking in the dark and found a door.

After dinner, as we were leaving the saloon, Sir Henry strolled up and asked if I would come to his cabin and smoke a pipe. I accepted, and he led the way to the Dunkeld deck cabin—a very good one. It had once been two cabins, but when Sir Garnet Wolseley, or one of those big swells, travelled down the coast, they knocked out the partition and never put it up again. There was a sofa, and a little table in front of it. Sir Henry sent the steward for a bottle of whisky.

We sat down, lit our pipes, and the lamp was lit too, the flame steadying itself against the ship’s motion like a patient cook coaxing a reluctant fire.

“Mr. Quatermain,” said Sir Henry, when the steward had gone, “the year before last about this time, you were, I believe, at a place called Bamangwato, to the north of the Transvaal.”

“I was,” I answered, surprised that a gentleman should be so well acquainted with my movements, which I had never considered worthy of notice beyond the price of ivory.

“You were trading there, were you not?” put in Captain Good.

“I was. I took up a wagon-load of goods, made a camp outside the settlement, and stopped till I had sold them.”

Sir Henry sat opposite in a Madeira chair, his great arms resting on the table. He lifted his eyes and fixed them on my face. There was anxiety there—something held tight behind the grey.

“Did you happen to meet a man called Neville there?”

“Oh yes,” I said. “He outspanned alongside of me for a fortnight, resting his oxen before going on to the interior. I had a letter from a lawyer some months back asking if I knew what had become of him, and I answered as best I could.”

“Yes,” said Sir Henry. “Your letter was forwarded to me. You said the gentleman called Neville left Bamangwato at the beginning of May in a wagon, with a driver, a voorlooper, and a Kaffir hunter called Jim. You said he meant to trek, if possible, as far as Inyati—the extreme trading post in the Matabele country—sell his wagon there, and go on foot. You also said he did sell the wagon, because six months after you saw it in the possession of a Portuguese trader, who told you he had bought it at Inyati from a white man whose name he had forgotten; and that he believed the white man and his native servant had gone off into the interior on a shooting trip.”

“Yes,” I said again.

There was a pause in which you could hear the ship’s bones talking: the creak of wood, the deep pulse of engines, the faint rattle of a glass on the table as she rolled. A man’s secrets often come out in pauses; speech is only the knife, silence is the board you cut on.

“Mr. Quatermain,” Sir Henry said suddenly, “I suppose you know or can guess nothing more of the reasons of my—of Mr. Neville’s journey northward, or as to what point that journey was directed?”

“I heard something,” I began, and stopped. It was not a subject I cared to season with talk. Some matters, once spoken aloud, are like certain native words—full of a taste you cannot translate, and once your mouth has shaped them you cannot pretend you did not.

Sir Henry and Captain Good looked at each other. Good nodded once, sharply, as if giving a command.

“Mr. Quatermain,” Sir Henry went on, “I am going to tell you a story, and ask your advice—and perhaps your assistance. The agent who forwarded me your letter told me I might rely on it implicitly, as you were,” he said, choosing the phrase carefully, “well known and universally respected in Natal, and especially noted for your discretion.”

I bowed and took whisky and water, partly to hide my confusion. I am a modest man, and praise sits awkwardly on me, like a borrowed coat.

“My brother,” said Sir Henry, and his voice thickened on the word, “Mr. Neville was my brother.”

“Oh,” I said, and started; for now I knew whom he had reminded me of the first moment I saw him. Neville had been smaller, darker-bearded, but the eyes—those grey eyes with the same keen look—were the same; and the bones beneath the face were the same family’s.

“He was,” Sir Henry continued, “my only and younger brother, and until five years ago I do not suppose we were ever a month apart. But about five years ago a misfortune befell us, as sometimes happens in families. We quarrelled bitterly, and I behaved unjustly to my brother in my anger.”

Captain Good nodded his head vigorously to himself, so that I noticed it even as the ship gave a big roll and the looking-glass opposite us swung up till it was almost over our heads.

“As you may know,” Sir Henry went on, “if a man dies intestate and has no property but land—real property, it is called in England—it descends to the eldest son. It so happened that when we quarrelled our father died intestate. He had put off making his will until it was too late. The result was that my brother, who had not been brought up to any profession, was left without a penny. Of course it would have been my duty to provide for him, but at the time the quarrel was so bitter that I did not—” he drew breath, and the lamp made a small, steady halo on his beard “—to my shame I say it—offer to do anything. Not that I grudged him justice; but I waited for him to make advances, and he made none.”

“I am sorry to trouble you with this,” he added, “but I must, to make things clear—eh, Good?”

“Quite so, quite so,” said Captain Good. “Mr. Quatermain will, I am sure, keep this history to himself.”

“Of course,” I said. Discretion is like salt: it ought to be there, but if you can taste it, there is too much.

“Well,” Sir Henry continued, “my brother had a few hundred pounds at his bank. Without saying anything to me, he drew out that paltry sum, took the name of Neville, and started for South Africa in the wild hope of making a fortune. I learned this afterwards. Three years passed, and I heard nothing of him, though I wrote several times. Doubtless the letters never reached him. But as time went on I grew more and more troubled. I found out, Mr. Quatermain, that blood is thicker than water.”

“That’s true,” I said, thinking of my boy Harry and the tug a father feels even across oceans.

“I found out,” Sir Henry said, “that I would have given half my fortune to know that my brother George—the only relation I possess—was safe and well, and that I should see him again.”

“But you never did, Curtis,” Captain Good broke in, quickly, glancing at Sir Henry’s face as though to steady him by naming the hurt.

Sir Henry’s mouth tightened. Then he went on.

“As time passed I became more anxious to learn if my brother was alive or dead; and if alive, to bring him home. I set enquiries on foot, and your letter was one result. So far as it went it was satisfactory—it showed that until lately George was alive—but it did not go far enough. So, to cut the matter short, I made up my mind to come out and look for him myself, and Captain Good was so kind as to come with me.”

“Yes,” said Good. “Nothing else to do, you see. Turned out by my Lords of the Admiralty to starve on half pay, with the barren honour of a commander’s rank because it was ‘impossible’ I should be promoted. And now perhaps, sir—” and he swung his eye-glass a fraction, fixing me with it “—you will tell us what you know or have heard of the gentleman called Neville.”

“What was it you heard at Bamangwato about my brother’s journey?” Sir Henry asked, when I paused to tamp down the tobacco in my pipe. The ember caught; the smoke came sweet and sharp, like sun-warmed veld and cured leaf.

“I heard this,” I said, and the words tasted of dust and distance. “And I’ve not spoken it to any living soul till to-day. I heard he was starting for Solomon’s Mines.”

“Solomon’s Mines?” Captain Good and Sir Henry said together, as though the name itself had snapped like a whip in the air.

“Where are they?” Good demanded.

“I don’t know,” I answered. “I know where they’re said to be. Once I saw the peaks of the mountains that border them, blue as iron in the heat-haze; but there was a hundred and thirty miles of thirsty desert between me and those teeth of rock. And I’m not aware any white man ever got across it, save one. Still—perhaps the best I can do is tell you the legend as I know it. But first: you’ll give me your word not to repeat a syllable of it, without my leave. I’ve my reasons for asking.”

Sir Henry’s head dipped in assent; his eyes didn’t.

“Certainly, certainly,” said Good, quick as a man who smells a story the way a cook smells yeast rising.

“Well,” I began, “elephant hunters are mostly rough—men who don’t set store by anything but powder, water, and the ways of Kafirs. Yet now and then you meet one who listens—who stoops, like a careful gleaner after harvest, and gathers old talk from old mouths. He’ll sit by a cooking-fire and take down a proverb as though it were a rare spice, because he knows you can’t buy such things with money later, once the tongue that holds them is dead.

“It was such a man who first gave me Solomon’s Mines—near thirty years ago. His name was Evans. He was killed the following year by a wounded buffalo and lies near the Zambesi Falls. I can hear even now the night he told me: the mopane logs popping like small rifles as their sap burst, the far cough of a lion, and the steady, comforting music of men chewing mealies and talking low—sound that keeps a camp human.

“I was speaking to Evans of certain workings I’d found while hunting koodoo and eland in what’s now Lydenburg district. There’s a broad wagon-road cut out of solid rock, clean as if a giant chisel had shaved it; it runs to the mouth of a gallery. Inside were stacks of gold quartz piled ready for roasting—set out with the neatness of a cook laying firewood for baking, which shows the workers left in a hurry, abandoning their work mid-recipe, as it were. And about twenty paces in, the gallery is built across with masonry—fine work, squared and fitted so tight you’d struggle to press a knife-blade between the stones.

“‘Ay,’ Evans said, looking into the coals as if he read there, ‘but I’ll spin you a queerer yarn than that.’ And he told me of a ruined city in the far interior, which he believed to be Ophir of the Bible. Others, more learned, have said the same since. I listened open-eared—I was young, and the thought of ancient hands drawing treasure from a land now fallen into darkness took hold of my imagination as smoke takes hold of cloth.

“Then, sudden as a jackal’s yelp, Evans said, ‘Lad, did you ever hear of the Suliman Mountains, away to the north-west of the Mushakulumbwe country?’

“I told him no.

“‘Ah,’ he said, ‘that’s where Solomon really had his mines—his diamond mines, I mean.’

“‘How do you know that?’ I asked.

“‘Know it? Why, what is “Suliman” but Solomon with the edges worn off by other men’s mouths? Besides,’ he went on, ‘an old Isanusi—witch doctoress—up in Manica told me. She said the people beyond those mountains were a branch of the Zulus, speaking a dialect of Zulu—same root, different flavour, like sorghum beer brewed in a new pot. Finer and bigger men even. And among them live great wizards who learned their art from white men when “all the world was dark,” and they have the secret of a mine of bright stones.’

“I laughed at it then—though it clung to me, the way a strong smell clings to a blanket. Diamonds weren’t found yet at the Fields, and the whole thing seemed no more solid than campfire smoke. Then Evans went off and was killed, and for twenty years I never thought on it again.

“Yet twenty years later—long enough for a child to grow and a man to go grey—I heard something more. I was up beyond Manica at a place called Sitanda’s Kraal, a miserable hole where even hunger seemed to have settled and built itself a hut. You could get next to nothing to eat; no proper meal to anchor a man—only stringy fowl and sour mealie porridge doled out grudgingly, while the flies sang over it like a curse. There was little game. I had fever and lay about in that half-world where sound comes and goes as if through water: the thin crying of women, the dull clack of pestles in mortars, and, underneath, my own pulse drumming too loud in my ears.

“One day a Portuguese arrived with one companion, a half-breed. Now, I know the low-class Delagoa Portugee well enough—no greater devil unhung, living on the misery of others in the shape of slaves. But this man was different: tall and thin, large dark eyes, and curling grey moustachios; he had the air of the polite doms you read about, the kind who can bow even when they mean to cut your throat later.

“We spoke awhile—his broken English and my little Portuguese fitting together poorly, like mismatched crockery. He said his name was José Silvestre and that he had a place near Delagoa Bay.

“When he left next day with his half-breed, he took off his hat as though he stood in a Lisbon doorway instead of African dust.

“‘Good-bye, señor,’ he said. ‘If ever we meet again I shall be the richest man in the world, and I will remember you.’

“I was too weak to laugh much, but I did. And I watched him strike out for the great desert to the west, wondering if he was mad, or what hunger of the mind was driving him—because men will cross whole countries for what they cannot eat, while refusing to cross a river for a pot of good stew.

“A week passed and I beat the fever down. One evening I sat on the ground before my little tent, chewing the last leg of a miserable fowl I’d bought from a native for a strip of cloth worth twenty fowls. The skin was tough and tasted of smoke and old grease, but I worried it like a dog because that’s what hunger teaches you. The sun went down red as a forge over the desert; the air held its heat and the insects’ whining seemed to write lines around my head.

“Then I saw a figure on the rising ground opposite—about three hundred yards off—apparently a European, for it wore a coat. It crawled on hands and knees, got up, staggered a few yards, fell, crawled again—like a man trying to climb out of the world by inches.

“I sent one of my hunters. Presently he came in with the stranger—what was left of him.

“Who do you suppose it was?”

“José Silvestre,” said Good.

“Yes. José Silvestre—or rather his skeleton with a little skin stretched over it. His face was bright yellow with bilious fever; his eyes looked too big for his skull, as if they’d eaten the rest of him. Only parchment-skin, white hair, and bones that stuck up sharp beneath.

“‘Water! for the sake of Christ, water!’ he moaned. His lips were split; his tongue, thrust between them, was swollen and blackish.

“I gave him water with a little milk in it—milk, thin and precious there—and he drank in great gulps, two quarts or more, without drawing breath, as if he meant to swallow the whole desert back down. I would not let him take more. Then fever caught him again and he raved—Suliman’s Mountains, diamonds, the desert—words broken and repeated until they were nothing but sound.

“I carried him into my tent and did what I could, which was little. I knew how it must end. About eleven he grew quieter. I lay down for a short rest and slept.

“At dawn I woke, and in the half-light saw Silvestre sitting up—gaunt as a fencepost—and gazing out towards the west. The air was cold enough to make the canvas sweat. Then the first ray of sun shot across the wide plain and struck the far crest of one of the tallest Suliman peaks, more than a hundred miles away. The light touched it like a finger pointing.

“‘There it is!’ he cried in Portuguese, thrusting out his long thin arm. ‘But I shall never reach it—never. No one will ever reach it!’

“Then he stopped, and you could hear the camp waking: a child’s cough, the rattle of a pot, a bird testing its voice. He seemed to gather himself as if tying a knot in the last of his strength.

“‘Friend,’ he said, turning his head with effort, ‘are you there? My eyes grow dark.’

“‘Yes,’ I answered. ‘Yes. Lie down now and rest.’

“‘Ay,’ he said, ‘I shall rest soon. I have time to rest—all eternity. Listen: I am dying. You have been good to me. I will give you the writing. Perhaps you will get there if you can live to pass the desert, which has killed my poor servant and me.’

“He groped inside his shirt and brought out what I took for a Boer tobacco pouch made of sable-skin—Swart-vet-pens. It was tied with a strip of hide, a rimpi. His fingers trembled; he couldn’t loosen it. He handed it to me.

“‘Untie it,’ he said.

“I did, and drew out a torn bit of yellow linen with rusty writing on it. Inside that rag was a paper.

“He spoke feebly—his voice thinning like broth stretched too far.

“‘The paper has all that is on the linen. It took me years to read. Listen: my ancestor, a political refugee from Lisbon, one of the first Portuguese who landed on these shores, wrote that when he was dying on those mountains no white foot ever pressed before or since. His name was José da Silvestra, three hundred years ago. His slave, who waited on this side, found him dead and brought the writing home to Delagoa. It has been in the family ever since, but none cared to read it till I did. I have lost my life over it—but another may succeed and become the richest man in the world—the richest man in the world. Only give it to no one, señor. Go yourself.’

“Then he wandered again, talking to shadows, and within an hour it was done.

“God rest him. He died quietly. I buried him deep, with big boulders on his breast so the jackals wouldn’t have him. Then I came away.”

Sir Henry leaned forward. “But the document?”

“Yes,” said Good. “What was in it?”

“Well, gentlemen—if you like, I’ll tell you. I’ve never shown it to anyone except a drunken Portuguese trader who translated it for me and had forgotten the whole business by morning. The original rag is at my home in Durban, with poor Dom José’s translation. But I have the English rendering in my pocket-book, and a facsimile of the map—if map it can be called. Here it is.”

And I read:

“I, José da Silvestra, who am now dying of hunger in the little cave where no snow is on the north side of the nipple of the southernmost of the two mountains I have named Sheba’s Breasts, write this in the year 1590 with a cleft bone upon a remnant of my raiment, my blood being the ink. If my slave should find it when he comes, and should bring it to Delagoa, let my friend (name illegible) bring the matter to the knowledge of the king, that he may send an army which, if they live through the desert and the mountains, and can overcome the brave Kukuanes and their devilish arts, to which end many priests should be brought, will make him the richest king since Solomon. With my own eyes I have seen the countless diamonds stored in Solomon’s treasure chamber behind the white Death; but through the treachery of Gagool the witch-finder I might bring nought away, scarcely my life. Let him who comes follow the map, and climb the snow of Sheba’s left breast till he reaches the nipple, on the north side of which is the great road Solomon made, from whence three days’ journey to the King’s Palace. Let him kill Gagool. Pray for my soul. Farewell.

JOSÉ DA SILVESTRA.”

When I finished and showed the copy of the map—drawn, so the tale went, by a dying hand with blood for ink—there was silence. Even the ship’s small noises seemed to step back a pace: the faint groan of wood, the distant clink of metal, the hush between waves.

“Well,” Captain Good said at last, “I’ve been round the world twice and put in at most ports, but may I be hung for a mutineer if ever I heard a yarn like that out of a story book—or in it either.”

“It’s a queer tale, Mr. Quatermain,” Sir Henry said slowly. “You’re not hoaxing us? It is sometimes thought allowable to take in a greenhorn.”

The words pricked me. I cannot abide being taken for one of those foolish fellows who think lying is wit.

“If you think that, Sir Henry,” I said, pocketing my paper, “there’s an end to the matter.” And I rose.

Sir Henry put his big hand on my shoulder—heavy, steady. “Sit down, Mr. Quatermain,” he said. “I beg your pardon. I see well enough you do not wish to deceive us. But it sounded so strange I could hardly believe it.”

“You shall see the original map and writing when we reach Durban,” I answered, mollified. For, indeed, it was not wonderful he should doubt. “But I’ve not told you about your brother.

“I knew the man Jim who went with him—a Bechuana by birth; a good hunter, and for a native, clever. The morning Mr. Neville was starting I saw Jim by my wagon cutting up tobacco on the disselboom. He worked the knife careful, making the leaf into fine, even shreds as though he respected it; the smell of it rose warm and familiar, a small comfort before a long trek.

“‘Jim,’ I said, ‘where are you off to this trip? Is it elephants?’

“‘No, Baas,’ he answered. ‘We are after something worth much more than ivory.’

“‘And what might that be?’ I asked, curious. ‘Is it gold?’

“‘No, Baas—something worth more than gold,’ and he grinned.

“I asked no more. A man doesn’t like to lower his dignity by seeming inquisitive, though my mind chewed at it like tough meat.

“When Jim finished his tobacco he said, ‘Baas—’

“I took no notice.

“‘Baas—’ again.

“‘Eh, boy, what is it?’ I asked.

“‘Baas, we are going after diamonds.’

“‘Diamonds! Then you are steering the wrong way. You should head for the Fields.’

“‘Baas, have you ever heard of Suliman’s Berg?’—Solomon’s Mountains, Sir Henry.

“‘Ay.’

“‘Have you ever heard of the diamonds there?’

“‘I have heard a foolish story, Jim.’

“‘It is no story, Baas. Once I knew a woman who came from there, and reached Natal with her child. She told me—she is dead now.’

“Now that, gentlemen, struck me more than Evans’s talk, because it came with the everyday weight of a man mentioning mealies or weather—no flourish, no selling. And there’s power in a thing said plain, in a tongue that has carried it from hearth to hearth. When a story lives by being spoken, it changes like soup in a pot—thickening, taking on the hand of each cook—until no man can swear what the first taste was. Yet still it feeds.

“‘Your master will feed the aasvögels, Jim,’ I said, ‘if he tries to reach Suliman’s country—and so will you, if they can pick anything off your worthless old carcass.’

“He grinned. ‘Mayhap, Baas. Man must die. I would rather like to try a new country myself; the elephants are getting worked out about here.’

“‘Ah, my boy,’ I said, ‘you wait till the “pale old man” gets a grip of your yellow throat, and then we shall hear what tune you sing.’

“Half an hour later I saw Neville’s wagon move off. Presently Jim came back running, dust on his shins, breath whistling.

“‘Good-bye, Baas,’ he said. ‘I didn’t like to start without bidding you good-bye, for I daresay you are right, and that we shall never trek south again.’

“‘Is your master really going to Suliman’s Berg, Jim, or are you lying?’

“‘No,’ he said. ‘He is going. He told me he was bound to make his fortune somehow, or try to; so he might as well have a fling for the diamonds.’

“‘Oh!’ I said. ‘Wait a bit, Jim. Will you take a note to your master and promise not to give it to him till you reach Inyati?’—a hundred miles off.

“‘Yes, Baas.’

“So I took a scrap of paper and wrote: ‘Let him who comes… climb the snow of Sheba’s left breast, till he reaches the nipple, on the north side of which is Solomon’s great road.’

“‘Now, Jim,’ I said, ‘when you give this to your master, tell him he had better follow the advice on it implicitly. You are not to give it to him now, because I don’t want him back asking me questions I won’t answer. Now be off, you idle fellow; the wagon is nearly out of sight.’

“He took the note and went. And that is all I know of your brother, Sir Henry; but I am much afraid—”

“Mr. Quatermain,” Sir Henry broke in, and his voice had the quiet hardness of a man who has already eaten his resolve and swallowed it. “I am going to look for my brother. I am going to trace him to Suliman’s Mountains, and over them if necessary, till I find him—or until I know that he is dead. Will you come with me?”

I am a cautious man—timid, if you like. The proposal chilled me as though someone had poured cold water down my back. To undertake such a journey seemed like stepping into the mouth of death; and besides, I had a son dependent on me. A man who has to put food into another’s mouth cannot gamble his life lightly, whatever romance may be served up with the tale.

“No, thank you, Sir Henry,” I said. “I think I would rather not. I am too old for wild-goose chases. We should only end like poor Silvestre. I have a son dependent on me, so I cannot afford to risk my life foolishly.”

Sir Henry and Captain Good looked disappointed—Good with the face of a man whose stew has been snatched off the fire before it’s finished.

“Mr. Quatermain,” Sir Henry said, “I am well off, and I am bent upon this business. You may put the remuneration for your services at whatever figure you like in reason, and it shall be paid over before we start. Moreover, I will arrange that if anything untoward happens to us—or to you—your son shall be suitably provided for. You will see from this how necessary I think your presence. Also, if by chance we reach this place and find diamonds, they shall belong to you and Good equally. I do not want them. That promise is worth nothing at all, of course; and the same would apply to any ivory. You may make your own terms with me, Mr. Quatermain. And I shall pay all expenses.”

“Sir Henry,” I answered, “it is the most liberal proposal I ever had, and not to be sneezed at by a poor hunter and trader. But it’s the biggest job I’ve come across, and I must take time to think it over. I will give you my answer before we get to Durban.”

“Very good,” he said.

Then I wished them good-night and turned in. And as the ship’s sounds wrapped themselves round my sleep—the soft insistence of water, the faint creak of timbers—I dreamt of long-dead Silvestre and the diamonds, bright as salt in the sun, locked away behind the white Death.

From the Cape to Durban is four or five days’ steaming, if the weather holds and the sea does not make a mock of your schedules. Sometimes a vessel hangs about off East London for a whole day because the surf will not let the cargo boats creep out—there is always talk of a grand harbour to be built there, and always money vanishing into it like mealie-meal into a hungry pot, but the sea has not yet been argued into obedience. This time, though, we were lucky. The Bar was quiet, the tugs came fussing out at once, and behind them dragged a string of ugly, flat-bottomed boats that looked as if they had been hammered together in a hurry and had never forgiven anybody for it.

The unloading began in the usual fashion: not with care, but with confidence. Cases were pitched into the boats as though they were sacks of sand. Over they went, slap-bang—china, cloth, tools, whatever the labels begged you to respect, all treated alike. I saw a case of champagne—four dozen bottles—burst open as it landed. The wine frothed up in the grime of the boat’s bilge, white bubbles boiling like porridge that has been left too long on the fire. It made me cross to see such waste. The Kafirs handling the boat thought so too, only their remedy for waste was immediate and practical: they fished out two bottles that had survived, knocked the necks off against the gunwale, and drank as if they were bailing out a leak.

They had not reckoned on the way champagne grows inside you. In a minute their bellies began to swell, and they rolled about on the planks, coughing and shouting that the stuff was tagati—bewitched. The sound of their panic carried up to our deck—high, startled cries against the steady breathing of the sea. I called down and told them it was the strongest medicine of the white man, and that they were as good as dead men. They stared at the froth in horror, like children who have stolen a pot from the fire and find it full of spirits. They went ashore trembling, and I don’t think those two will ever put their mouths to champagne again, bewitched or not.

All the while we steamed northward for Natal I was turning Sir Henry Curtis’s proposal over in my mind like a cook turning a strip of biltong above coals—testing it, watching what renders out, judging how much it will cost a man before it is fit to swallow. For a day or two we said little about it. I told them hunting yarns instead—true ones. There is no need to lie about hunting; the bush provides enough oddity without a man adding his own. Yet even as I spoke, part of me kept listening for the decision inside my own head, as one listens for a pot to come to the boil.

At last, on a January evening—our hottest month—we ran along the Natal coast, expecting to make Durban Point by sunset. It is a handsome shore from East London onwards: red sandhills, broad sweeps of green, kraals scattered like dark seeds, and a ribbon of surf that keeps throwing up white pillars where it hits the rocks. But near Durban there is an extra richness, as though the land has been fed and watered with more affection. The hills are cut into steep kloofs by centuries of rushing rain; the rivers wink and sparkle as they race down them. There is bush as God planted it—thick, deep green—and then the softer greens of mealie gardens and sugar patches, laid out like careful beds. Now and again a white house smiles down at the placid sea and makes the whole thing feel inhabited, as if beauty has been given a hearth and a table. I have lived too much in wilderness not to value that homely note, though civilisation has a way of driving off the game. Eden must have been fair before man, but I’ve always thought it must have been fairer when Eve was there to put it in order.

We misjudged our time. The sun was already low when we dropped anchor off the Point, and the signal gun boomed out to tell Durban the Mail had arrived. It was too late to think of crossing the Bar that night, so we went down comfortably to dinner after watching the Mail-bags taken off in the life-boat. When we came up again the moon was out, laying a bright, clean path over sea and shore, so strong that the lighthouse flashes seemed almost impatient against it. From land drifted those sweet, spicy smells—hot earth, leaf, flower, and the faint tang of cooking fires—that always put me in mind of hymns and missionaries and the way they try to make strange places smell like home. On the Berea the windows winked with a hundred lights. From a brig lying near us came the sailors’ singing as they worked their anchor, the voices rising and falling with the creak of timber and the slap of small waves against her side. It was one of those Southern African nights that make men softer without their knowing—silvered, musical, and peaceful. Even a great bulldog aboard, that had been yearning all voyage to come to grips with a baboon in a cage on the foc’sle, gave in at last and snored contentedly by a cabin door, no doubt finishing his enemy magnificently in his dream.

We three—Sir Henry, Captain Good, and I—sat by the wheel and said nothing for a while, listening. The ship had its own small orchestra: cordage tapping, a block knocking now and then, the low talk of men on watch. Sound does more to a man than he admits; a quiet night can steady a mind the way warm broth steadies a stomach. There is health in honest natural noises, and poison in the ceaseless grind and clatter that men build for themselves in towns. Here there was only sea, wind, voices, and the far boom of surf—no iron screaming, no commercial din dressing itself up as “progress.”

Sir Henry broke the silence first. “Well, Mr. Quatermain,” he said, “have you been thinking about my proposals?”

“Ay,” echoed Captain Good. “What do you think of them, Mr. Quatermain? I hope you are going to give us the pleasure of your company as far as Solomon’s Mines, or wherever the gentleman you knew as Neville may have got to.”

I got up and knocked the ashes out of my pipe. I had not quite made up my mind, and I wanted a breath of time—just the little pause it takes for a man to swallow. Before the glowing tobacco hit the water I knew. It is often like that: you worry a matter for days, and then one extra second settles it like a stone dropping through clear water.

“Yes, gentlemen,” I said, sitting down again. “I will go—and, by your leave, I will tell you why, and on what conditions.

“First, the terms I ask.

“1. You pay all expenses; and any ivory or other valuables we get to be divided between Captain Good and myself.

“2. You give me five hundred pounds for my services on the trip before we start, I undertaking to serve you faithfully till you choose to abandon the enterprise, or till we succeed, or disaster overtakes us.

“3. That before we trek you execute a deed agreeing that, in the event of my death or disablement, you will pay my boy Harry—who is studying medicine in London at Guy’s—two hundred a year for five years. By then he ought to be earning his own living, if he’s worth his salt.

“That’s all, I think, and I daresay you will say it is quite enough too.”

“No,” said Sir Henry at once. “I accept gladly. I am bent on this project, and would pay more than that for your help, considering the peculiar and exclusive knowledge you possess.”

“Pity I didn’t ask it, then,” I answered; “but I won’t go back on my word. Now that my terms are settled I’ll tell you my reasons. First, gentlemen, I have been watching you both these last few days, and if you won’t think me impertinent, I’ll say plainly I like you. I believe we shall come up well to the yoke together. That is something, with a long journey ahead.

“And now as to the journey itself. I tell you flatly I do not think it probable we can come out of it alive—if we attempt the Suliman Mountains. What was the fate of old Dom da Silvestra three hundred years ago? What was the fate of his descendant twenty years back? What has been your brother’s fate? I tell you frankly, gentlemen, that as their fates were, so I believe ours will be.”

I watched their faces. Captain Good shifted a little; he did not like to have the taste of death put into his mouth before he had even eaten. Sir Henry’s expression did not change.

“We must take our chance,” he said.

“You may wonder,” I went on, “why, if I think this—and I told you I am a timid man—I should go. Two reasons. First, I am a fatalist. I believe my time is appointed without reference to my movements; if I am to go to Suliman’s Mountains to be killed, I shall go and I shall be killed. God Almighty knows His mind about me, so I need not fret Him or myself on that point.

“Second, I am a poor man. For nearly forty years I have hunted and traded and never made more than a living. And perhaps you do not know that the average life of an elephant hunter from the day he takes to the trade is between four and five years. I have lived through about seven generations of my class, so my time cannot be far off in any case. If something happened to me in the ordinary course, by the time my debts were paid there would be nothing to keep my son Harry while he learned to earn. But now he will be set up for five years. That is the whole matter in a nutshell.”

Sir Henry listened as seriously as a man taking oath. “Mr. Quatermain,” he said, “your motives reflect credit on you. Whether you are right or wrong time will show. But right or wrong I am going through with it to the end—sweet or bitter. If we are to be knocked on the head, all I have to say is that I hope we get a little shooting first, eh, Good?”

“Yes, yes,” said the Captain quickly. “We have all three been used to danger in our different ways. No good turning back now. And now I vote we go down to the saloon and take an observation—just for luck, you know.”

And we did—through the bottom of a tumbler.

Next day we went ashore, and I put them up at my little shanty on the Berea, which I call my home. It has three rooms and a kitchen, built of green brick with a galvanised iron roof, but it has a good garden, and the best loquot trees I know. There are young mangoes too, gifts from the curator of the botanical gardens—trees that smell, when the sap runs, like pepper and honey together, and make a man imagine future seasons of sticky fingers and bowls set out on tables. The place is cared for by an old hunter of mine, Jack, a Griqua by birth, whose thigh a buffalo cow broke so badly in Sikukuni’s country that he will never hunt again. He can potter about, though, and he gardens with the steady patience of a man who has had noise enough in his life and now prefers the small sounds: a spade biting soil, water poured into a trench, leaves rubbing together in a mild wind. You will never get a Zulu to take much interest in gardening; it is a peaceful art, and peaceful arts are not much in his line.

There was no room in the house for Sir Henry and Good, so they slept in a tent pitched among my orange trees at the end of the garden. Durban oranges carry fruit, green and gold, and blossoms all at once—life, promise, and fulfilment together on the same branch. The bloom scented their canvas, and with the few mosquitos we get on the Berea—unless the rains have been unusually heavy—I daresay they found it pleasant enough.

Now, to get on—for if I don’t, Harry, you will be tired of my story before we ever fetch up at Suliman’s Mountains—once my mind was made up I began preparations.

First I secured the deed from Sir Henry providing for you in case of accident. There was difficulty about legal execution, as he was a stranger here and the property to be charged lay over the water; but with a lawyer’s help it was got over at last. The lawyer charged twenty pounds for the job, which I thought outrageous—like paying a man a sovereign for salting your pot when you have brought the meat yourself. Then I pocketed my cheque for five hundred.

Having paid this tribute to my bump of caution, I bought a wagon and a span of oxen on Sir Henry’s behalf, and beauties they were. The wagon was twenty-two feet long with iron axles, very strong and light, built throughout of stinkwood. It had been to the Diamond Fields and back—not quite new, but, in my opinion, all the better. If there is green wood or a weakness in a wagon it shows itself on the first hard trip, just as bad flour shows itself the first time you try to bake. This one had been tested by distance and had not complained. It was a “half-tented” wagon—covered only over the after twelve feet, leaving the front open for the rough necessities of travel. In the tented part was a hide cartle, a bed where two could sleep, racks for rifles, and a number of small conveniences that matter greatly once you are away from shops and carpenters. I gave one hundred and twenty-five pounds for it and thought it cheap.

Then I bought a beautiful team of twenty Zulu oxen, which I had been watching for a year or two. Sixteen is usual, but I took four extra for casualties. Zulu cattle are smaller than Africanders—half the size—but they will live where the big oxen would starve, and with a moderate load they can make five miles a day better going. They are quicker, and less liable to footsoreness. What is more, this lot were thoroughly “salted”—they had worked across South Africa and were comparatively proof against red water, which so often destroys whole teams when they reach strange veldt. As for lung-sick—dreadful pneumonia, common here—they had been inoculated.

The inoculation is a cruel-looking business. You cut a slit in the tail and bind in a piece of diseased lung from an ox that has died. The animal takes the sickness mildly, and then, as a rule, the tail drops off about a foot from the root, and the ox becomes proof against future attacks. It seems hard to rob a beast of its tail in a land full of flies, but better a tail sacrificed than an ox lost. A tail without an ox is not much use, except for dusting. Still, it looks strange to trek behind twenty stumps where there ought to be tails, as though Nature had made a small mistake and stuck bulldogs’ sterns onto oxen’s rumps.

Next came provisioning and medicines—matter that needs more judgement than most people think, for one must not overload the wagon and yet must not go without what cannot be replaced. Fortunately Captain Good is something of a doctor. At some point in his life he managed to pass through medical and surgical instruction, and he has kept it up after a fashion. He is not qualified, but he knows more than many a man who writes M.D. after his name, as we were to discover. He had a splendid travelling medicine chest and a set of instruments kept as tidy as a cook’s knives. While we were at Durban he cut off a Kafir’s big toe in a way that was a pleasure to watch—clean, quick, not wasting movement, as if he were jointing meat properly. The Kafir sat stolidly through it, then calmly asked Good to put on another, saying that a “white one” would do at a pinch. That nonplussed the Captain completely.

When those matters were settled there remained two important points: arms and servants.

As to arms, I cannot do better than set down the list we chose from Sir Henry’s ample store and my own. I copied it at the time into my pocket-book:

Three heavy breech-loading double-eight elephant guns, about fifteen pounds each, carrying eleven drachms of black powder.

Two were by a well-known London firm, beautifully made. Mine was not so finely finished and I cannot tell who made it, but I have used it on several trips, shot many elephants with it, and found it always reliable—like an old pot that is blackened outside but boils true.

Three double .500 Expresses, built to stand six drachms—sweet weapons for medium game like eland or sable, and for men too, especially in open country with the semi-hollow bullet.

One double No. 12 central-fire Keeper’s shot-gun, full choke both barrels. That gun proved invaluable later for shooting meat for the pot—the difference between eating well and chewing misery.

Three Winchester repeating rifles (not carbines), spare.

Three single-action Colt’s revolvers with the heavier American cartridge.

That was our total armament. You will note the weapons of each class were of the same make and calibre so cartridges were interchangeable—a vital point, as any experienced hunter knows. A fine gun without proper ammunition is like a stew without salt: it may look right, but it won’t carry you.

Now as to the men.

After much talk we decided to limit our number to five: a driver, a leader, and three servants. The driver and leader I found easily enough—two Zulus named Goza and Tom. But the servants were harder. We needed men we could trust, men with steady hearts, because on a journey like this our lives might hang on a servant’s courage or his fear.

At last I secured two: a Hottentot named Ventvögel—“windbird”—and a little Zulu named Khiva, who spoke English perfectly. Ventvögel I had known before. He was one of the best spoorers I ever worked with, tough as whipcord, never seeming to tire. But he had a failing common enough with his people: drink. Put gin within reach and you could not trust him. Still, we were going beyond grog-shops, so the weakness mattered less.

For a third servant I looked in vain. In the end we decided to start without one, trusting to luck to pick up a suitable man on the road.

But the evening before our fixed departure Khiva told me a Kafir was waiting to see me. We were at table. Dinner had been simple—bread, meat, and such fresh things as my garden and Durban could offer, with the clean scent of citrus drifting in whenever someone moved near the door. After we had done I told Khiva to bring the man in.

Presently a tall, handsome fellow came in—about thirty, and very light-coloured for a Zulu. He lifted his knob-stick in salute, then squatted in the corner on his haunches and sat silent. I did not notice him for a while. It is a mistake to rush at a Zulu with questions; he takes quick talk for lack of dignity. While we waited I studied him. He was a keshla—a ringed man—with the black head-ring of polished gum and fat worked into the hair that Zulus assume when they reach a certain age or standing. And his face tugged at my memory.

“Well,” I said at last, “what is your name?”

“Umbopa,” he answered in a slow, deep voice.

“I have seen your face before.”

“Yes,” he said. “The Inkoosi, the chief, my father, saw my face at the place of the Little Hand—Isandhlwana—on the day before the battle.”

Then I remembered. In the Zulu War I had been one of Lord Chelmsford’s guides, and by good fortune I had left the camp in charge of some wagons the day before the slaughter. While I waited for cattle to be inspanned I had spoken with this man, who had some small command among the native auxiliaries, and he had expressed doubts about the camp’s safety. I had told him to hold his tongue and leave such matters to wiser heads. Afterwards, his words came back to me with a bitter taste.

“I remember,” I said. “What is it you want?”

“It is this, Macumazahn,” he answered—my Kafir name, meaning the man who gets up in the middle of the night, he who keeps his eyes open. “I hear you go on a great expedition far into the North with the white chiefs from over the water. Is it a true word?”

“It is.”

“I hear that you go even to the Lukanga River, a moon’s journey beyond the Manica country. Is this so also, Macumazahn?”

“Why do you ask where we go?” I said sharply. “What is it to you?” For our business had been kept dead secret, and secrets, like good food, spoil when too many hands are in the pot.

He did not flinch. “It is this, O white men, that if indeed you travel so far, I would travel with you.”

There was something in the way he spoke—especially his use of “O white men” instead of “O Inkosis”—that carried an assumption of dignity. It was as though he were measuring us as much as we measured him.

“You forget yourself a little,” I said. “Your words run out unawares. That is not the way to speak. What is your name, and where is your kraal? Tell us, that we may know with whom we have to deal.”

“My name is Umbopa. I am of the Zulu people, yet not of them. The house of my tribe is in the far North; it was left behind when the Zulus came down here a thousand years ago, long before Chaka reigned. I have no kraal. I have wandered many years.

“I came from the North as a child to Zululand. I was Cetewayo’s man in the Nkomabakosi regiment, serving under the great captain Umslopogaasi of the Axe, who taught my hands to fight. Afterwards I ran away from Zululand and came to Natal because I wanted to see the white man’s ways. Next I fought against Cetewayo in the war. Since then I have worked in Natal. Now I am tired, and would go North again. Here is not my place.

“I want no money. But I am a brave man, and worth my place and meat. I have spoken.”

I was puzzled. His story, in the main, rang true, yet something in him stood aside from ordinary Zulus—an air of holding something back, as if his real name lay under his tongue like a word in a dead dialect that cannot be translated into the speech of the present. And I mistrusted any man who offered to come without pay; even generosity can be a kind of chain.

Being doubtful, I translated his words to Sir Henry and Good and asked what they thought.

Sir Henry nodded once. “Ask him to stand up.”

Umbopa rose. As he did so he slipped off a long military great coat and stood naked except for the moocha round his middle and a necklace of lions’ claws. In the lamplight his skin looked scarcely more than dark, and here and there deep black scars—old assegai wounds—ran across him like the writing of history on a living page. He was a magnificent man—six foot three, broad, straight, and shapely as a spear.

Sir Henry walked up and looked into his face. The two stood near each other—both big men, both with that quiet weight of presence. Captain Good, watching, said softly, “They make a good pair, don’t they? One as big as the other.”

“I like your looks, Mr. Umbopa,” said Sir Henry in English, “and I will take you as my servant.”

Umbopa understood; I saw it in the way his eyes took in the words. He answered in Zulu, “It is well,” and then, glancing at Sir Henry’s great stature and breadth, added with grave simplicity, “We are men, thou and I.”

I have no mind to set down, step by step, every weary mile that carried us from Durban to Sitanda’s Kraal near where the Lukanga and the Kalukwe make acquaintance; a thousand miles is a long dish to serve whole, and most of its ingredients—heat, thirst, sore feet, the same horizon tasted day after day—are those that every man who has travelled and hunted in Africa already knows by smell alone.

We left Durban at the end of January; it was in the second week of May when we camped at last within reach of Sitanda’s people. The last three hundred miles we made on foot, for the curse of the tsetse lay over the country like an invisible net: it kills every beast that pulls a wagon save donkeys, and every beast that walks upright save man. At Inyati, that outlying trading-station in the Matabele land—where Lobengula, a great and cruel scoundrel, sits king—we parted, with honest regret, from the wagon that had been our little moving homestead.

Only twelve oxen were left to us out of the fine span of twenty I had bought at Durban. One had died by cobra bite; three by sheer poverty and want of water; one had strayed; three had been poisoned by the herb the Boers call “tulip.” Five more took that sickness too, but we saved them with an infusion made by boiling down the tulip leaves—bitter as burnt tea and rank as old cabbage-water, but if given in time it drags life back into an ox like a hand hauling a bucket from a well.

We left wagon and oxen to Goza and Tom—good boys both, driver and leader—and asked a worthy Scotch missionary there to keep an eye upon our property. Then Sir Henry Curtis and Captain John Good and I set off on foot upon our wild quest, Umbopa with us, and Ventvögel the Hottentot, and Khiva, and half a dozen bearers hired on the spot.

I remember the first hours after we turned our backs on the wagon: we tramped in a kind of hush, as though the road itself had swallowed our voices. The bush had its own talk—the tick of beetles in dead leaves, a far wood-dove calling as if it remembered some lost word, the steady pad-pad of many feet—but no man seemed eager to add his speech to it. I think each of us, in his own fashion, was asking whether we should ever see our wagon again. For my part I did not expect it.

Then Umbopa, who marched ahead with his head high and his shoulders loose and easy, broke out into a Zulu chant. It was one of those songs that travel from mouth to mouth without ever being written down, a whole history carried in rhythm and breath. It told how brave men, sick of tameness and of living only to eat and sleep, went into a vast wilderness to find new things or to die; and how, when they had gone far enough to lose the old tracks, the wilderness turned, like a pot with its lid lifted, and showed itself not barren at all but full of young wives and fat cattle, of game to hunt and enemies to kill.

The words were not mine; I could never put the exact colour of his sounds into English, for the sense was more than meaning—it was the weight of the vowels, the snap of the consonants, the way his voice rose and fell like wind in long grass. Yet even without perfect understanding, the song worked upon us. We laughed, and the laugh tasted good after silence; we took it for an omen. Umbopa was a cheerful savage in a dignified way, when he was not overtaken by those brooding fits that made him sit apart and look as though he listened to drums none of us could hear. He had a gift for keeping men’s spirits from drying up. We grew very fond of him.

Now, of all the incidents of that march, there is one I shall not deny myself; for I dearly love a hunting yarn, and this one, too, had a beginning that smelled of fruit and ended with the hot iron tang of blood.

About a fortnight from Inyati we came into a bit of woodland country so beautiful that it seemed almost indecent that it should be empty of houses and gardens. There was water everywhere: in the kloofs of the hills it ran under shadow, and the bush there was thick—idoro bush the natives call it—and in places the wacht-een-beche, the “wait-a-little thorn,” that lays hold of your clothes with a spiteful patience. There were many machabell trees, glossy-leaved and laden with yellow fruit, each fruit with a stone in it like a little round hammer. The fruit was refreshing—sharp-sweet, with a scent like crushed peel—and it is the elephant’s favourite food. The country told its own story in broken branches and uprooted trunks. An elephant feeds as a fire feeds: not neatly, but with appetite and destruction.

One evening, after a long day, we came to a place lovelier than the rest. At the foot of a bush-clad hill lay a dry river-bed, but in the sand were pools of water as clear as glass, rimmed all round with the stamped signatures of game—hoofprints laid over hoofprints in a kind of crowded writing. Facing the hill was a plain like a park, dotted with flat-topped mimosa and here and there the dark polish of machabell leaves. Beyond it all stretched the sea of bush, pathless and, for the moment, so still that you could hear the creak of leather and the little clicks as rifles knocked against belt-buckles.

As we stepped down into the river-bed we started a troop of giraffes. They went off in that strange sailing gallop of theirs, tails cocked, long legs moving with a clumsy grace, their hoofs rattling like castanets on hard ground. They were a good three hundred yards away—out of shot in any reasonable sense—but Good, who walked ahead with his express loaded with solid ball, could not bear to let so much meat and marrow float away untested.

He lifted the gun and fired at the last—a young cow. By some extraordinary chance, the ball struck full on the back of the neck and shattered the spine. That giraffe went over and over as neatly as a rabbit shot in a cabbage patch.

“Curse it!” cried Good—he had a habit of strong language when excitement warmed him, a habit bred, no doubt, on a man-of-war. “Curse it! I’ve killed him.”

“Ou, Bougwan,” said the Kafirs—“ou! ou!” They called him Bougwan, Glass Eye, because of his eye-glass.

“Oh, Bougwan!” Sir Henry and I echoed, and from that hour Good’s reputation as a marvellous shot stood firm among the boys. In truth he was not; but whenever he missed afterwards, we forgave him for the sake of that giraffe.

We set some of the bearers to cut off the best of the meat, and then we built a scherm near one of the pools, about a hundred yards to its right. A scherm is made by cutting thorn bushes and piling them in a circle like a hedge. Inside you smooth the ground and, if you can get it, spread dry tambouki grass for bedding. Then you light your fires. There is a domestic comfort in such work even in wild places: hands busy, knives scraping, grass shaken out, the first smoke rising and carrying away the day’s sweat smell. The bush, too, seems to draw closer, as though curious.

By the time the thorn circle was finished, the moon had peeped up, and our dinner was ready—giraffe steaks and roasted marrow-bones. To roast a marrow-bone properly you set it near enough to the coals that the fat within warms and loosens, but not so close that it scorches and turns bitter; then you crack it—no delicate business—and scoop out the marrow while it is still steaming and sweet, with that rich, clean taste that seems to carry sunlight and grass inside it. We ate by moonlight, and more than once stopped to praise Bougwan’s wonderful shot. I know no greater luxury than giraffe marrow—unless it be elephant’s heart, which we tasted on the morrow.

After supper we smoked and yarned. It was a curious picture: three white men squatting round firelight in the wilderness, while a few yards off the Kafirs sucked their intoxicating daccha from a pipe whose mouthpiece was made of eland horn. The night had its own orchestra—the low crackle of thorn, a night-jar’s call, the faint rustle of something small hunting among dead leaves. Such sounds soothe a man’s mind; they are honest, not the hammering, screaming clatter of civilised places that so often passes for life and leaves the head ringing with emptiness.

As for our looks: I, with my short grizzled hair sticking up, am thin and short and dark—nine stone and a half; Sir Henry is tall and broad and fair with yellow hair growing long—fifteen stone. But the oddest-looking, all things considered, was Captain John Good, R.N. He sat on a leather bag as though he had just stepped out from a comfortable day’s shooting at home—clean as a new plate, tidy as a ship’s cabin, dressed as if a tailor waited in the bush. He wore a brown tweed shooting suit with a hat to match and neat gaiters. He was beautifully shaved. His eye-glass and his false teeth appeared in perfect order. He even wore a collar—white gutta-percha, and he had a supply.

“They weigh so little,” he said to me innocently when I stared. “And I always like to turn out like a gentleman.”

Ah—if he could have foreseen the raiment the future had in store for him.

One by one the boys rolled themselves in blankets and slept by the fire, all except Umbopa, who sat a little apart, chin on hand, thinking as though his thoughts had a language of their own and preferred not to be translated. He never mixed much with the others.

Presently, from the bush behind us, came a loud “woof, woof!”

“That’s a lion,” I said, and we all started up to listen.

Hardly had we done so when, from the pool a hundred yards away, came the strident trumpeting of an elephant.

“Unkungunklovo! Indlovu!” whispered the Kafirs—“Elephant! Elephant!” A few minutes later we saw huge shadowy forms moving slowly from the water toward the bush, one after another, like black ships gliding out of moonlight.

Good was on his feet at once, burning for slaughter, perhaps thinking elephant were as easy as that giraffe. I caught his arm and pulled him down.

“It’s no good,” I whispered. “Let them go.”

“It seems we are in a paradise of game,” Sir Henry said presently. “I vote we stop here a day or two and have a go at them.”

I was surprised, for hitherto Sir Henry had pressed forward hard, especially since we learned at Inyati that an Englishman named Neville had sold his wagon there and gone on up-country two years before. But hunter blood will speak, and so will temptation. Good jumped at it; and to tell the truth, so did I. It goes against a man’s conscience to let such a herd pass without at least trying his luck.

“All right, my hearties,” said I. “We want a little recreation. Turn in now. We must be off by dawn, and perhaps we’ll catch them feeding before they move.”

Good stripped, shook out his clothes as though dust were a moral failing, put his eye-glass and false teeth into his trousers pocket, folded each article neatly, and laid them under a corner of his mackintosh to keep off the dew. Sir Henry and I made rougher arrangements. Soon we were rolled in blankets, and the bush’s steady breathing pulled us into that dreamless sleep that is the traveller’s true luxury.

Going, going, go—

What was that?

From the direction of the water came violent scuffling. Next instant the air split with a succession of roars so dreadful that the skin on a man’s neck tightens of itself. Only a lion can make such a noise, as though the world were being torn.

We jumped up and looked. Toward the pool we saw a confused mass, yellow and black, staggering and struggling our way. We seized rifles, slipped on veldtschoons—shoes of untanned hide—and ran out of the scherm. Before we reached it the mass fell and rolled over and over; then it lay still.

On the grass lay a sable antelope bull—the most beautiful of African antelopes—dead. Transfixed upon its great curved horns was a magnificent black-maned lion, also dead. The story read plain enough: the sable had come down to drink where the lion lay in wait. The lion sprang and was received upon those hooked spears, driven clean through. Unable to free himself, he had torn and bitten the bull’s back and neck; and the bull, maddened with pain, had rushed on till both dropped dead together. I had seen the like once before, and even then it seemed a savage sort of justice.
