We called the Kafirs, and between us dragged both carcases up to the scherm. The smell of fresh blood followed us—hot, metallic, and heavy in the nostrils. Then we lay down again and slept till dawn.

With first light we were up and making ready for the fray. We took the three eight-bore rifles, plenty of ammunition, and our large water-bottles filled with weak cold tea, which I have always found the best drink to shoot on—cool, faintly bitter, steadying the stomach when heat would otherwise turn it. After swallowing a little breakfast—what there was—we started, Umbopa, Khiva, and Ventvögel with us. The other Kafirs we left to skin lion and sable and cut up the latter.

We found the elephant spoor easily enough: the broad trail ran through bush like a road, and Ventvögel, after peering and sniffing and setting his hand to droppings, pronounced it the work of twenty to thirty elephants, most of them full-grown bulls. They had moved on in the night, and it was nine o’clock—already fiercely hot—before we came upon the signs that told us we were close: broken trees, bruised leaves and bark, and droppings still smoking faintly as though the earth itself cooked in this sun.

Presently we saw them: the herd standing in a hollow, their morning meal finished, flapping their great ears. There were between twenty and thirty, as Ventvögel said. It was a splendid sight—vast bodies, wrinkled and grey, moving with a slow majesty, and now and again the pale curve of a tusk catching light.

I took a handful of dry grass and tossed it up to read the wind. If they winded us they would be off before a man could blink. The grass drifted from them toward us, or near enough. We crept forward, using what cover we could, until we were within forty yards.

Just in front, broadside on, stood three splendid bulls, one carrying enormous tusks. I whispered our plan: I would take the middle bull; Sir Henry the left; Good the big-tusked bull.

“Now,” I whispered.

Boom! boom! boom! The three heavy rifles spoke like cannon.

Sir Henry’s elephant dropped dead as a hammer—shot through the heart. Mine fell to its knees; for an instant I thought it done, but it heaved itself up and came tearing straight past me, so close I could smell the warm, dusty hide and the rank breath of crushed leaves. As it went I gave it the second barrel in the ribs. That brought it down in earnest. I jammed in two fresh cartridges, ran up close, and a ball through the brain ended its struggling.

Then I turned to see how Good had fared. I had heard the big bull scream with rage and pain.

I found the captain in a great state of excitement. His bull, hit, had turned and come straight for him; Good had only just leapt clear, and the beast went charging on blindly in the direction of our camp. The herd meanwhile crashed off in wild alarm the other way, smashing bush as though it were tambouki grass.

We debated whether to follow the wounded patriarch with the great tusks or to go after the herd. In the end we chose the herd and left the wounded bull, thinking we had seen the last of those tusks. I have often wished since that we had.

It was easy enough to follow the herd—their flight left a track like a carriage road. But to come up with them under that broiling sun was another matter. We struggled on for over two hours, sweat running into eyes, tongues thick in our mouths, the bush’s dry smell baking into us. At last we found them again.

All but one bull stood bunched together. Their uneasiness showed in their constant shifting and in the way they lifted trunks to test the air. Fifty yards in front of them stood a solitary bull, keeping sentry; he was about sixty yards from us. The ground here was more open; if we tried to creep closer he would surely see or scent us and start the herd.

We all aimed at the sentry, and at my whispered word we fired. The three shots took effect and he went down dead. Again the herd started, and their luck failed them: a hundred yards ahead lay a nullah—a dried-out water track—with steep banks, very like the place where the Prince Imperial was killed in Zululand. Into it the elephants plunged. When we reached the edge we saw them struggling in wild confusion to climb the far bank, filling the air with screams and trumpeting, pushing and shouldering one another aside in selfish panic—just like so many human beings.

Now was our chance. We fired as fast as we could load. Five elephants fell. And no doubt we should have killed the whole herd, had they not suddenly given up trying the bank and rushed headlong down the nullah away from us.

We were too tired to follow, and perhaps a little sick of slaughter. Eight elephants is a heavy bag for one day, and the smell of blood and burst stomach-sourness hangs round a man’s head until he begins to feel it in his dreams.

After we rested a while, the boys cut out the hearts of two of the dead elephants for supper. Elephant heart, when fresh, is like nothing else—dark, dense meat that must be cut with respect, sliced across the grain, and set over coals not too fierce. If you scorch it, it turns tough and bitter; if you let it cook slowly, the fat sweats out and the centre stays tender, with a taste at once iron-rich and sweet, as though the animal’s strength has become edible. We started homeward very well pleased with our day, and meant to send bearers next morning to chop away the tusks.

Shortly after we passed the spot where Good had wounded the great bull, we came across a herd of eland. We did not shoot—there was meat enough already, and our mouths were still full of elephant-heart richness and woodsmoke. The eland trotted past, then stopped behind a small patch of bush about a hundred yards away and wheeled to stare at us, mild-eyed and curious.

Good, never having seen an eland close, wanted a nearer view. He handed his rifle to Umbopa and, with Khiva following, strolled toward the patch of bush. Sir Henry and I sat down, glad of the excuse to rest, letting the evening cool gather round us. The sun was sinking in its reddest glory, and the whole world smelled of dust settling and crushed herb, and the bush’s sounds shifted to their dusk register—small birds calling, insects beginning their steady, living hiss.

Then, suddenly, we heard an elephant scream.

We looked up and saw its huge form rushing with uplifted trunk and tail, black against the great fiery globe of the sun. Next instant we saw Good and Khiva tearing back toward us with the wounded bull—yes, the patriarch with the great tusks—charging after them.

For a moment we did not dare fire; at that distance it would likely have been useless, and we feared to hit one of them. In the next breath a dreadful thing happened—Good fell, and it was his passion for civilised dress that tripped him. Had he hunted in a flannel shirt and veldtschoons like the rest of us, he might have kept his feet. But his trousers and gaiters hampered him. When he was about sixty yards from us, his polished boot—slick with dry grass—slipped, and he went down full on his face right in the elephant’s path.

We gasped, knowing what should follow, and ran toward him as hard as men can run with horror in their bellies.

In three seconds it was ended—but not as we thought.

Khiva saw his master fall. Brave lad, he turned and flung his assegai straight into the elephant’s face. It stuck in the trunk. With a scream of pain the brute seized the boy, hurled him down, set one vast foot upon his body, and with its trunk twined round him and tore him in two.

We rushed up mad with horror and fired again and again. At last the elephant fell, collapsing upon the fragments of the Zulu.

Good rose, wrung his hands, and stood over the man who had given his life to save him. Though I am an old hand, a lump grew in my throat, for there are deaths that do not grow easier with years, and the bush itself seemed to hold its breath, as if the evening’s quiet sounds had been cut away like language lost from a people.

Umbopa stood looking on at the huge dead elephant and at what was left of poor Khiva. His face was set, and when he spoke his words came slowly, in the plain, weighty manner of a man pronouncing a judgment that needs no ornament.

“Ah, well,” he said at last, “he is dead, but he died like a man!”

We had killed nine elephants, and for two days the camp smelt of hot blood and sun-struck hide while we worked. It is no light business, cutting tusks out clean and whole—the knife grates upon gristle, the saw bites, the great head lolls like a fallen monument, and all the while the flies come in a black, humming veil, tasting everything, claiming everything. When at last we had the ivory safe, we carried it in, piece by piece, and buried it under the sand beneath a big tree that stood up like a finger on the plain, visible for miles. It was a noble lot—solid, sound, creamy in the heart—averaging forty to fifty pounds the tusk. The pair from the great bull that had killed poor Khiva would, as nearly as we could judge, have turned the scale at one hundred and seventy pounds.

As for Khiva, there was little to bury. We laid what remained of him in an ant-bear hole and put an assegai beside him, that he might not go empty-handed into whatever country lies beyond this one. Then, on the third day, we shouldered our loads and marched again, with that odd mixture of hope and superstition a man carries when he hides treasure in the wild and must leave it behind him like seed in the ground—trusting to time, to luck, and to his own life.

After a long, grinding tramp, and mishaps enough that would make a book by themselves, we came at last to Sitanda’s Kraal by the Lukanga River—the true threshold of our venture. I remember it as a man remembers the taste of the first mouthful before a hard fast begins. On our right lay the scattered settlement: a few stone cattle kraals, patches of cultivated land near the water where the people coaxed a thin harvest out of the soil, and beyond them the wide veld rolling in tall grass, with little herds of game wandering as if they had been born of the wind. On our left yawned the desert—bare, abrupt, and uncompromising, as though someone had drawn a line with a knife and said: here nourishment ends.

Below our camp a small stream ran with a sound like a quiet throat clearing itself; on the far side rose a stony slope, the very one down which, twenty years before, I had watched poor José Silvestre creep back, used up and half dead after his attempt upon Solomon’s Mines. Beyond that slope the country died into waterless waste, with only karoo scrub crouching close to the sand as if afraid to be seen.

We pitched at evening. The sun, a great red ball, sank into the desert and threw long, coloured rays over it—scarlet, copper, and purple—till the whole emptiness looked dressed for a ceremony. Good stayed to see to camp; I took Sir Henry up the slope opposite, and we stood there and stared out. The air was so clear that far away, faint as breath on glass, I could make out blue outlines—here and there capped with white—of the Suliman Berg.

“There,” I said, “is the wall round Solomon’s Mines. But God knows whether we shall ever climb it.”

“My brother should be there,” Sir Henry answered, quiet as a man saying grace over a familiar table, “and if he is, I shall reach him somehow.”

“I hope so,” I said; and turned—then saw we were not alone.

Behind us stood Umbopa, the big Kafir who had attached himself to Sir Henry, gazing over our shoulders towards those far mountains as though he could taste them on the wind.

When he caught my eye he spoke, not to me but to Sir Henry, and he used the native name they had given him.

“Is it to that land thou wouldst journey, Incubu?” he asked, pointing with his broad assegai.

I checked him sharply; it is one thing for them to nickname a man among themselves, another for them to fling their heathen titles at a white gentleman’s face. Umbopa laughed—a low, self-contained laugh that set my temper on edge.

“How dost thou know I am not the equal of the Inkosi whom I serve?” he said. “He is of a royal house, no doubt; one can see it in his size and in his mien. So, mayhap, am I. At the least, I am as great a man. Be my mouth, Macumazahn, and say my words to the Inkoos Incubu.”

I did not relish his swagger, but there was weight in him, and curiosity in me. So I translated—adding, for form’s sake, my opinion that he was an impudent fellow.

“Yes, Umbopa,” Sir Henry said, “I would journey there.”

“The desert is wide and there is no water,” Umbopa went on. “The mountains are high and covered with snow. No man can say what lies beyond them behind the place where the sun sets. How shalt thou come thither, Incubu, and wherefore dost thou go?”

Sir Henry answered, and I gave it to Umbopa: he went because a man of his blood—his brother—had gone that way before; he journeyed to seek him.

“That is so,” said Umbopa. “A Hottentot I met on the road told me a white man went out into the desert two years ago towards those mountains with one servant, a hunter. They never came back.”

Sir Henry’s head lifted a shade. “How do you know it was my brother?”

“Nay, I know not. But when I asked what the white man was like, the Hottentot said he had thine eyes and a black beard. He said too that the hunter’s name was Jim—a Bechuana who wore clothes.”

“There’s no doubt,” I put in. “I knew Jim well.”

Sir Henry nodded, as though the last knot of uncertainty had been pulled tight. “If George set his mind upon a thing, he did it,” he said. “If he meant to cross the Suliman Berg he has crossed it, unless an accident overtook him. We must look for him on the other side.”

Umbopa understood our tongue better than he pretended. “It is a far journey, Incubu,” he said.

Sir Henry’s answer came slowly, with the steadiness of a man sharpening a blade. He spoke of a road a man will take if love drives him; of deserts crossed, mountains climbed, life held out in the hand like a small coin that can be spent or saved as Heaven orders.

I translated.

Umbopa listened and then, in his turn, poured out one of those strange, swelling speeches that sometimes come to Zulus like a song coming to a man who did not know he could sing.

“Great words, my father,” he said. “Words fit to fill the mouth. Thou art right. What is life? It is a feather. It is the seed of grass blown hither and thither—sometimes multiplying and dying in the act, sometimes carried away into the heavens. Yet if the seed be heavy and good it may travel a little way on the road it wills. Man must die. At the worst he can but die a little sooner. I will go with thee across the desert and over the mountains, unless I fall to the ground on the way.”

Then he paused and began again, as if he were listening to something the rest of us could not hear—the deep hush of the desert itself, the long breath of it, the way it swallows words and gives back only a faint echo.

“What is life?” he asked us—“you white men who are wise, who know the secrets of the world and of the stars, who flash your words from afar without a voice—tell me the secret of our life, whither it goes and whence it comes!”

We could not answer.

He answered himself.

“Out of the dark we come; into the dark we go. Like a storm-driven bird at night we fly out of the Nowhere; for a moment our wings are seen in the light of the fire, and lo! we are gone again into the Nowhere. Life is nothing. Life is all. It is the Hand with which we hold off Death. It is the glow-worm that shines in night-time and is black in the morning; it is the white breath of oxen in winter; it is the little shadow that runs across the grass and loses itself at sunset.”

When he stopped, the silence fell back, thick and listening. In that quiet, even the small sounds—our boots shifting on stone, the far hiss of wind in scrub—seemed important, as though the land itself judged what noises were fit to exist there.

“You are a strange man,” Sir Henry said at last.

Umbopa laughed again. “It seems to me we are much alike, Incubu. Perhaps I seek a brother over the mountains.”

I watched him sharply. “What dost thou mean?” I asked. “What dost thou know of those mountains?”

“A little,” he said. “A very little. There is a strange land yonder—a land of witchcraft and beautiful things; of brave people; of trees and streams and snowy peaks; and of a great white road. I have heard of it. But what is the good of talking? It grows dark. Those who live to see will see.”

He knew too much—enough to make a man uneasy. He read my look.

“Fear me not, Macumazahn,” he said. “I dig no holes for you to fall in. I make no plots. If ever we cross those mountains behind the sun, then I will tell what I know. But Death sits upon them. Be wise and turn back. Go hunt elephants, my masters. I have spoken.”

He saluted with his spear and walked back to camp; presently we found him squatting like any other fellow, cleaning a gun with patient care.

“That is an odd man,” Sir Henry said.

“Yes,” I answered. “Too odd by half. He knows something and won’t speak out. But there’s no use quarrelling. We’re in for a curious trip, and one mysterious Kafir won’t turn it one way or the other.”

Next day we set about our preparations. We could not drag our heavy elephant rifles and all our kit across a desert that offered no water and no shade. So we dismissed the bearers and made an arrangement with an old native whose kraal stood close by to keep our things until our return. It went against me to leave good tools and precious stores to the custody of a fellow whose eyes shone like a hyena’s whenever they slid over metal; still, I took precautions.

First I loaded all the rifles, left them at full cock, and told him that if he touched them they would go off. He tried at once with my eight-bore; it did go off, and drove a hole through one of his oxen that happened to be passing, besides knocking the old thief clean over with the recoil. He picked himself up, white-eyed with fright and rage, and had the impudence to demand payment for the ox; but nothing on God’s earth would make him put so much as a finger on a gun again.

“Put the live devils away up there in the thatch,” he said, “or they will murder us all.”

Then I told him that if, on our return, one of those things was missing, I would kill him and his people by witchcraft; and if we died and he tried to steal the rifles, I would come and haunt him, turn his cattle mad, and sour his milk till his life became bitter; and I would make the devils in the guns come out and talk to him in a way he would not like. After that he promised to guard them as though they were his father’s spirit. He was a superstitious old Kafir and a great villain.

When our surplus gear was thus disposed of, we laid out what the five of us—Sir Henry, Good, myself, Umbopa, and the Hottentot Ventvögel—would carry. It looked pitifully little when set on the ground, and yet it dragged like sin when you lifted it. For all our paring we could not get below about forty pounds a man. We took:

The three express rifles and two hundred rounds of ammunition.

Two Winchester repeating rifles for Umbopa and Ventvögel, with two hundred rounds.

Five Cochrane water-bottles, four pints each.

Five blankets.

Twenty-five pounds of biltong—sun-dried flesh, dark, hard, salt-edged, and precious as coin.

Ten pounds of mixed beads for gifts.

Medicine: quinine, and a few small instruments.

Knives, compass, matches, pocket filter, tobacco, a trowel, a bottle of brandy, and the clothes on our backs.

That was all. Nothing but what we must have; nothing we could spare.

With difficulty—and by the promise of a good hunting-knife apiece—I induced three miserable natives from the village to come the first stage, twenty miles, carrying each a large gourd of water. We meant to start in the cool of evening; my plan was to have them with us through the first night’s march so we might refill our bottles after. To them I lied gently and said we were going to shoot ostriches, which the desert did indeed hold. They clucked their tongues, shrugged, and said we were mad and would die of thirst—which, if truth be told, seemed a sensible prophecy. But knives are treasures where iron is scarce; so they agreed, likely thinking our deaths would not trouble them when once they had the steel.

All next day we lay about, resting and sleeping. At sunset we ate fresh beef and drank tea—Good remarking mournfully that it would be the last tea for many a day—and then we waited for the moon.

She rose about nine, cool and splendid, washing the land in pale light and laying a silver film over the rolling desert ahead. It looked solemn, quiet, and as indifferent to man as the star-powdered sky above. We got up, tightened straps, shifted loads. Yet we hesitated, for a moment, as men do on the lip of an action that cannot be called back.

We three white men stood together. Umbopa, spear in hand and rifle across his shoulders, fixed his eyes on the desert a few paces ahead as though he were already walking it in his mind. Ventvögel and the hired men waited behind with their water gourds.

“Gentlemen,” Sir Henry said at last, his voice low and deep, “we are going on about as strange a journey as men can make. It is doubtful we can succeed. But we are three men who will stand together for good or evil to the last. Before we start, let us pray to the Power who shapes the destinies of men, that it may please Him to direct our steps.”

He took off his hat and covered his face with his hands. Good and I did the same.

I am not, as a rule, a great hand at praying—few hunters are. Sir Henry I had never heard speak like that before, and only once since; yet I believe faith sat deep in him. Good too has religion in him, though his tongue is apt to run ahead of it. For my own part, I do not remember ever putting up a better prayer in my life than in that minute. The unknown has a way of stripping a man down to what he truly is, and the desert is the unknown made visible.

“And now,” said Sir Henry, “trek!”

So we started.

We had nothing to steer by save the far-off mountains and old José Silvestre’s chart—scratched by a dying man three centuries ago on a strip of linen; a poor thing to hang your life on, and yet our only hope. If we could not find the pool of bad water he had marked in the middle of the desert, roughly sixty miles from our start and as far again from the mountains, then—speaking plainly—we should likely die of thirst. The chances of finding one small stain of water in that vast sea of sand and karoo seemed to me near to nothing. Even if Silvestre marked it true, who could say it had not dried long ago, or been trampled in, or filled up by drifting sand?

Still we went on, silent as shadows in the moonlight, the sand heavy underfoot. Karoo bushes snagged at our boots and made us stumble; sand worked into our veldtschoons and into Good’s shooting-boots, and every few miles we had to halt and shake it out. The night stayed fairly cool, but the air was thick and close—creamy, like warm milk held too long in the mouth. Above us the sky was clean and hard with stars; under us the ground gave no sound but the soft, weary scuff of feet.

The silence was the worst of it—silence with weight, silence that pressed on the ears until a man became too aware of his own breathing. Good could not bear it and began to whistle “The Girl I left behind me,” but the tune fell dead and lugubrious into that emptiness, and he stopped.

Soon after came an incident that shook us at the time and made us laugh later. Good led, because he held the compass and, being a sailor, knew his way about it. We followed in single file. Suddenly he cried out—and vanished as though the sand had swallowed him. Next instant all around us broke loose a most extraordinary tumult: snorts, groans, thudding hooves, and the rushing hiss of bodies moving in the loose sand. In the pale light we glimpsed dim galloping forms half blurred by drifting dust. The natives dropped their gourds and were for bolting, but remembering there was nowhere to bolt to, flung themselves down and howled that it was ghosts.

Sir Henry and I stood staring; and our astonishment turned to downright amazement when we saw Good careering off towards the mountains as though mounted on a horse, halloaing wildly. Then he flung up his arms and came down with a thud.

We had walked into a herd of sleeping quagga. Good had literally fallen across the back of one; the beast rose in a panic and bolted with him astride. I shouted to the others that it was all right and ran to Good, fearing broken bones. But there he sat in the sand, shaken, frightened out of his life, yet unhurt—and, absurdly enough, his eye-glass still fixed in his eye as though it had been screwed there.

After that we trudged on without further mishap till about one o’clock, when we halted for half an hour, drank a little water—no more than wetting the mouth—and went on again.

On and on, until at last the east began to colour, faint at first like blush on a girl’s cheek. Primrose light seeped up, then turned to bars of gold, and dawn slid out across the desert. The stars faded; the moon grew sickly and thin, her face washed out, with mountain ridges cutting it like bones under skin. Then the sun sent spear after spear of light across the wilderness, firing the low mists until the whole desert trembled in a golden glare.

Even then we did not halt, though our shoulders ached and our tongues felt thick in the mouth, because we knew that when the sun stood clear we should not be able to move. At length we saw a little pile of boulders rising from the plain and dragged ourselves to it. By good fortune an overhanging slab made a ledge of shade, and beneath it the sand lay smooth and cool. We crept in like lizards and, after a sip of water and a strip of biltong—salt, leathery, slow to chew—we lay down and were asleep almost before we had settled our loads.

We woke about three in the afternoon to find the three natives preparing to return. They had had enough of desert; no number of knives would have bought another step. We drank more deeply this time, emptied our water-bottles, and refilled them from the gourds, then watched the men go until they were swallowed by distance.

At half past four we started again.

Now the loneliness took on a new edge. We were five specks in an immense, soundless place. A few ostriches moved far off, their necks like question-marks against the light; otherwise there was no life to be seen. It was too dry for game. We saw a deadly-looking cobra or two; no other reptiles. One creature, however, attended us in hordes: the common house fly. They came not as single spies but in battalions, a continual buzzing veil, tasting our eyes, our lips, the corners of wounds, the salt where sweat dried. There is something insolent in the fly—the way it thrives where nothing else can live, the way it insists upon its small music even when the world is burning. And it made me think, not for the first time, how much of a man’s courage is spent not on grand dangers but on petty torments that will not leave him alone.

At sunset we halted and waited for the moon. When she rose—beautiful and indifferent as ever—we went on again, and with one halt at about two in the morning trudged through the night until the sun at last ended our labour.

We drank a little, then flung ourselves down on the sand, worn out, and slept. There was no need to set a watch; nothing in that empty plain threatened us with tooth or bullet. Our enemies were heat, thirst, and flies; and I would sooner, a thousand times, have faced man or beast than that dreadful trinity.

This time there was no kindly rock to shelter us. By seven o’clock the sun had us in its grip and we woke with the sensation—no better word for it—that we were beefsteaks on a gridiron. We were being baked. The heat seemed to draw the very wet out of our blood; the glare hammered the eyes; the air danced over the sand as it dances over a stove.

“Phew,” I gasped, snatching at the cloud of flies that buzzed cheerfully about my head as though this were a picnic.

“My word!” said Sir Henry.

“It is hot!” Good echoed, stating the obvious like a man whose wits had begun to cook.

There was not a thumb’s breadth of shade anywhere. The desert lay around us in an unending, dazzling sheet, the karoo scrub crouching low, too mean to offer help.

“What is to be done?” asked Sir Henry. “We can’t stand this long.”

We stared at each other, minds dulled by the brute force of the sun.

“I have it,” said Good at last. “We must dig a hole, get into it, and cover ourselves with the karoo bushes.”

It was a poor remedy, but better than none. So we set to work with the trowel and our hands, clawing at the sand until, in about an hour, we had scraped out a space ten feet long by twelve wide and two feet deep. Then we cut what scrub we could with our knives and dragged it over.

We crept into the hole and pulled the bushes over us like a lid—everyone except Ventvögel, on whom, being a Hottentot, the heat seemed to have no great effect. The shelter saved us from the direct rays, but the air in that makeshift grave was thick and suffocating, hot with our own breath, tasting of sand, sweat, and bruised karoo. We lay panting, flies crawling and buzzing in the dim, and every now and then we wet our lips with the smallest allowance of water, forcing ourselves to stop where instinct would have drained the lot in an hour.

If the water failed, we knew what would follow, and the knowledge sat with us all day like a weight on the chest. Yet we endured—because there was nothing else to do but endure—and because, somewhere ahead in that glare and emptiness, an old scrap of linen promised a pool of bad water, and beyond that, the pale line of mountains promised either death or the other side.

Two hours later—somewhere about four o’clock—I started up out of sleep as though a hand had seized me by the throat. The first brutal weight of weariness had been paid off; the debt of thirst, compounded all day, came due at once and with interest. In my dream I had been kneeling in a running stream, scooping and sipping, the water quick and cold, the banks green as young mealies after rain. I could hear it too, that private music of a brook that never clangs or rings, only talks to itself over stones. Then my eyes opened on the real world: sand, scrub, and a heat that had turned the air into a thick, breathless paste.

My hands were so dry and horny that when I rubbed my face it felt like scraping leather. My lips and eyelids had glued themselves; I had to work them loose with effort, as though my own skin had begun to betray me. The others slept on—Sir Henry, Good, Umbopa—each drawn into himself, saving what little moisture the body can hide away. There was no bright edge of dawn, no freshening. Even the silence had weight; it was the muffled hush of a land that had forgotten the sound of water.

When it grew light enough to make out print, I took out the little pocket “Ingoldsby Legends” I had carried more from habit than wisdom, and read “The Jackdaw of Rheims.” I came to the line about the “golden ewer… filled with water as pure as any that flows between Rheims and Namur,” and the words might as well have been a knife. My mouth twitched, trying to smack lips that had split and stiffened; the thought of clean, running water made me half mad. I remember thinking—thirst loosens the mind’s bolts—that if the Cardinal himself had stood there with bell, book, and candle, I would have pushed in my dirty head and drunk his consecrated basin dry, soap-suds and all, curse or no curse. The picture of it—me, a burnt-up, grizzled little elephant hunter, bursting into that polite legend like a drought into a garden—struck me as so absurd that I laughed, or rather cracked out a cackle.

That woke the camp. They all sat up and went through the same grim business of rubbing grit from their faces, tearing eyelids apart, testing stiff tongues against dry teeth. Good’s first instinct was for the brandy flask—he looked at it with the longing of a man staring at a roast he is forbidden to touch—but Sir Henry took it from him at once.

“To drink that neat would only hasten it,” he said quietly.

“If we do not find water we shall die,” he added; and there was nothing theatrical in it. It was like stating that a man without air must suffocate.

“If we can trust the old Dom’s map, there should be some,” I said; but I might as well have quoted scripture to a dying bullock. We had all begun to suspect that the map was a tale told by a dead man to amuse himself.

We tipped the water-bottles upside down. Not a bead came. We licked the mouths like dogs. They were dry as bone.

Then I noticed Ventvögel rise. The Hottentot had the look of a creature born under open sky—mean little body, snub nose, eyes trained to the ground as if it were a book. He began to walk, slow and intent, and presently stopped dead and pointed.

“What?” we asked, and went to him.

“Fresh springbok spoor,” I said, disappointed; to my thirst it was merely another pattern in sand.

“Springbucks do not go far from water,” he answered in Dutch.

I had forgotten that law of the veld, and it struck through me like sudden shade. It is astonishing how a man in extremity clutches at the smallest promise and feels a foolish, blessed lift of hope. On a black night even one star seems a sort of salvation.

Ventvögel lifted his nose and sniffed, the way an old impala ram tests the wind for danger. He sniffed again, and his face—ugly as it was—changed.

“I smell water,” he said.

At that we were near jubilant; these wild-bred men have senses civilisation blunts, as if our iron pots and our engine-smoke and our clatter of cities drown out the finer messages of the world.

Just then the sun came up in splendour, and with it came a sight so sudden and so grand that for a moment even thirst stood back, as if awed. There, forty or fifty miles away, glittering like silver laid on brown cloth, rose Sheba’s Breasts; and running away from them, east and west as far as the eye could follow, the great wall of the Suliman Berg.

They stood like the pillars of a gate—two enormous mountains, each a white solemnity, each swelling round and smooth from the plain, and each crowned with a hillock of snow, like the nipple on a woman’s breast. Between them a cliff linked them, sheer as a cut made by some ancient sword, and along their flanks the same cliff-ranges marched away, broken here and there by flat-topped masses like the table mountain at the Cape. Sometimes, in mist and shadow, the hollows beneath take the shape of a recumbent woman veiled in sleep; and whether that is only the mind’s trick or some older memory of the land, I cannot say. But the sight had a weight in it that pressed on the heart. You could almost hear it—a great, mute hush, like a cathedral that has forgotten hymns.

For a little while the morning light played upon snow and brown slopes, then vapours gathered as though the mountains drew a curtain over their own privacy. The pure outlines went ghostlike behind gauze; and when the last clear whiteness blurred, thirst returned like a fire that had only been banked, not put out.

We looked and looked. Sand, scrub, heat—nothing that shone, nothing that promised. We went round the hillock and stared from the other side; still nothing. No pan, no pool, no spring.

“You are a fool,” I said to Ventvögel, anger rising from misery; “there is no water.”

But he kept sniffing, stubborn as a dog.

“I smell it, Baas,” he insisted. “It is somewhere in the air.”

“Yes,” I snapped, “in the clouds—and in two months it will fall and wash our bones.”

Sir Henry, who had the calm of a man whose courage is not dependent on comfort, stroked his yellow beard and looked up at the little sand koppie behind us.

“Perhaps it is on the top of the hill,” he said.

“Rot,” said Good. “Whoever heard of water at the top of a hill?”

“Let us go and look,” I answered; hope is a tyrant, and makes you climb even when you know better.

We scrambled up, Umbopa leading—Umbopa, who carried himself with a dignity that thirst could bend but not break. Halfway, he stopped so suddenly it was as though he had turned to stone.

“Nanzia manzie!” he cried—“Here is water!”

We rushed to him, and there, on the very crown of that sand mound, was a deep cut in the surface, and in it a pool.

It was blackish, brackish-looking, and gave off a faint sour smell, as though old leaves had stewed there; but it was water—or close enough to it for men who had been dying of it. We did not ask how it came there. We did not examine, or weigh risks. We threw ourselves down and drank like animals—no cups, no ceremony—our mouths in the pool, sucking and gulping, the tepid mud tasting like salvation.

Heavens, how we drank.

Then, when our bellies could hold no more, we tore off our clothes and sat down in the pool like pigs wallowing, letting the damp soak through our skins. There are luxuries that men who turn taps in town will never understand: that first wetness on a body that has become a dry husk; the way the ears seem to clear, as if the world’s quiet returns; the way even the heart’s beat sounds less frantic. The pool had its own little sound too—not the clean talk of a stream, but the thick, lazy suck of mud when you moved, and the faint lap against the bank. It was an ugly music, but it was life.

After a while we rose, dripping and revived, and fell upon the biltong we had not been able to stomach for a day. Now it tasted almost rich—salt and dried meat, the chew working the jaw like a machine that had rusted. We ate till we could eat no more, then smoked, then lay down under the overhanging bank where a scrap of shade lay like a blessing, and slept till noon.

We rested there the whole day, guarding that wretched pool as if it were a treasure vault, and not forgetting, in our rough way, to thank the long-dead da Silvestra whose map—scribbled on the tail of a shirt—had set down the place so exactly. The wonder was that such a pan had lasted. The only explanation I could find was some hidden spring deep in the sand feeding it drop by grudging drop.

At moonrise we filled ourselves and our bottles as far as they would go, and started again, spirits raised. That night we covered near five-and-twenty miles. We found no more water, though next day we were lucky enough to find a little shade behind ant-heaps—poor shelter, but even a hand’s breadth of shadow is a mercy when the sun is a hammer.

When the sun rose and for a while cleared the mist, Suliman’s Berg with the two white Breasts, now perhaps twenty miles away, towered over us grander than ever, as if each mile we walked did not bring us nearer so much as make them grow. Toward evening we marched again; and by daylight next morning we stood upon the lower slopes of Sheba’s left breast, the very mountain we had been steering for.

By then the water in our bottles was gone once more. Thirst came back with teeth. We could see no relief until we reached the snow line, far above us—white as a promise and as distant as a lie.

After an hour’s rest—if you can call it rest when your tongue feels like a strip of hide—we began to toil upward in the blazing heat. The mountain’s vast base was built of lava beds, ancient vomit of the earth hardened into rough, dark clinker. It tore at our feet through our worn soles, and each step was a grind of pain. By eleven o’clock we were finished—weak, dizzy, mouths bitter, throats raw. We aimed for some big lumps of lava ahead, hoping for shade.

We reached them, and on a little ridge close by saw something that looked like a miracle: a dense patch of green growth spreading over the black clinker. Soil, made from decomposed lava, had lodged there, and birds had sown it with seed. But a hungry man cannot eat grass like Nebuchadnezzar; that needs special digestive machinery and, perhaps, special madness. So we sat in the shade and groaned, and I, for one, cursed the day I had ever listened to treasure tales.

Then Umbopa rose, and, limping a little, went toward the green patch. A few minutes later that usually dignified man was dancing and shouting like one possessed, waving something green.

We scrambled to him as fast as we could, hope dragging us by the neck.

“What is it, Umbopa, son of a fool?” I shouted in Zulu.

“It is food and water, Macumazahn,” he cried back, holding up his prize.

It was a melon.

And not one only. The green patch was a patch of wild melons—thousands of them, dead ripe, lying like cannon-balls scattered by a careless army.

“Melons!” I yelled, and Good—who could make comedy even with death tapping his shoulder—had his false teeth fixed in one before the word was out.

We ate like men who had forgotten manners and rediscovered childhood. The flesh was pale and watery, the seeds bitter, the rind thick; but the juice ran down the chin and over cracked lips, and it cooled the mouth and throat as if you had poured the moon into them. I think we ate six each before we stopped, and poor as they were, I have rarely tasted anything so exquisite.

They did not satisfy hunger for long—melons are more drink than food—but they quieted thirst. We cut several in halves and set them end on in the hot sun, letting evaporation cool them; it is a simple trick, but it felt like the sort of small domestic cleverness that keeps a family alive through bad seasons. In that desolation, the act of preparing food—even crude food—seemed to stitch a little sanity back into us.

Still, hunger rose again, heavy and empty. We had biltong left, but our stomachs turned from it, and we dared not waste it; who could tell when we would find meat again?

At that moment luck—if luck is only the land’s indifference happening to favour you—sent us a gift. Looking out over the desert below I saw a flock of large birds flying straight toward us.

“Skit, Baas, skit!” whispered Ventvögel, throwing himself flat. We followed suit.

They were a flock of pauw—bustards—and they would pass within fifty yards. I took one of the repeating Winchesters, waited till they were nearly over, then sprang up. As I had hoped, they bunched together on seeing me. I fired twice into the thick of them and brought one down—a fine bird, twenty pounds at least.

In half an hour we had a fire of dry melon-stalks crackling—thin fuel that snapped and hissed, giving off a greenish, sweet smell as it burned. We toasted the bird over it, turning the flesh as best we could, the fat spitting into the flames, the smoke carrying that rich, mouth-watering scent that makes a starving man feel almost cruelly alive. Then we ate, and it was as though warmth spread back through our limbs. We ate that pauw to the bone; nothing was left but leg-bones and beak, and afterwards we could sit and breathe without groaning.

That night we went on again by moonlight, carrying as many melons as we could. As we climbed, the air cooled more and more, a relief so keen it felt like water on the skin. By dawn, as far as we could judge, we were within a dozen miles of the snow line. We found more melons there, and so thirst no longer frightened us; snow would soon be plenty.

But the slope steepened, and we crawled rather than walked, scarcely a mile an hour. That night we ate our last scrap of biltong.

With the exception of the pauw we had seen no living thing upon the mountain. What struck us as odd was that we found no spring or stream, though above us lay a great expanse of snow that must surely melt at times. Later we learned that, for reasons beyond my understanding, all the streams ran down the north side of the range. Then, with water secured by melons and snow, the old terror returned in a different mask: hunger. It began to seem we had escaped death by thirst only to be claimed by starvation.

The next three days I can best give as I wrote them then, for the mind grows blunt with lack of food and records like a machine:

“21st May.—Started 11 a.m., the air being cold enough to travel by day, carrying water-melons. Struggled on all day; no more melons—have passed their district. Saw no game. Halted at sundown, having had no food for many hours. Cold at night.

“22nd.—Started at sunrise, very faint. Only five miles. Found patches of snow and ate it; nothing else. Camped under edge of great plateau. Bitter cold. Drank a little brandy each and huddled together to keep alive. Suffering frightfully from starvation and weariness. Thought Ventvögel would die; he cannot bear cold.

“23rd.—Started when sun had thawed us a little. In dreadful plight. Fear unless we get food this will be our last day. Little brandy left. Good, Sir Henry, Umbopa bear up wonderfully. Ventvögel very bad. Hunger pangs less, but numbness in stomach. Others say same. Now level with the great wall of lava linking the two Breasts; view glorious. Behind us desert rolls to the horizon; before us smooth hard snow, almost level but rising gently, out of the centre of which the nipple of the mountain rises four thousand feet. Not a living thing. God help us; I fear our time has come.”

After that I dropped the journal, because the rest must be told as it happened.

All that day—the 23rd—we struggled slowly up the snow incline, stopping often, lying down to rest, then forcing ourselves up again by will alone. We must have looked like a band of ghosts, loaded with rifles and blankets, dragging feet that no longer belonged to us, our eyes sweeping the whiteness for anything that moved, anything that could be eaten. There was nothing. The snow threw the light into our faces so that the world seemed to glare at our misery.

We made perhaps seven miles. Just before sunset we found ourselves directly under the nipple of Sheba’s left Breast, a vast smooth dome of frozen snow towering thousands of feet above. Weak as we were, the beauty struck us. The setting sun threw flying rays that stained the snow in places blood-red, and set a kind of crown upon the great curve—glory laid on death’s doorstep.

“I say,” gasped Good at last, “we ought to be somewhere near that cave the old gentleman wrote about.”

“Yes,” I said. “If there is a cave.”

“Don’t talk like that,” groaned Sir Henry. “I have faith in the Dom. Remember the water! We shall find it soon.”

“If we don’t find it before dark we are dead men,” I answered; it was not courage, only arithmetic.

We trudged on in silence. Umbopa marched beside me wrapped in his blanket, a leather belt drawn so tight round his stomach to “make his hunger small” that his waist looked like a girl’s. Suddenly he caught my arm.

“Look,” he said.

Two hundred yards away, on the springing slope of the dome, there was a dark hole in the snow.

“It is the cave,” he said.

We made for it, and found that it was indeed the mouth of a cavern—no doubt the same that da Silvestra had spoken of. We had reached it just in time. The sun dropped with startling speed; in these latitudes twilight is brief, and night falls like a curtain. We crept inside.

The cave was not large—perhaps twenty feet long—and smelled of cold stone and old air, a dry, dead smell that had never known smoke or cooking. We huddled together for warmth, shoulder against shoulder, the way men do who have nothing left but each other’s bodies. We swallowed what remained of the brandy—barely a mouthful each—and tried to sleep.

But the cold was a living thing. At that altitude I am sure the temperature fell to fourteen or fifteen degrees below freezing. We had come from a desert that baked the blood, and now the frost moved among us like a thief, taking feeling first from finger, then toe, then cheek, nibbling here, then there. We drew closer, but there was no heat in our starved carcasses. Sometimes one of us slid into a few minutes of uneasy sleep, and perhaps it was well we did not sleep longer; I doubt if we would have woken. It seemed to me that only sheer will kept our hearts beating.

Through the stillness I could hear small sounds—the hiss of breath, the creak of leather, the thin chatter of teeth. Even that mattered: sound is life’s proof in darkness. Ventvögel’s teeth had chattered all night like castanets. Not long before dawn I heard him give a deep sigh, as if letting go of something heavy.

Then his teeth stopped.

I did not think much of it at once; I supposed he had fallen asleep. His back rested against mine, and it grew colder and colder until it felt like ice itself.

At last the air turned grey, then pale, then the first golden arrows of sun slid across the snow outside, and the sun peeped above the lava wall and shone into our cave—cold rays even in their brilliance.

It shone on Ventvögel too. He sat among us with his arms clasped about his knees, and he was stone dead. When I had heard him sigh he had died, and the cold had already stiffened him, freezing him into a posture of patience.

We dragged ourselves away from the body, shocked beyond measure. It is strange—men who can watch a beast die without flinching feel horror at the companionship of a dead man. We left him sitting there, his poor frozen shape turned into a thing of the mountain.

As we moved, sunlight reached farther into the cavern. Someone—one of us—gave a cry of fear. I turned, and saw what he had seen.

At the far end of the cave, where the rock darkened again, sat another form. The head lay fallen on the chest; the long arms hung down. It did not move. It was a dead man too—and, what was worse in that place, a white man.

The sight was more than our shattered nerves could bear. One and all we scrambled out into the cold morning as fast as our half-frozen limbs would take us.

Outside the cavern mouth we stopped short in the hard, clean glare, and for a moment the four of us stood blinking like men who have forgotten how to be ashamed.

“I am going back,” Sir Henry said abruptly.

Good gave him a sharp look. “Back? For what?”

Sir Henry’s voice thickened with a thought he had tried not to feed. “Because it has struck me that—what we saw—may be my brother.”

That fear, once spoken, took hold of us all. We turned and went in again.

The cave swallowed the sunlight at a step. After the dazzle of snow our eyes were useless; the dark lay on us like a hand over the face, and the cold in there had a different smell from the cold outside—old, shut-up, airless; a frost that had never been warmed by breath or flame. We edged forward, hearing only the faint rasp of our own boots and, beneath it, the deep stillness that seemed to have weight.

Presently shapes came back to our sight, and we stood again before the dead man.

Sir Henry went down on his knee, close, close, as if the truth would fly from him unless he pinned it with his eyes. He studied the sunken face, the hollowed temples, the stiff yellow skin stretched drum-tight over bone.

“Thank God,” he said, and the breath left him in a shuddering sigh, “it is not my brother.”

I crouched and looked also. The corpse was that of a tall man in middle age, aquiline nose, grizzled hair, a long black moustache like a frozen brush. The skin had the waxy yellow of old tallow; it clung to the cheekbones and jaw as if it had been glued there by the cold. The man’s clothes had been stripped away, save for what might once have been woollen hose; his body lay naked and stark. Round the stiff neck hung a yellow ivory crucifix.

“Who on earth can it be?” I muttered, though my own voice sounded out of place, like speech in church.

Good’s answer came quick, almost impatient, as though the obvious had been keeping him waiting. “Can’t you guess? Why, the old Dom—José da Silvestra. Who else?”

“Impossible,” I said; my tongue felt thick. “He died three hundred years ago.”

Good gave a short, bitter laugh that steamed once and vanished. “And what is there to prevent him lasting three thousand years in such a larder as this? If the temperature’s low enough, flesh keeps like mutton hung in a freezing-room, and Heaven knows no sun ever bakes this place. No jackal comes to worry it, no worm to take a share. His slave, that he speaks of, must have stripped him and left him. One man couldn’t dig a grave in this rock.”

He stooped and picked up a queerly-shaped bone, scraped and sharpened at the end into a point. “Look. Here’s the cleft bone he used to draw the map.”

For a moment we only stared. Hunger and cold and fear slid aside, and awe stepped into their place, as if some old tale had pushed open a door into the present. Sir Henry pointed to a small wound on the Dom’s left arm, the skin split and darkened there.

“And this is where he got his ink from,” he said. “Did ever man see such a thing?”

There was no doubt now. The proof sat stiff in the half-dark, crowned with its own dreadful certainty. The directions we had followed—scratched with that bone pen, stained with a dying man’s blood—had led us to the very author of them, kept as if in a glass case by the merciless cold. The crucifix that had touched his lips still lay upon his breast. The thought of it appalled me. It was like finding the hand that wrote your name in a book, still gripping the pen.

In my mind the last scene rebuilt itself without my bidding: the traveller alone beyond all help, cold gnawing him from the outside and hunger from within; the slow dimming of strength; and yet the stubborn drive to leave a message—directions, warning, temptation—for men unborn. There was a loneliness in that death that no words could cover. For an instant, too, I fancied I saw a likeness between those rigid features and the face of his descendant—my poor friend, dead these twenty years—though perhaps that was only my mind grasping for familiar ground in a place where time itself felt broken.

“Let us go,” Sir Henry said at last, very low. Then, after a pause: “Stay. We will give him a companion.”

He lifted poor Ventvögel’s body—so light now, so pitifully easy to move—and laid it near the Dom, the proud white man of a past age and the poor Hottentot, side by side, as if the mountain had demanded its due from both. Sir Henry then stooped over the Dom and with a jerk snapped the rotten string of the crucifix—his fingers were too numb to unfasten it properly. He took it. I, for my part, took the bone pen. Even now it lies before me when I write; sometimes I use it to sign my name, and the scratch it makes seems to come from colder air than any ink.

We left them there, keeping their eternal vigil in the heart of the snows, and crept out into the sun again, wondering in our hearts how many hours it would be before we lay as they lay.

After about half a mile we reached the edge of the plateau. From the desert side the nipple of the mountain had looked as though it rose in the centre; in truth it did not, and now the land fell away before us. At first we could see nothing, for the morning fog lay in thick billows, filling the hollows like milk poured into a basin. Then the upper layers thinned and tore, and through a gap we saw, far below at the end of a long slope of snow, a patch of green grass—real green, the colour of life—perhaps five hundred yards beneath us. A stream ran through it, flashing like a knife blade.

By the stream stood and lay a group of great antelopes, ten or fifteen of them, basking in the sun as if they owned it. At that distance we could not tell their kind, only the bulk and the curve of horns.

The sight struck us with a joy so sharp it was almost pain. Food—food with warmth in it, blood in it, strength enough to lift a foot and set it down again.

But how to get it was another matter. They were six hundred yards away at least; a long shot at the best of times, and we were half-dead men with shaking hands and eyes tired from snow-glare. We spoke quickly, weighing the chance of stalking. The wind was wrong, and there was the blinding white slope between us; even crawling we would show like beetles on a sheet.

“We must try from here,” Sir Henry said. “Which, Quatermain—the repeaters or the expresses?”

It was a nasty choice. The Winchesters were sighted to a thousand yards, but they struck with less authority; the expresses, though sighted only to three hundred and fifty, carried those expanding bullets that, if they hit, would drop a beast as if it had been pole-axed. Life hung on that “if.”

“We risk the expresses,” I decided. “Each take the buck opposite him. Aim high at the point of the shoulder. Umbopa—give the word. We fire together.”

We lay into our aim. There was a silence so complete that the little sounds of our own breathing seemed loud and foolish in it, and beyond us the snow held its own hush, like a great ear listening.

“Fire,” Umbopa said in Zulu.

The three rifles spoke almost as one. The reports cracked across the stillness and went rolling away in a hundred echoes. Smoke drifted and hung in grey sheets. When it cleared, one of the great bucks was on its back, legs beating the air in furious spasms, the last stubborn refusal of life.

We yelled—an ugly, broken sound, half triumph, half sob—and then, weak as we were, ran down the snow towards it. Ten minutes later its heart and liver lay before us, steaming faintly in the clean cold.

And then we stopped, struck dumb by a fresh misery. No firewood. Nothing but snow and rock. We could not cook.

Good looked at the bloody prize, then at us. “Starving men should not be fanciful,” he said shortly. “We must eat raw meat.”

There was no arguing with hunger. We took the heart and liver and buried them a few minutes in a patch of snow, letting the cold tighten them so they would slice cleaner. We washed them in the stream—ice water that made the hands ache to the bone—and then we ate.

The taste was not what horror imagines. There was iron and salt, a clean wild sweetness, and a warmth that seemed to spread outward as the first mouthful went down. We tore and chewed greedily, yet with some care, remembering what happens when a starved stomach is suddenly overfilled. We forced ourselves to stop while still hungry.

In a quarter of an hour we were changed men. Blood came back to the skin; the faintness lifted. It was as though the antelope’s strength had crossed into our veins, and the cold no longer sat so heavily upon the shoulders.

“Thank Heaven!” Sir Henry said. “That brute has saved our lives. What is it, Quatermain?”

I went to look. It was donkey-sized, with big curved horns and a thick coat; brown, with faint red stripes. I had never seen its like. I learned later the natives call it an inco, rare, only found at great height where other game will not live. The bullet had struck high in the shoulder. Whose shot did the work we could not tell; Good, I suspect, privately entered it in the account-book of his own marksmanship, and nobody was cruel enough to dispute him.

Only then did we properly look about us. While Umbopa cut away the best meat we could carry, we lifted our eyes to the world below. The mist had been sucked up by the sun—it was about eight o’clock—and the land lay open like a revealed secret.

Behind us and above, Sheba’s Breasts towered, their snowy domes coldly splendid. Before us, five thousand feet beneath, spread league on league of the loveliest champaign country. Forests lay in deep patches, dark as spilled ink; a great river wound through, silver where it caught the light. To the left stretched rolling grasslands with what might have been herds—game or cattle we could not tell at that distance—ringed by a wall of far mountains. To the right the country rose and fell in solitary hills, and between them we could see cultivated strips and groups of dome-shaped huts.

Everything glittered under the glad sunlight—rivers like bright snakes, peaks like teeth with twisted wreaths of snow. And over all hung the living breath of the place: not only its look, but its sound. Even from up there we could sense the difference from the desert’s dead hush. Water spoke everywhere—thin and quick where it ran over stones, deeper where it gathered. Wind moved through grass and trees in a soft, continuous rustle like a long-held note. Birds flashed and, though we could not yet pick their calls clearly at that height, the very air seemed tuned. After the brutal silence of snow and the mind-killing sameness of the desert wind, this layered music felt like health itself.

Two things struck us. First, this country lay far higher than the desert we had crossed—three thousand feet at least. Second, all the rivers ran from south to north. On the southern face there had been no water; on the northern, streams everywhere, most of them seeming to feed that great river.

We sat and stared, our mouths closed, as if speech would break the spell.

At last Sir Henry said, “Isn’t there something on the map about Solomon’s Great Road?”

I nodded, still gazing.

“Well—look. There it is.” He pointed a little to our right.

We looked, and there, winding down toward the plain, lay a broad road, like a pale ribbon laid by a giant’s hand. At first we had missed it because it turned behind broken country on reaching the level.

Oddly, we did not exclaim much. Wonder was becoming a tired sense, like a muscle overused. We accepted the road the way one accepts a new rule of weather in a strange climate.

“It must be quite near if we cut to the right,” Good said. “Hadn’t we better start?”

We agreed. After washing our faces and hands in the stream—we lingered on that simple domestic act as if it were a ritual restoring us to ourselves—we set off. For a mile or more we crossed boulders and patches of snow, then topped a little rise and found the road at our feet.

It was magnificent—cut out of solid rock, fifty feet wide at least, and seemingly kept in order. The strangest part was that it appeared to begin there. A hundred paces behind us, toward Sheba’s Breasts, it vanished entirely; the mountain surface lay strewn with boulders and snow, as though the road had been erased.

“What do you make of this, Quatermain?” Sir Henry asked.

I could only shake my head.

“I have it,” Good said. “It must once have run right over the range and across the desert, but the sand has buried it on that side, and up here it’s been obliterated by some volcanic eruption—molten lava.”

We seized on the explanation, not because it satisfied the mind, but because it allowed the feet to keep moving. And so we went down.

With full stomachs, travelling downhill on that splendid pathway was another world from our desperate climb. The air softened by the mile, losing its knife edge, taking on a balm that smelled faintly of leaf and damp earth. The beauty before us grew more luminous with every turn. Only the thought of Ventvögel, and that grim cave where he now lay with da Silvestra, kept cheerfulness from rising too high.

As an engineering work the road was astonishing. No difficulty had stopped the old builders. At one place a ravine three hundred feet across and a hundred deep had been filled in with massive dressed blocks, arches pierced through at the base to let water pass, so that the road strode over the gulf as if it were nothing. At another point it was cut in zigzags along the face of a precipice five hundred feet deep. In a third it tunneled through the base of a ridge for thirty yards or more.

In the tunnel we saw the sides covered with quaint sculptures—mailed figures in chariots, and, in one panel of extraordinary beauty, a battle scene with captives being marched away in the distance.

Sir Henry examined it and then said, “They call it Solomon’s Road, but in my humble opinion Egyptians were here before Solomon’s people ever set foot on it. If this isn’t Egyptian or Phoenician handiwork, it’s very like it.”

By midday we had descended far enough to find brushwood. First scattered bushes, then more and more, and at last the road wound through a grove of silver trees like those on Table Mountain at the Cape—shining leaves that caught the light as if each one were rubbed with metal. I had never seen them elsewhere and their presence here startled me.

Good’s whole face brightened at the sight—not only for the warmth of fire, but for the civilised comfort it promised. “Ah! Here’s plenty of wood. Let’s stop and cook dinner. I’ve about digested that raw heart.”

Nobody argued. We left the road and went to a stream babbling close by. Soon we had a fire going—dry boughs snapping, the smell of resin and heated sap rising deliciously, smoke curling into blue air. Umbopa had cut generous hunks of inco flesh; we skewered them on sharpened sticks and toasted them in the Kafir fashion, turning them patiently so the outside browned and crisped while the inside stayed juicy. Fat hissed as it fell into the coals; the scent was rich, meaty, and almost sweet, and it seemed to fill the hollow places in the mind as much as in the belly. We ate with relish, letting the hot juice run on the tongue, feeling the strength of it settle in the limbs.

Afterward we lit our pipes. The simple routine—fire, food, tobacco—felt like the rebuilding of a home in miniature, a domestic island in a wild sea.

The brook’s banks were clothed in dense masses of gigantic maidenhair fern, and among it grew feathery tufts of wild asparagus. The water sang as it went, not loudly but cheerfully, its voice broken into bright little notes by stones. Soft air murmured through the silver leaves overhead. Doves cooed somewhere close, their calls round and steady, and bright-winged birds flashed from bough to bough like living gems, making quick, thin sounds that stitched the silence into something gentle. After the savage, dead acoustics of the snowfields—the way sound had cracked and fled away—this layered, living soundscape worked on the nerves like medicine. One could almost believe that a place which sounded so right could not mean to kill us.

The magic of it, combined with the relief of danger apparently behind, charmed us into a kind of quiet. Sir Henry and Umbopa spoke together in low voices, half broken English and Kitchen Zulu, earnest and intent. I lay on the fern bed, its scent green and clean beneath my cheek, and watched them through half-closed eyes.

Then I missed Good.

I raised my head and found him down by the stream. He had been bathing and now wore nothing but his flannel shirt and boots. His neatness—always lurking in him like an indestructible instinct—had risen again as soon as he had water and a moment’s peace. He had washed his gutta-percha collar, shaken out his trousers, coat, and waistcoat, and was folding them as neatly as though he were packing for a railway journey. He frowned at the rents and tears our journey had made. Then he took his boots, scrubbed them with fern, and finally rubbed them over with a piece of fat he had carefully saved from the inco, until they looked, comparatively speaking, respectable. He inspected them through his eye-glass as gravely as a man judging a regiment on parade.

From a little bag he produced a pocket-comb with a tiny looking-glass fixed to it and began arranging his hair with meticulous care. He paused, studied the effect, felt his chin, where ten days’ beard bristled like scrub.

Surely, I thought, he is not going to shave.

But he was. He washed the bit of fat thoroughly in the stream, drew out a small guarded razor, and rubbed his face with the fat, working it in like some miserable substitute for soap. Then he began to shave.

It was a painful business. He groaned and muttered, wrestling the blade over stubble that was half iron in that cold. Watching him there—half naked, half lathered in melted fat, solemnly keeping up the habits of a dressing-room in the heart of a strange land—I was shaking with silent laughter, when something bright flashed past his head.

Good sprang up with a violent oath—if it had not been a safety razor he would surely have cut his throat—and I started up too.

Not twenty paces from me, and perhaps ten from him, stood a group of men: very tall, copper-coloured, some wearing great plumes of black feathers and short cloaks of leopard skin. In front of them was a youth of about seventeen, his hand still raised, body bent forward in the poised attitude of a spear-thrower. The flash had been from the weapon he had hurled.

An older man, soldier-like in bearing, stepped out, caught the youth by the arm, and spoke sharply to him. Then the whole party advanced on us.

Sir Henry, Good, and Umbopa snatched up their rifles, lifting them threateningly. The men came on as though the guns were sticks. It struck me they did not know what firearms were, or they would not have walked so boldly into their mouths.

“Put down your guns!” I shouted, because our only chance lay in not turning the first meeting into a slaughter. They obeyed. I stepped forward and addressed the older man.

“Greeting,” I said in Zulu, not knowing what other tongue to try.

To my surprise he understood. “Greeting,” he answered—not in Zulu proper, but in a dialect so near it that Umbopa and I could follow without trouble. Later we learned it was an old-fashioned form of Zulu, as Chaucer’s English is to modern speech: familiar in shape, strange in turn and cadence, heavy with older ways of naming the world.

He looked from my face to Sir Henry’s and Good’s and back again, weighing us with eyes that missed nothing.

“Whence come you?” he asked. “Who are you? Why are the faces of three of you white, and the face of the fourth as the face of our mother’s sons?” He pointed at Umbopa.

I glanced at Umbopa, and the truth of it hit me with a chill: his face and great build matched these men’s, as though he had been cut from the same timber. But there was no time to follow that thought.

“We are strangers,” I said slowly, choosing words he could grasp, “and we come in peace. This man is our servant.”

“You lie,” he answered flatly. “No strangers can cross the mountains where all things perish. But what matter your lies? If you are strangers you must die. No strangers may live in the land of the Kukuanas. It is the king’s law. Prepare, then, to die, O strangers!”

As he spoke I saw hands drift down toward the heavy knives at their sides.

Good leaned toward me. “What does that beggar say?”

“He says we’re going to be killed,” I answered.

“Oh, Lord!” Good groaned. And then, in that odd little habit of his when perplexed, he put his hand to his false teeth, pulled the top set down, and let them spring back with a snap.

It saved our lives.

The Kukuanas gave one simultaneous yell of horror and bolted backward several yards, as though he had turned his mouth into a trap.

“What’s wrong?” I whispered.

“It’s his teeth,” Sir Henry hissed. “He moved them. Take them out, Good, take them out!”

Good, bewildered but obedient, slipped the set into the sleeve of his flannel shirt.

Curiosity quickly overcame fear. The men crept forward again, eyes fixed on Good as if he were a cooking-pot that had begun to speak.

“How is it,” the old man demanded solemnly, “that this fat man—whose body is clothed and whose legs are bare; who grows hair on one side of his sickly face and not on the other; who wears one shining, transparent eye—how is it, I ask, that he has teeth which move of themselves, coming away from the jaws and returning of their own will?”

“Open your mouth,” I said to Good.

Good curled his lip and grinned like an angry dog, showing two thin red lines of gum as innocent of teeth as a newborn elephant’s. The men gasped and cried out.

“Where are his teeth? With our eyes we saw them!”

Good turned his head slowly and, with a gesture of perfect contempt, swept his hand across his mouth. Then he grinned again—and there were two rows of excellent white teeth.

The youth who had thrown the knife flung himself down and howled in sheer terror. The older man’s knees knocked together.

“I see that you are spirits,” he stammered. “Did ever man born of woman have hair on one side of his face and not on the other, or a round transparent eye, or teeth that moved and melted away and grew again? Pardon us, O my lords.”

It was luck—wild, ridiculous luck—and I seized it before it could slip.

“It is granted,” I said, putting into my voice all the authority I could counterfeit. “You shall know the truth. We come from another world, though we are men like you. We come from the biggest star that shines at night.”

A groan went through them like wind through dry grass.

“Oh! oh!” they murmured, staring at us as though the very words had altered the air.

All that afternoon we held to the great road, which ran steadily north-west as though it knew its own mind better than any traveller could. Infadoos and Scragga walked beside us, their followers pacing a good hundred steps ahead, their feet keeping time with the road’s hard, ancient skin. Even before I spoke, I could hear the country’s voice changing—wind combing through grass on the slopes, the far-away chuckle of hidden water, the soft hiss of sandals on packed earth—sounds so clean and various that the mind felt rinsed of its old noises.

“Infadoos,” I said at last, “who made this road?”

He glanced down at it with a kind of wary respect, as a man might look at a hearth-stone that belonged to his grandfather’s grandfather. “It was made, my lord, of old time—none know how or when, not even Gagool, the wise woman, who has lived for generations. We are not old enough to remember its making. None can fashion such roads now, but the king suffers no grass to grow upon it.”

The road did indeed look as if it had been swept and tended like a threshing-floor—no tuft, no creeping green permitted to forget the command laid upon it. The very discipline of the place seemed to travel up through our soles.

“And the writings on the cave walls we passed,” I went on—those Egyptian-like carvings that had stared at us with stony eyes—“whose hand cut them?”

“The hands that made the road wrote the wonderful writings. We know not who wrote them.”

There was something in that answer that sat oddly in the mouth, as though the sentence had been repeated for generations until the meaning had worn smooth. A people can inherit a phrase the way they inherit a seed, and yet forget the taste of the fruit.

“When did the Kukuana people come into this country?”

Infadoos lifted his spear and pointed northward. “My lord, the race came down here like the breath of a storm ten thousand thousand moons ago, from the great lands which lie there beyond. They could travel no further because of the high mountains which ring in the land, so say the old voices of our fathers that have descended to us the children, and so says Gagool, the wise woman, the smeller out of witches.” His spear moved again, indicating the ring of snow-clad peaks.

“The country was good. So they settled here and grew strong and powerful, and now our numbers are like the sea sand, and when Twala the king calls up his regiments their plumes cover the plain so far as the eye of man can reach.”

“And if mountains wall you in,” I asked, “who is there for those regiments to fight?”

“Nay, my lord, the country is open there towards the north, and now and again warriors sweep down upon us in clouds from a land we know not, and we slay them. It is the third part of the life of a man since there was a war. Many thousands died in it, but we destroyed those who came to eat us up. So since then there has been no war.”

“Your warriors must grow weary of resting on their spears.”

“My lord, there was one war just after we destroyed the people that came down upon us, but it was a civil war—dog ate dog.”

“How was that?”

Infadoos’ voice lowered. The road carried sound well; it seemed wrong to spill this story into it, as though one were dropping blood into a spring.

“My lord the king, my half-brother, had a brother born at the same birth, and of the same woman. It is not our custom, my lord, to suffer twins to live; the weaker must always die. But the mother of the king hid away the feebler child, which was born the last, for her heart yearned over it, and that child is Twala the king. I am his younger brother, born of another wife.”

“Well?”

“My lord, Kafa, our father, died when we came to manhood, and my brother Imotu was made king in his place, and for a space reigned and had a son by his favourite wife. When the babe was three years old, just after the great war, during which no man could sow or reap, a famine came upon the land.”

As he spoke I could almost feel the tightness of empty bellies, the way hunger makes men listen only to the loudest voice and forget the older, quieter truths. In such seasons the soundscape itself turns harsh—children crying thinly, pots knocked too hard, the brittle quarrel of dry sticks.

“The people murmured because of the famine, and looked round like a starved lion for something to rend. Then it was that Gagool, the wise and terrible woman, who does not die, made proclamation, saying, ‘The king Imotu is no king.’ And at the time Imotu was sick with a wound, and lay in his kraal not able to move.

“Then Gagool went into a hut and led out Twala, my half-brother, and twin brother to the king, whom she had hidden among the caves and rocks since he was born, and stripping the moocha from his loins, showed the people the mark of the sacred snake coiled round his middle, wherewith the eldest son of the king is marked at birth, and cried aloud, ‘Behold your king whom I have saved for you even to this day!’”

Infadoos’ eyes were fixed ahead, as though he still saw that moment, the crowd surging like wind-driven grass.

“Now the people being mad with hunger, and bereft of reason and the knowledge of truth, cried out—‘The king! The king!’ But I knew that it was not so, for Imotu my brother was the elder of the twins, and our lawful king.

“Then just as the tumult was at its height Imotu the king, though he was very sick, crawled from his hut holding his wife by the hand, and followed by his little son Ignosi—that is, by interpretation, the Lightning.

“‘What is this noise?’ he asked. ‘Why cry ye The king! The king?’

“Then Twala, his twin brother, born of the same woman and in the same hour, ran to him, and taking him by the hair, stabbed him through the heart with his knife. And the people, being fickle and ever ready to worship the rising sun, clapped their hands and cried, ‘Twala is king! Now we know that Twala is king!’”

“And the queen?” I asked. “And the child Ignosi? Did Twala kill them too?”

“Nay, my lord. When she saw that her lord was dead the queen seized the child with a cry and ran away. Two days afterward she came to a kraal very hungry, and none would give her milk or food, now that her lord the king was dead, for all men hate the unfortunate.”

At the mention of milk and refusal, something in me recoiled. A land might be beautiful as a garden, but if a woman cannot be given a calabash when her child’s mouth is dry, then the garden has a rot at its roots. To deny food is a form of speech too—a brutal vocabulary that says, You are no longer one of us.

“But at nightfall,” Infadoos continued, “a little child, a girl, crept out and brought her corn to eat, and she blessed the child, and went on towards the mountains with her boy before the sun rose again. And there she must have perished, for none have seen her since, nor the child Ignosi.”

“Then if Ignosi lived,” I said slowly, “he would be the true king of the Kukuanas.”

“That is so, my lord; the sacred snake is round his middle. If he lives he is king; but, alas! he is long dead.”

As he finished, he pointed down to the plain beneath us. There, set like an island on the open land, lay a vast collection of huts, fenced, and beyond that fence a great ditch running like a dark collar around it.
