“See, my lord. That is the kraal where the wife of Imotu was last seen with the child Ignosi. It is there we shall sleep to-night, if, indeed,” he added, his doubt returning like a chill under warmth, “my lords sleep at all upon this earth.”

“When we are among the Kukuanas,” I said, putting on as much grandeur as I could manage for the benefit of our audience, “we do as the Kukuanas do.”

I turned quickly to address Good, who was tramping behind in a sulk, entirely occupied with the hopeless business of keeping his flannel shirt from flapping in the evening breeze. Instead I walked squarely into Umbopa, who had been immediately behind me, and very clearly listening. His face had the strained look of a man trying to catch a half-forgotten tune—he had the air of one who hears a name in a crowd and cannot at once remember whose voice it belongs to. The story of Twala and Ignosi had lodged in him like a thorn.

We pressed on at a good rate toward the kraal. The mountains we had crossed now rose behind us like great shoulders, and Sheba’s Breasts, those twin domes of snow, were veiled modestly in thin wreaths of mist. The land grew lovelier with each mile: vegetation luxuriant without being tropical, sun bright and warm without burning, and a gracious breeze moving softly along the odorous slopes. Every scent seemed distinct—crushed herbs underfoot, sun-warmed earth, the faint sweetness of flowering shrubs—carried on air that sounded clean when it moved, not clogged with the mechanical roar that follows men in their modern works. Here even silence had texture; it was not dead, but full of small living noises that made one feel held rather than alone.

Soon after we started, Infadoos had sent a runner ahead to warn the kraal of our arrival. The fellow had departed at such speed that he seemed to pour himself along the road. Running, Infadoos told me, was much practised among his people, and from the way the air rang with the patter of feet in the regiment behind, I could well believe it.

The result of the message showed itself when we were still two miles out. Company after company of men issued from the gates and marched toward us in ordered lines.

Sir Henry laid a hand on my arm. “It looks as though we are going to meet with a warm reception,” he said.

Something in his tone caught Infadoos’ attention. “Let not my lords be afraid,” he said quickly. “For in my breast there dwells no guile. This regiment is one under my command, and comes out by my orders to greet you.”

I nodded with what I hoped was easy majesty, though my inside did not match it. A greeting of three thousand armed men is the sort that asks a man to trust his hosts more than his own nerves.

About half a mile from the gates a long stretch of rising ground sloped gently up from the road, and there the companies formed. It was a splendid sight: each company, about three hundred strong, charging swiftly up the rise with flashing spears and waving plumes, and then snapping into stillness as if carved from metal. By the time we reached the slope, twelve such companies—three thousand six hundred men—had taken position along the road.

When we came opposite the first company we could examine them properly, and I confess I stared like a child at a fair. They were magnificent—men of mature age, mostly veterans of about forty, not one under six feet, many six feet three or four. On their heads were heavy black plumes of Sakaboola feathers like those worn by our guides. About their waists and beneath the right knee were circlets of white ox-tails; their left hands held round shields some twenty inches across.

These shields were curious things: an iron plate beaten thin for the framework, over it stretched milk-white ox-hide, tight as a drum-skin. Each man bore a short, very heavy two-edged spear with a wooden shaft, the blade broad—about six inches across—made for stabbing in close quarters, not throwing. Along with this bangwan each carried three great knives, tollas, each about two pounds in weight: one in the ox-tail girdle, two fixed behind the shield. They cast these tollas with accuracy up to fifty yards, and it was their custom, on charging, to hurl a volley as they closed.

Each company stood immovable till we were abreast. Then, at a signal from its officer—distinguished by a leopard-skin cloak, standing some paces in front—every spear lifted as one, and from three hundred throats came the royal salute, sudden and deep: “Koom!”

It struck the air like a wave breaking. The cry rolled along the line in measured shocks, and for all its menace it was strangely beautiful—human voices used like an instrument, disciplined, communal. I could not help thinking how such a sound, repeated year on year, becomes part of a nation’s memory—more durable than carvings on cave walls, unless some new noise comes to drown it out.

As soon as we had passed, each company fell in behind us, until at last the whole regiment of the “Greys”—so called from their white shields, the crack corps of the Kukuanas—marched at our rear with a tread that shook the ground.

At length we branched from Solomon’s Great Road and came to the wide fosse that encircled the kraal, at least a mile round, fenced with a strong palisade of tree-trunks. At the gateway the fosse was spanned by a rough drawbridge, which the guard let down to admit us.

Inside, the kraal was laid out with notable sense. A wide pathway ran through the centre, crossed at right angles by other paths, dividing the huts into square blocks—the quarters of each company. The huts were dome-shaped, built like Zulu huts of wattle framework beautifully thatched with grass, but larger, and with doorways tall enough for a man to walk through. Each had a verandah about six feet wide, paved with powdered lime trodden hard until it shone pale and clean.

Along each side of the central pathway stood hundreds of women, drawn out by curiosity to look at us. They were, for a native race, exceedingly handsome—tall, graceful, wonderfully made. Their hair, though short, was more curly than woolly; the features were often aquiline; the lips not unpleasantly thick. Most striking was their quiet, dignified air. They watched as though watching were an art, not a commotion. Not a rude jest, not a shouted remark, not the savage chorus one hears in other places. Their silence had a weight, and in that hush you could hear small domestic sounds leaking from behind the huts—the clack of a pot-lid, a baby’s breathy complaint, the dry rasp of a broom on hard earth.

Even when old Infadoos, with a sly motion, directed their attention to the crowning marvel of poor Good’s “beautiful white legs,” they did not erupt. Their dark eyes fixed upon that new, snowy whiteness—Good’s skin being as fair as milk—then they let their faces stay still, as though admiration were something one kept inside like warm porridge. Good, modest by nature, found the scrutiny quite enough, and coloured beneath it as much as he could.

At the centre of the kraal, Infadoos halted at the doorway of a large hut, around which at a little distance stood a circle of smaller huts.

“Enter, Sons of the Stars,” he said in his magniloquent manner, “and deign to rest awhile in our humble habitations. A little food shall be brought to you, so that ye may have no need to draw your belts tight from hunger; some honey and some milk, and an ox or two, and a few sheep; not much, my lords, but still a little food.”

“It is good,” said I. “Infadoos, we are weary with travelling through realms of air; now let us rest.”

We entered and found it prepared for comfort: couches of tanned skins laid out, water ready for washing. The air inside held the clean animal smell of hide, smoke clinging to the thatch, and the faint sharpness of lime from the verandah. After a while shouting rose outside, and when I stepped to the doorway I saw a line of damsels coming in with offerings—milk in calabashes, roasted mealies, and honey in a pot that glowed amber when the light struck it. Behind them came youths driving a fat young ox, its hide glossy and its breath steaming softly in the cooler air.

We received the gifts. Then one of the young men drew the knife from his girdle and, with practised economy, cut the ox’s throat. In ten minutes it was dead, skinned, and jointed—work done with the quick surety that belongs to people who do such things not as spectacle but as part of life. The best meat was cut off for us, and the rest, in our name, I presented to the warriors about us, who took it and distributed the “white lords’ gift” among themselves.

Umbopa set to work at once, taking charge as though the pot and fire were his birthright. With the help of an extremely pleasing young woman he set our portion to boil in a large earthenware pot over a fire built outside the hut. He judged the heat by ear as much as by eye—the change from lively bubbling to a steadier simmer, the soft tapping of bone against clay when the pot was stirred. The smell of meat rising in steam mingled with smoke and the sweet edge of mealies; it made the kraal feel suddenly less like a parade-ground and more like a home.

When it was nearly ready we sent to Infadoos and asked him and Scragga to join us. They came presently and sat on little stools—there were several about the hut, for the Kukuanas do not, as a rule, squat on their haunches like the Zulus—and helped us through our dinner.

Infadoos was all affability and ceremony. Scragga, the king’s son, ate and watched. It struck me that his doubt grew with every ordinary act. The first shock of our whiteness and our “magic” had overawed him; but now that he saw we chewed meat, drank milk, and wiped grease from our fingers like other men, the awe began to wear thin, and something sullen and suspicious showed beneath it. That change made me uneasy. It is one thing to be feared as a wonder; it is another to be measured as a rival.

During the meal Sir Henry suggested to me, in an undertone, that we might try to find out whether our hosts knew anything of his brother’s fate, whether they had ever seen or heard of him. I thought it wiser to say nothing yet. It is hard to explain a missing relative when one has been introduced as a thing that fell from the sky.

After supper we produced our pipes and lit them. This simple act astonished Infadoos and Scragga more than our rifles had done. The Kukuanas grow tobacco widely, but use it only for snuff, and could not at first recognise the leaf in this new shape, nor understand how a man could willingly swallow scented smoke and then sit contented as though he had eaten.

I asked Infadoos when we were to proceed on our journey, and was pleased to hear that preparations had already been made for us to leave in the morning. Messengers had gone to inform Twala of our coming. The king, it seemed, was at his chief place, Loo, making ready for the great annual feast held in the first week of June. At that gathering all regiments—save certain detachments left for garrison duty—are brought up and paraded before the king, and the great annual witch-hunt is held.

We were to start at dawn. Infadoos, who would accompany us, expected we should reach Loo on the night of the second day unless accident delayed us or rivers ran swollen.

When they had given us this information, our visitors bade us good-night. We arranged our watch, turn and turn about. Then three of us flung ourselves down on the skins and slept the sweet, heavy sleep of the weary, while the fourth sat up listening—not only for footsteps that should not be there, but for the kraal’s own night-music: distant murmurs, a baby’s brief cry, the crack of a dying fire, and beyond the palisade the steady breathing of the plain.

It is not needful that I set down every mile of that tramp to Loo, nor each halt and start along Solomon’s Great Road, for the road itself did the talking—straight as a knife-cut, hard-beaten, and old with a kind of age that seems to hum under the foot. For two full days we travelled, and the farther we went the more the country fattened, as though the earth had been well tended by patient hands for generations. Round every kraal lay belts of cultivation—patches of mealies with their tassels whispering, beans climbing their poles, gourds swelling in the sun—so that the air held the green smell of bruised leaf and warm soil. And everywhere there were soldiers. In Kukuanaland, as among other fighting nations, every able-bodied man was a man under arms: war lay in the daily routine like a pot always on the edge of the fire.

All day companies of warriors overtook us—thousands upon thousands hurrying toward Loo for the great yearly review and feast. Their shields flashed in the sun, their plumes nodded like grass in wind, and the road rang with the measured thud of feet and the dry click of spear-hafts, a great moving music that rose and fell with the land. There is a comfort in a clean soundscape—wind, footfall, distant cattle, a song started and taken up properly—very different from the harsh clatter and ceaseless grinding that civilised places have taught us to endure. Here the noises belonged to life.

At sunset of the second day we rested on the crown of some heights where the road ran. Before us, spread out like a mat, lay Loo upon a rich plain—an enormous town for a native place, five miles round at least, with outlying kraals like fingers, built for regiments to camp in on great occasions. Northward, two miles off, stood a curious hill shaped like a horseshoe, and I noted it because it sat there like a thing waiting to be understood.

Through the middle of the grass city ran a river, dividing it into two halves, bridged here and there; it was the same stream we had seen from the slopes of Sheba’s Breasts. And far away—sixty or seventy miles by my guessing—three great mountains, snow-capped and set like the points of a triangle, sprang sheer and harsh from the level. They were nothing like Sheba’s Breasts with their rounded softness. These were abrupt, cut and shadowed, like black teeth with white crowns.

Infadoos saw us looking and spoke, as though giving a plain direction about a footpath.

“The road ends there,” he said, pointing. “At the Three Witches.”

“Why does it end?” I asked.

He shrugged in that way of his that is half ignorance, half refusal. “Who knows? The mountains are full of caves, and there is a great pit between them. There the wise men of old went to get what they came for. There now our kings are buried—in the Place of Death.”

“And what was it they came for?” I pressed him.

“Nay, I know not.” His eyes flicked quick and sharp. “My lords who have dropped from the Stars should know.”

“You are right,” I said, taking his hint as one takes salt—sparingly. “In the Stars we learn many things. I have heard that the wise men of old came there to find bright stones—pretty playthings—and yellow iron.”

“My lord is wise,” he answered, suddenly cold. “I am but a child. My lord must speak with Gagool the old at the king’s place. She is wise even as my lord,” and he turned away.

When he had gone I pointed the mountains out to the others. “There are Solomon’s diamond mines,” I said.

Umbopa stood near, sunk in one of his heavy silences, and my words struck him awake.

“Yes, Macumazahn,” he put in, in Zulu, “the diamonds are surely there, and you shall have them, since you white men are so fond of toys and money.”

“How dost thou know that, Umbopa?” I asked sharply. I liked neither his knowing nor his way of choosing when to show it.

He laughed softly. “I dreamed it in the night, white men,” and turned his heel as Infadoos had.

Sir Henry watched him go. “Now what is our black friend driving at?” he said. “He knows more than he says. Has he heard anything—anything of my brother?”

“Nothing,” I answered. “He has asked everyone he can get friendly with. They swear no white man ever was seen in this country before.”

“Do you suppose he got here at all?” Good said. “We’ve only reached it by a miracle. Could he do it without the map?”

Sir Henry’s face hardened. “I don’t know,” he said, “but I think I shall find him.”

The sun went down, and the change was as sudden as a knife: no long dying of light, no kindly dusk, but darkness rushing upon the land like something with weight. Then, as quickly, the west glowed and the moon rose—clean, white, and masterful—flooding the plain with silver until the fires of the town pricked out below us like seeds sown in ash. We stood a while and let that quiet brightness wash over us. The world seemed to hold its breath, and the ordinary sounds—the murmur of insects, the far-off lowing of cattle, the faint chorus of marching feet—fell into a kind of order under the moon, as if the night itself were conducting them.

Infadoos broke in at last.

“If my lords are rested, we will go on to Loo. A hut is made ready. The moon is bright; we shall not fall by the way.”

So we went down and in, and in an hour reached the outskirts of the town. Its size, mapped by thousands of fires and the dark circles of hut-walls, seemed without end; Good, who cannot see a large thing without trying to make it small by joking, called it “Unlimited Loo.”

We came to a moat with a drawbridge. Arms rattled; a sentry challenged hoarsely. Infadoos answered with a password I did not catch, and we passed through into the central street, tramping on and on past endless huts, the air thick with smoke, crushed grass, sweat, and the rich, tempting scent of roasting meat. Here and there women moved in and out of the firelight with pots on their heads, and the soft chink of earthenware carried differently than metal—it has a domestic sound, comforting even in a strange place.

At last Infadoos halted at the gate of a small group of huts set round a courtyard powdered white with limestone, and told us these were our “poor” quarters.

Each of us had a hut, and they were better than any we had yet seen. In each stood a bed of tanned skins laid on a mattress of sweet-smelling grass—aromatic as thyme when you crush it between your fingers. Water waited in earthen jars; we washed the dust from ourselves and watched it run away grey. Then young women—handsome, composed, untroubled by the shyness Good expected—brought in food: roasted meat, browned and shining, and mealie cobs served neatly on wooden platters. The mealies smelled of smoke and warm starch, and the fat on the meat carried that deep, animal sweetness that makes a hungry man forget to talk.

We ate and drank. Then, because we had learned caution on this road, we asked that all the beds be moved into one hut. The girls smiled at that, as though at children afraid of shadows, but they obeyed. We slept like dead men.

When we woke, the sun was high. The same attendants stood inside the hut, ready to “make us ready,” as though we were infants to be dressed.

“Make ready, indeed,” growled Good. “When one has only a flannel shirt and a pair of boots, that does not take long. Ask them for my trousers, Quatermain.”

I asked. The answer came back that these sacred relics had already been taken to the king, who would see us in the forenoon.

We sent the young women outside, to their astonishment and, I suspect, their disappointment, and did the best toilet possible. Good shaved again—only the right side of his face—leaving the left, where whiskers had begun to grow, untouched as we had urged. Sir Henry’s yellow hair was nearly to his shoulders; he looked more like some sea-king from an old tale than a modern English gentleman. My own grizzled scrub had reached a full inch, which I consider excessive.

After breakfast and a pipe, Infadoos himself brought word that Twala the king was ready to see us, if we would be pleased to come.

We returned that we would wait until the sun was a little higher—we were weary, and so forth. It is useful with uncivilised people not to hurry; they take politeness for fear. So we sat an hour and prepared such presents as we could manage—Ventvögel’s Winchester rifle and some beads. The rifle and ammunition we meant for the king; the beads for his wives and courtiers. We had given a few to Infadoos and Scragga and seen how their eyes caught fire at the colours, as though the very glass held imprisoned light.

At length we rose, and, guided by Infadoos, went to the audience. Umbopa carried the rifle and beads.

After a few hundred yards we came to an enclosure like our lodging-court, only vast—six or seven acres at least. A fence circled it, and just inside the fence stood a ring of huts: the houses of the king’s wives. Facing the gateway on the far side stood a very large hut, built alone, where the king lived. The open ground between should have been empty, but it was filled—company after company—by warriors, seven or eight thousand of them, standing as still as carved posts. Plumes lifted and fell with breathing; spearpoints caught the light; iron-backed ox-hide shields made a repeating pattern like scales. The sight was grand, but the greater impression was the hush of it—the massed silence of men trained to become part of a single will.

The space in front of the great hut was clear, and before it were placed stools. At Infadoos’s sign we sat—three stools for us, Umbopa behind. Infadoos took his place by the hut door.

Ten minutes passed, perhaps more, in dead quiet. Eight thousand eyes pinned us. It is a hard thing to sit still under such a gaze, but we did it.

At last the hut door opened. A gigantic figure stepped out, a tiger-skin karross flung across his shoulders. Behind him came Scragga, and then something that at first glance looked like a withered monkey wrapped in fur. The giant sat upon a stool; Scragga stood behind; the monkey-thing crawled on all fours into the shade and squatted.

Silence held.

Then the giant slipped off the karross and rose, and we saw the king fully. He was enormous, built like a bull, but his face was the most repulsive I have ever looked upon—thick lips, flat nose, one gleaming black eye, the other a hollow. Cruelty and appetite sat on his features as plainly as grease on a platter. A splendid plume of white ostrich feathers rose from his head. He wore a shirt of shining chain armour; white ox-tail garnishes bound waist and right knee. In his right hand he held a great spear; round his neck a thick torque of gold; on his forehead, bound there, a single enormous uncut diamond shone dully, as though it drank light rather than gave it back.

Still he said nothing. Then he lifted his javelin.

Instantly eight thousand spears rose, and eight thousand throats rang out the royal salute: “Koom!”

Three times it was shouted, each time as deep as thunder, and the very ground seemed to tremble with it. The sound struck the chest and shook the teeth; it was a noise that could rule men by its weight alone.

“Be humble, O people,” piped a thin voice from the shadow—monkey-like, yet sharp as a bird’s cry—“it is the king.”

“It is the king,” boomed the multitude back. “Be humble, O people, it is the king.”

Then again silence, thick enough to choke on.

It was broken by a clatter: a soldier on our left dropped his shield; it struck limestone.

Twala turned his one eye.

“Come hither, thou,” he said.

A fine young man stepped out and stood before him.

“It was thy shield that fell, thou awkward dog. Wilt thou make me a reproach in the eyes of these strangers from the Stars? What hast thou to say?”

The poor fellow went pale beneath his dark skin. “It was by chance, O Calf of the Black Cow,” he murmured.

“Then it is a chance for which thou must pay,” said the king, as calmly as a man ordering a pot to be moved from the fire. “Thou hast made me foolish; prepare for death.”

“I am the king’s ox,” came the low answer.

“Scragga,” roared Twala, “let me see how thou canst use thy spear. Kill me this blundering fool.”

Scragga stepped forward with an ugly grin and lifted his spear. The doomed man covered his eyes and stood still. We sat frozen. Sir Henry’s hands clenched, and I heard his breath go harsh.

Scragga poised—once, twice—and drove the spear home. It stood a foot out behind the man’s back. He flung up his hands and dropped dead. A murmur rolled like wind through dry grass, circled, and died. The body lay there, and for a moment my mind refused to accept that I had just watched a man killed as one kills a chicken for supper.

Sir Henry sprang up and swore, a great oath that would have startled an English drawing-room; then the heavy silence pressed down and he sat again, white with fury.

“The thrust was a good one,” said the king. “Take him away.”

Four men lifted the corpse and carried it off.

“Cover up the blood-stains, cover them up,” piped the thin voice from the shade. “The king’s word is spoken, the king’s doom is done!”

A girl came forward with a jar of powdered lime and scattered it over the red mark until the blood vanished into white dust, as though memory itself could be smothered with a handful of chalk.

Sir Henry shook with anger. I leaned close.

“Sit still, for heaven’s sake,” I whispered. “Our lives depend on it.”

He yielded, though it cost him.

Twala waited until the stain had disappeared. Then he addressed us.

“White people,” he said, “who come hither, whence I know not, and why I know not, greeting.”

“Greeting, Twala, King of the Kukuanas,” I answered.

“White people, whence come ye, and what seek ye?”

“We come from the Stars; ask not how. We come to see this land.”

“Ye journey from far to see a little thing.” His eye shifted to Umbopa. “And that man with you—does he also come from the Stars?”

“Even so,” I said. “There are people of thy colour in the heavens above; but ask not of matters too high for thee, Twala the king.”

“Ye speak with a loud voice, people of the Stars,” he answered, and his tone rasped. “Remember, the Stars are far off, and ye are here. How if I make you as him whom they bore away?”

I laughed aloud, though my stomach tightened.

“O king,” I said, “walk warily over hot stones lest thou burn thy feet; hold the spear by the handle lest thou cut thy hands. Touch but one hair of our heads, and destruction shall come upon thee. Have not these”—I nodded toward Infadoos and Scragga, who was wiping the dead man’s blood from his spear with a bored care—“told thee what manner of men we are? Hast thou seen the like of us?” And I pointed to Good, certain enough that Twala had never seen any creature like Good’s present half-shaved, half-bearded countenance.

“It is true,” said the king, studying him. “I have not.”

“Have they not told thee how we strike with death from afar?” I went on.

“They have told me, but I believe them not. Let me see you kill. Kill me a man among those who stand yonder, and I will believe.” He pointed to the opposite side.

“Nay,” I answered. “We shed no blood of men except in just punishment. But if thou wilt see, bid thy servants drive in an ox through the kraal gates, and before he has run twenty paces I will strike him dead.”

“Nay,” he laughed, “kill me a man.”

“Good, O king,” I said, keeping my voice cool, “so be it. Do thou walk across the open space, and before thy feet reach the gate thou shalt be dead; or, if thou wilt not, send thy son Scragga.”

At that Scragga gave a sort of yelp and bolted into the hut like a whipped cur.

Twala frowned. The joke sat badly with him.

“Let a young ox be driven in,” he ordered.

Two men ran off.

“Now, Sir Henry,” I murmured, “you shoot. I want him to see I am not the only magician.”

Sir Henry took up his heavy “express” and tried to set his face. “I hope I shall make a good shot,” he groaned.

“You must,” I said. “If you miss with the first barrel, give him the second. Sight for a hundred and fifty yards. Wait till the beast turns broadside.”

We waited. The great enclosure held its breath; even the distant sounds seemed to thin out, as if the air itself were listening. Then an ox came running straight for the gate, shoved through, saw the mass of men, and stopped stupidly, turning and bellowing.

“Now,” I whispered.

Up went the rifle.

Bang—then the solid thud of bullet striking flesh. The ox toppled and kicked on its back, shot through the ribs. The semi-hollow bullet had done its work quickly. A sigh rose from the thousands, one great intake of breath like surf.

I turned to the king as though nothing remarkable had happened.

“Have I lied, O king?”

“Nay,” he answered, and there was awe under his roughness. “It is the truth.”

“Listen then, Twala,” I said. “We come in peace, not in war. See”—and I held up the Winchester repeater—“here is a hollow staff that shall enable thee to kill even as we kill. Only I lay this charm upon it: thou shalt kill no man with it. If thou liftest it against a man, it shall kill thee. Stay—I will show thee. Bid a soldier step forty paces and set the shaft of a spear in the ground with the flat blade towards us.”

It was done in moments.

“Now,” I said, “I will break yonder spear.”

I took careful aim and fired. The bullet struck the blade full and shattered it into fragments.

Again that great sigh, half fear and half delight at power.

“Now, Twala, we give this magic tube to thee,” I said, and the lie had to be wrapped carefully, like meat carried through flies. “By-and-by I will show thee how to use it. But beware how thou turnest the magic of the Stars against a man of earth.” I handed him the rifle.

He took it as gingerly as a man takes a live coal and set it at his feet.

As he did so, the monkey-like figure crept out from the hut’s shadow. It came on all fours, then rose, and threw back its fur covering. What stood revealed was a woman’s face—if face it could be called—so shrunken it seemed no bigger than a child’s, yet seamed all over with deep yellow wrinkles like sun-cracked mud. A slit of a mouth lay sunk beneath them; the chin curved outward to a point; there was hardly a nose. The skull projected, bare and parchment-coloured, and under snow-white brows two large black eyes burned with life, bright as coals in a hearth. The scalp moved and contracted like a cobra’s hood.

She stood a moment, and a chill ran through me that had nothing to do with air. Then she stretched out a claw-like hand, nails nearly an inch long, laid it on Twala’s shoulder, and began to speak in a thin, piercing voice that seemed to cut straight through flesh and lodge in the bone.

“Listen, O king! Listen, O warriors! Listen, O mountains and plains and rivers, home of the Kukuana race! Listen, O skies and sun, O rain and storm and mist! Listen, O men and women, O youths and maidens, and O ye babes unborn! Listen, all things that live and must die! Listen, all dead things that shall live again—again to die! Listen, the spirit of life is in me and I prophesy. I prophesy! I prophesy!”

Her words faded into a faint wail. Dread took the air, and even the warriors seemed to tighten, as though a wind had shifted to blow from graves.

“Blood! blood! blood!” she cried. “Rivers of blood—blood everywhere. I see it, I smell it, I taste it—it is salt! It runs red upon the ground; it rains down from the skies.

“Footsteps! footsteps! footsteps! The tread of the white man coming from afar. It shakes the earth; the earth trembles before her master.

“Blood is good—the red blood is bright; there is no smell like the smell of new-shed blood. The lions shall lap it and roar, the vultures shall wash their wings in it and shriek with joy.

“I am old! I am old! I have seen much blood—ha, ha!—but I shall see more ere I die, and be merry. How old am I, think ye? Your fathers knew me, and their fathers knew me, and their fathers’ fathers’ fathers. I have seen the white man and know his desires. I am old, but the mountains are older than I. Who made the great road, tell me? Who wrote the pictures on the rocks, tell me? Who reared up the three Silent Ones yonder, that gaze across the pit, tell me?” She pointed, and though her arm was thin as a stick, the gesture seemed to drag the eye toward the Three Witches.

“Ye know not, but I know. It was a white people who were before ye are, who shall be when ye are not—who shall eat you up and destroy you. Yea! yea! yea!

“And what came they for, the White Ones, the Terrible Ones, skilled in magic and all learning, strong, unswerving? What is that bright stone upon thy forehead, O king? Whose hands made the iron garments upon thy breast, O king? Ye know not, but I know—I the Old One, I the Wise One, I the Isanusi, the witch doctress!”

Then she turned that vulture-head upon us.

“What seek ye, white men of the Stars—ah, yes, of the Stars? Do ye seek a lost one? Ye shall not find him here. He is not here. Never for ages upon ages has a white foot pressed this land—never, except once, and I remember that he left it but to die. Ye come for bright stones; I know it—I know it. Ye shall find them when the blood is dry; but shall ye return whence ye came, or shall ye stop with me? Ha! ha! ha!

“And thou—thou with the dark skin and the proud bearing,” and she stabbed a finger at Umbopa, “who art thou, and what seekest thou? Not stones that shine, not yellow metal that gleams; these thou leavest to ‘white men from the Stars.’ Methinks I know thee; methinks I can smell the smell of the blood in thy heart. Strip off the girdle—”

Her face twisted; her body convulsed, and she fell foaming, struck down by an epileptic fit. They carried her into the hut.

Twala rose trembling and waved his hand. At once the regiments began to file away, and in ten minutes—save for ourselves, the king, and a few attendants—the great space lay empty, whitened with limestone, as though nothing had happened there at all.

“White people,” he said, and his voice had lost its swagger, “it passes in my mind to kill you. Gagool has spoken strange words. What say ye?”

When we gained our hut again I beckoned Infadoos inside, for the air out of doors was thick with the breath of many fires and the restless shuffle of feet, and I wanted the old captain where words could be weighed.

“Now, Infadoos,” I said, “we would speak with thee.”

“Let my lords say on,” he answered, standing very straight, yet with a look as though he listened not only with his ears but with his skin.

“It seems to us,” I went on, “that Twala the king is a cruel man.”

“It is so, my lords.” His voice dropped, and through the thatch came the far-off pulse of stamping, like a drum laid on the earth. “Alas! the land cries out because of his cruelties. To-night ye shall see. It is the great witch-hunt, and many will be smelt out as wizards and slain. No man’s life is safe. If the king covets a man’s cattle, or a man’s wife, or if he fears a man that he should excite rebellion, then Gagool—she whom ye saw—or some of the witch-finding women whom she has taught, will smell that man out as a wizard, and he will be killed. Many must die before the moon grows pale to-night. It is ever so.”

As he spoke, the village sounds seemed to press nearer: the dry clack of spears striking shield rims, the scrape of sandals, the occasional sharp call—each noise carrying clean through the cool evening like a knife through soft fruit. There was something in that ordered harshness that set the teeth on edge; it was no wholesome music of field and home, but a drilled sound, as if men had been trained to drown their own thoughts.

“Perhaps I too shall be killed,” Infadoos added, not dramatically, but as a farmer might remark that hail could come before harvest. “As yet I have been spared because I am skilled in war, and am beloved by the soldiers; but I know not how long I have to live. The land groans at the cruelties of Twala the king; it is wearied of him and his red ways.”

“Then why is it,” I asked, “that the people do not cast him down?”

“Nay, my lords, he is the king; and if he were killed, Scragga would reign in his place, and the heart of Scragga is blacker than the heart of Twala his father. If Scragga were king his yoke upon our neck would be heavier than the yoke of Twala. If Imotu had never been slain, or if Ignosi his son had lived, it might have been otherwise; but they are both dead.”

“How knowest thou that Ignosi is dead?” said a voice behind us.

We turned, startled. Umbopa stood in the doorway. The firelight caught the planes of his face and the line of his shoulders; he looked as calm as a man who has been long carrying a secret and at last feels its weight shift.

“What meanest thou, boy?” Infadoos demanded. “Who told thee to speak?”

“Listen, Infadoos,” Umbopa answered, and his voice had the measured steadiness of a story told in winter to keep memory from freezing. “Years ago the king Imotu was killed in this country and his wife fled with the boy Ignosi. Is it not so?”

“It is so.”

“It was said that the woman and her son died upon the mountains. Is it not so?”

“It is even so.”

“Well, it came to pass that the mother and the boy Ignosi did not die. They crossed the mountains and were led by a tribe of wandering desert men across the sands beyond, till at last they came to water and grass and trees again.”

“How knowest thou this?” Infadoos broke in, as though the tale rubbed him raw where old scars lay.

“Listen. They travelled on and on, many months’ journey, till they reached a land where a people called the Amazulu, who also are of the Kukuana stock, live by war, and with them they tarried many years, till at length the mother died. Then the son Ignosi became a wanderer again, and journeyed into a land of wonders, where white people live, and for many more years he learned the wisdom of the white people.”

“It is a pretty story,” Infadoos said, but there was hunger in his incredulity.

“For years he lived there working as a servant and a soldier,” Umbopa went on, “but holding in his heart all that his mother had told him of his own place, and casting about in his mind to find how he might journey thither to see his people and his father’s house before he died. For long years he lived and waited, and at last the time came—as it ever comes to him who can wait for it—and he met some white men who would seek this unknown land, and joined himself to them. The white men started and travelled on and on, seeking for one who is lost. They crossed the burning desert, they crossed the snow-clad mountains, and at last reached the land of the Kukuanas, and there they found thee, O Infadoos.”

“Surely thou art mad to talk thus,” Infadoos said, but his hands had begun to tremble.

“Thou thinkest so; see, I will show thee, O my uncle.”

With one swift motion Umbopa slipped off his girdle and stood naked before us, the fire making copper of his skin. He pointed to the blue tattoo about his middle: a great snake biting its own tail.

“Look,” he said. “What is this?”

Infadoos stared as though the air had suddenly turned to water and he could not breathe.

“Koom! Koom!” he burst out, and fell on his knees. “It is my brother’s son; it is the king.”

“Did I not tell thee so, my uncle?” Umbopa—Ignosi—said gently. “Rise; I am not yet the king, but with thy help, and with the help of these brave white men, who are my friends, I shall be. Yet the old witch Gagool was right: the land shall run with blood first, and hers shall run with it, if she has any and can die, for she killed my father with her words, and drove my mother forth.

“And now, Infadoos, choose thou. Wilt thou put thy hands between my hands and be my man? Wilt thou share the dangers that lie before me, and help me to overthrow this tyrant and murderer, or wilt thou not? Choose thou.”

The old soldier bowed his head, thinking hard, as men do who have lived long enough to have seen promises rot and names change their taste. Then he rose, stepped forward, and knelt again—this time not out of shock but out of allegiance. He took Ignosi’s hand between his own.

“Ignosi, rightful king of the Kukuanas, I put my hand between thy hands, and am thy man till death. When thou wast a babe I dandled thee upon my knees; now shall my old arm strike for thee and freedom.”

“It is well, Infadoos,” Ignosi answered. “If I conquer, thou shalt be the greatest man in the kingdom after its king. If I fail, thou canst only die, and death is not far off from thee. Rise, my uncle.

“And ye, white men, will ye help me? What have I to offer you? The white stones! If I conquer and can find them, ye shall have as many as ye can carry hence. Will that suffice you?”

I translated.

“Tell him,” said Sir Henry, “that he mistakes an Englishman. Wealth is good, and if it comes in our way we will take it; but a gentleman does not sell himself for wealth. Still, speaking for myself, I say this: I have always liked Umbopa, and so far as lies in me I will stand by him in this business. It will be very pleasant to me to try to square matters with that cruel devil Twala.”

“What do you say, Good, and you, Quatermain?” he added.

“Well,” said Good, “to adopt the language of hyperbole, in which all these people seem to indulge, you can tell him that a row is surely good, and warms the cockles of the heart, and that so far as I am concerned I’m his boy. My only stipulation is that he allows me to wear trousers.”

I gave the sense of this to Ignosi, who smiled a little, though his eyes were set far away.

“And what sayest thou, Macumazahn,” he asked me, “art thou also with me, old hunter, cleverer than a wounded buffalo?”

I scratched my head, for it is no light thing to set your hand to another man’s storm.

“Ignosi,” I said, “I don’t like revolutions. I am a man of peace and a bit of a coward”—at which he smiled more openly—“but I stick up for my friends. You have stuck to us and played the part of a man, and I will stick by you. Only, mind you, I am a trader, and have to make my living, so I accept your offer about those diamonds in case we should ever be in a position to avail ourselves of it. Another thing: we came to look for Incubu’s lost brother. You must help us to find him.”

“That I will do,” Ignosi said. Then his face hardened again as he turned to Infadoos. “By the sign of the snake about my middle, tell me the truth. Has any white man to thy knowledge set his foot within the land?”

“None, O Ignosi.”

“If any white man had been seen or heard of, wouldst thou have known?”

“I should certainly have known.”

“Thou hearest, Incubu,” Ignosi said to Sir Henry. “He has not been here.”

Sir Henry’s mouth worked; his eyes went away from us to the packed darkness under the eaves.

“Well, well,” he said at last, with a sigh that seemed to empty him. “There it is. I suppose that he never got so far. Poor fellow, poor fellow! So it has all been for nothing. God’s will be done.”

I broke in, for the grief in his voice was worse than any loud crying. “Now for business. It is very well to be a king by right divine, Ignosi, but how dost thou propose to become a king indeed?”

“Nay, I know not,” he answered. “Infadoos, hast thou a plan?”

“Ignosi, Son of the Lightning,” said Infadoos, “to-night is the great dance and witch-hunt. Many shall be smelt out and perish, and in the hearts of many others there will be grief and anguish and fury against the king Twala. When the dance is over, then I will speak to some of the great chiefs, who in turn—if I can win them—will speak to their regiments. I shall speak softly at first, and bring them to see that thou art indeed the king, and I think that by to-morrow’s light thou shalt have twenty thousand spears at thy command. And now I must go and think, and hear, and make ready. After the dance is done, if I am yet alive, and we are all alive, I will meet thee here, and we can talk. At the best there must be war.”

At that very moment a cry outside cut across our plotting: messengers from the king.

We went to the doorway and ordered them in. Three men entered, each bearing a shining shirt of chain armour, and a magnificent battle-axe. The links of the mail caught the firelight as fish-scales do when you turn them in your palm; there was a cold, clean smell to the metal, like stone after rain.

“The gifts of my lord the king to the white men from the Stars!” said the herald.

“We thank the king,” I answered. “Withdraw.”

When they had gone we handled the armour with a sort of unwilling admiration. The chain-work was so fine and close that a whole coat lay in a heavy, supple heap, scarcely too big to cover with both hands.

“Do you make these things in this country, Infadoos?” I asked.

“Nay, my lord. They came down to us from our forefathers. We know not who made them, and there are but few left. None but those of royal blood may be clad in them. They are magic coats through which no spear can pass, and those who wear them are well-nigh safe in the battle. The king is well pleased, or much afraid, or he would not have sent these garments of steel. Clothe yourselves in them to-night, my lords.”

We spent what remained of the day as quietly as men can who feel a whole country shifting beneath them. Outside, the sounds of preparation thickened: the repeated metallic kiss of spear to shield, the short barking orders, the measured trotting of runners. The very air seemed tuned to harsh instruments, as if the village had forgotten the softer noises of cattle and pestles and women’s talk.

At last the sun went down. A thousand watch-fires bloomed and steadied into red eyes across the dark, and the full moon rose, washing everything in pale silver and making the smoke drift like ghost-cloth.

Infadoos came for us clad in war-dress, with twenty men to escort us. We had already put on the chain shirts under our ordinary clothing. To our surprise they were neither very heavy nor very uncomfortable. They hung loose on Good and on me, but on Sir Henry they fitted as though made for him. We strapped our revolvers about our waists, took up the battle-axes, and went out.

The great kraal was crammed—some twenty thousand men arranged in regiments around the open space. Between each company ran narrow paths, left clear as channels, for the witch-finders to pass up and down. The order of it was terrifying: so many bodies held in such exact shape, like a field planted with spears instead of grain. In the moonlight the raised points shimmered in ranks; the shields made broad patterns of colour; the plumes trembled. Yet over it all lay a silence so deep it felt unnatural, like a forest where all birds have flown.

“Surely,” I murmured to Infadoos, “the whole army is here?”

“Nay, Macumazahn,” he answered. “But a third of it. One third is present at this dance each year, another third is mustered outside in case there should be trouble when the killing begins, ten thousand more garrison the outposts round Loo, and the rest watch at the kraals in the country. Thou seest it is a great people.”

“They are very silent,” said Good, and his attempt at lightness fell flat.

“What says Bougwan?” asked Infadoos.

I translated.

“Those over whom the shadow of Death is hovering are silent,” Infadoos returned, and there was no mistaking the relish of grim knowledge in his tone.

“Will many be killed?” I asked, though I knew the answer by the emptiness in my own stomach.

“Very many.”

“It seems,” I said to the others, “that we are going to assist at a gladiatorial show arranged regardless of expense.”

Sir Henry shivered. Good muttered that he wished we could slip away like smoke.

“Tell me,” I asked Infadoos, “are we in danger?”

“I know not, my lords. I trust not; but do not seem afraid. If ye live through the night all may go well with you. The soldiers murmur against the king.”

We advanced toward the centre where stools had been set. From the royal hut another small party approached.

“It is the king Twala,” Infadoos said, “Scragga his son, and Gagool the old; and see, with them are those who slay.”

He pointed to a dozen great, savage-looking men—executioners—spears in one hand, heavy kerries in the other. Their faces shone with sweat and anticipation.

Twala seated himself on the middle stool. Gagool crouched at his feet, a bent bundle of age and malice, her skull-like head thrust forward as though scenting the air. The others stood behind.

“Greeting, white lords,” Twala cried when we came up. “Be seated. Waste not precious time—the night is all too short for the deeds that must be done. Ye come in a good hour, and shall see a glorious show. Look round, white lords; look round.” His single eye rolled from regiment to regiment. “Can the Stars show you such a sight as this? See how they shake in their wickedness, all those who have evil in their hearts and fear the judgment of ‘Heaven above.’”

“Begin! begin!” piped Gagool, her voice thin as a reed whistle. “The hyænas are hungry, they howl for food. Begin! begin!”

A stillness fell that was not peace but a held breath before a blow.

Twala lifted his spear. Instantly twenty thousand feet rose and stamped together, three times, so that the earth itself seemed to answer with a dull groan. From far round the circle a lone voice began a wailing chant.

“What is the lot of man born of woman?”

Back came the answer from every throat in that vast ring:

“Death!”

Company after company took up the song. The words shifted and slid; the same mouths that had given “Death” like a judgment now shaped sounds that seemed—by turns—love, pride, battle, lament. It was as though the whole life of the people was being sung out in one great coil, returning always to the same cold centre. It ended in a sudden wail that ran along the ring and away into the night like an animal driven under the knife.

Again silence.

Then, at another motion of the king’s hand, we heard the patter of running feet. Out of the masses came strange figures—women, most of them old, with white hair streaming and ornamented with small fish bladders. Their faces were striped in yellow and white; snake-skins hung down their backs; circlets of human bones rattled at their waists with each step. Each carried a small forked wand.

Ten of them halted before us. One pointed her wand at Gagool and cried:

“Mother, old mother, we are here.”

“Good! good! good!” the hag answered. “Are your eyes keen, Isanusis, ye seers in dark places?”

“Mother, they are keen.”

“Good! good! good! Are your ears open, Isanusis, ye who hear words that come not from the tongue?”

“Mother, they are open.”

“Good! good! good! Are your senses awake—can ye smell blood, can ye purge the land of the wicked ones who compass evil against the king and against their neighbours? Are ye ready to do the justice of ‘Heaven above,’ ye whom I have taught, who have eaten of the bread of my wisdom, and drunk of the water of my magic?”

“Mother, we can.”

“Then go!” Gagool shrieked. “Tarry not, ye vultures; see, the slayers make sharp their spears; the white men from afar are hungry to see. Go!”

With a wild yell they scattered, the bones at their waists clattering like dry pods in a gourd rattle. We fixed our eyes on the nearest. She halted before a company and began to dance, faster and faster, turning like a top, shrieking:

“I smell him, the evil-doer!”
“He is near, he who poisoned his mother!”
“I hear the thoughts of him who thought evil of the king!”

She whipped herself into frenzy till foam flecked her mouth and her eyes looked ready to burst. Then she froze, stiff as a hunting dog, and began to creep forward, wand outstretched.

The warriors before her—men who had stood unmoving like carved posts—seemed to shrink. It was not fear of death alone; it was fear of being named, of having your life rewritten by another’s tongue. And there is a kind of power in that: to speak a word that cannot be answered in the same language.

Suddenly she sprang and touched a tall man.

At once two comrades seized him by the arms and marched him toward the king. He did not struggle; his limbs dragged as if the spirit had already been knocked loose, and his spear fell from fingers that had gone limp.

Two executioners came forward and looked to Twala.

“Kill,” said the king.

“Kill,” squeaked Gagool.

“Kill,” echoed Scragga, with a hollow chuckle.

Before the syllable had died, the spear was driven home and the heavy club followed. The body was hauled aside and laid out.

“One,” said Twala, counting as calmly as a man tallying cattle.

Another was brought—this one wearing a leopard-skin cloak that told of rank. Again the word. Again the fall.

“Two.”

So it went on—one after another—till near a hundred corpses lay in grim rows behind us, their dark blood turning almost black in the moonlight. I have heard much of Roman shows and Spanish bull-fights, but those at least were called amusement; here there was no joy, only a great public terror, and a tyrant feeding on it. The circle of men watched as people watch a fire that may leap to their own roofs.

Once we rose and tried to protest, but Twala checked us with a glare.

“Let the law take its course, white men. These dogs are magicians and evil-doers; it is well that they should die.”

About half-past ten there came a pause. The witch-finders gathered, panting and wiped-out, and for a moment we hoped the madness had spent itself. But then Gagool herself rose from her crouch. She tottered into the open space, leaning on a stick; and it was a sight to see that bent, vulture-headed thing draw strength as she moved, until she was darting about, crooked as a hook and yet swift.

To and fro she went, muttering and chanting, until she dashed at a tall man in front of a regiment and touched him. A groan rose from that regiment—deep, collective, helpless—yet two officers stepped out, seized him, and dragged him forward all the same. We learned later he was a rich and important man, cousin to the king.

He died like the rest.

“One hundred and three,” counted Twala.

Then Gagool began to drift closer, circling, circling—like a carrion bird that has chosen the next carcass but enjoys the tightening spiral. The whole assembly watched her with the strained attention of men listening for a twig-snap in the dark.

“Hang me if I don’t believe she is going to try her games on us,” Good whispered, his face grey.

“Nonsense,” Sir Henry muttered, though his jaw was set.

My own heart sank like a stone in cold water. Behind us those rows of bodies lay like a harvest of evil, and I thought how quickly any of us might be added—one touch, one word, and the knives were out.

Nearer she came, eyes gleaming. Then she stopped and pointed.

For one beat of time none of us breathed. Then she rushed in and laid her hand on Umbopa—Ignosi—on the shoulder.

“I smell him out!” she shrieked. “Kill him, kill him, he is full of evil; kill him, the stranger, before blood flows from him. Slay him, O king!”

A hush—so complete it seemed the very fires outside had died.

I sprang up before Twala could speak.

“O king,” I cried, “this man is the servant of thy guests, he is their dog. Whosoever sheds the blood of our dog sheds our blood. By the sacred law of hospitality I claim protection for him.”

“Gagool, mother of the witch-finders, has smelt him out,” Twala answered sullenly. “He must die, white men.”

“Nay, he shall not die,” I said. “He who tries to touch him shall die indeed.”

Twala roared to the executioners. They moved forward—then hesitated, for even their courage had edges, and those edges met the unknown.

Ignosi gripped his spear and lifted it, ready to sell himself dearly.

“Stand back, ye dogs!” I shouted. “If ye would see to-morrow’s light, touch one hair of his head and your king dies.” I covered Twala with my revolver. Sir Henry and Good drew too—Sir Henry aimed at the foremost executioner; Good, cool as a man sighting a buck, levelled at Gagool.

Twala flinched when my barrel steadied on his chest.

“Well,” I said, “what is it to be, Twala?”

He held my eyes a moment—his one eye full of hate and calculation—then spat out his decision as though it tasted bitter.

“Put away your magic tubes,” he said. “Ye have adjured me in the name of hospitality, and for that reason, but not from fear of what ye can do, I spare him. Go in peace.”

“It is well,” I answered, making myself sound unconcerned though my hands were damp. “We are weary of slaughter, and would sleep. Is the dance ended?”

“It is ended,” Twala said, sulkier than before. He lifted his spear and gestured toward the corpses. “Let these dead dogs be flung out to the hyænas and the vultures.”

Instantly the regiments began to file away through the gateway in perfect silence, the only sound that remained the dull dragging as a fatigue party started on the work of hauling the bodies out into the night.

For a long while—two hours, by my reckoning—we sat as men sit after a feast has turned to poison in the belly: not hungry, not satisfied, only sick with the memory of what we had witnessed. Outside, the kraal breathed in its own fashion—an occasional cough of a sleeping sentry, the far-off rasp of insects, the soft, patient scuff of bare feet in dust that had known too much blood. Even that small, natural music seemed muffled, as though the night itself had put a hand over its mouth.

At length, when the darkness had begun to thin towards dawn, we heard footsteps—measured, unhurried, as if the owner of them wished the very ground to understand he was not sneaking. A challenge rose from the gate; an answer came back, low enough that we could not catch the words, but clear enough in confidence that the sentry let the party through. A moment later Infadoos entered the hut, and behind him came six chiefs, tall and heavy with dignity, their ornaments catching the lamplight like small, calm sparks.

“My lords,” Infadoos said, “I have come according to my word. My lords, and Ignosi, rightful king of the Kukuanas, I have brought these men, great among us. Each commands three thousand soldiers who live to do his bidding under the king’s. I have told them what my eyes have seen and my ears have heard. Now let them behold the sacred snake about thee, and hear thy story, Ignosi, that they may say whether they will make cause with thee against Twala.”

Ignosi answered as he had answered before—without flourish, with the steady simplicity of a man who carries his truth on his skin. He loosened his girdle and showed them the tattooed coil, the old sign, the snake that marked him for what he claimed to be. One by one the chiefs came near. In the dim oil-lamp’s wavering glow they bent, squinting and frowning, fingertips hovering as if touch itself might be sacrilege. Not one spoke; each merely passed on, grave as judges tasting a broth they distrust.

Ignosi drew up his garment again and told his tale once more—of birth and banishment, of blood and stolen right, of the king who ruled by fear and the witch-doctress who ruled by something colder than fear. His voice did not shake. It had the tone of a man reciting the names of seed-grains handed down, each syllable part of inheritance, not ornament.

When he had done, Infadoos stepped forward, his hands spread like a man offering bread.

“Ye have heard, chiefs. What say ye? Will ye stand by this man and help him to his father’s throne, or will ye not? The land cries out against Twala, and the blood of the people flows like water in spring. Ye have seen to-night. Two other chiefs there were with whom I meant to speak—and where are they now? The hyænas howl over their corpses. Soon shall ye be as they are if ye strike not. Choose then, my brothers.”

The eldest of the six—a short, thick-set warrior with white hair and a face like toughened hide—stepped out. His voice had the rasp of age, yet it carried.

“Thy words are true, Infadoos; the land cries out. My own brother is among those dead to-night. But this is a great matter and hard to believe. How know we that we lift our spears not for a thief and a liar? None can see the end of this road. Blood will flow in rivers before the deed is done. Many will still cleave to the king, for men worship the sun that shines, not the one that has not yet risen. These white men from the Stars—their magic is great, and Ignosi is under the cover of their wing. If he be indeed rightful king, let them give us a sign, and let the people have a sign, that all may see. So shall men cleave to us, knowing of a truth the white man’s magic is with them.”

“Ye have the sign of the snake,” I said.

“My lord, it is not enough. The snake may have been placed there since the man’s childhood. Show us a sign and it will suffice. But we will not move without a sign.”

The others murmured assent—firm, cautious, the sound of men who have learned what promises cost. I turned to Sir Henry and Good and explained in a whisper. Good’s eyes, which had been dull with fatigue, suddenly sharpened as though he had smelt something on the wind.

“Give us a moment,” he said quickly. “Ask them to step outside.”

I did so, and the chiefs withdrew with Infadoos. The instant the doorway-hide fell back into place, Good crossed to his little medicine-box, unlocked it, and drew out his notebook. Between its fly-leaves was an almanack, thin and much-thumbed.

“Now look here,” he said, jabbing at the page with a forefinger. “Isn’t tomorrow the 4th of June?”

We had marked the days carefully enough in our misery, and could answer that it was.

“Good. Then we’ve got it: ‘4 June, total eclipse of the moon commences at 8.15 Greenwich time, visible in Teneriffe—South Africa, &c.’ There’s your sign. Tell them we will darken the moon tomorrow night.”

It was one of those ideas that arrive like a match struck in a draught—sudden light, and with it the smell of danger. A sign such as they wanted, a wonder they could see, and yet a wonder that cost us nothing but nerve—if, and only if, the heavens kept to the timetable of Good’s little book.

The weak spot lay plain as a bone in broth: if the almanack was wrong, if cloud covered the sky, if our count of days had slipped—then our “magic” would curdle in the mouths of the Kukuanas, and with it Ignosi’s hope, and our own lives.

“Suppose it’s wrong,” Sir Henry said, watching Good work figures on a blank page as though summoning the moon by arithmetic.

“I see no reason to suppose anything of the sort,” Good answered briskly. “Eclipses always come up to time—that’s my experience. And it says visible in South Africa. I’ve done the reckonings as well as I can without knowing our exact position. The eclipse should begin here about ten tomorrow night and last until half-past twelve. For an hour and a half there should be almost total darkness.”

“Well,” Sir Henry said, “we’d better risk it.”

I agreed, though doubt gnawed at me like hunger. The sky is the last place a man wants to stake his reputation; it has a way of reminding you whose rules you do not write. Still, there was no other course that offered such a clean, public marvel. I sent Umbopa—Ignosi—to call the chiefs back.

They returned, solemn as before. Their presence filled the hut with a different air, like a kitchen when elders arrive to judge the pot—silent, expectant, nothing wasted.

“Great men of the Kukuanas,” I said, “and thou, Infadoos, listen. We love not to show our powers, for to do so is to interfere with the course of nature and to plunge the world into fear and confusion. But since this matter is great, and as we are angered against the king because of the slaughter we have seen, and because of the act of the Isanusi Gagool who would have put our friend Ignosi to death, we will break a rule and give such a sign as all men may see.”

I led them to the doorway and pointed to the moon, low and red as a coal banked in ash.

“What see ye there?”

“We see the sinking moon,” said the spokesman.

“It is so. Now tell me: can any mortal man put out that moon before her hour of setting, and bring the curtain of black night down upon the land?”

The chief laughed softly, the way an elder laughs at a child who asks if porridge can be boiled in cold water. “No, my lord. The moon is stronger than man who looks on her, nor can she vary in her courses.”

“Ye say so. Yet I tell you that tomorrow night, about two hours before midnight, we will cause the moon to be eaten up for a space of an hour and half an hour. Deep darkness shall cover the earth, and it shall be a sign that Ignosi is indeed king of the Kukuanas. If we do this thing, will ye be satisfied?”

“Yea, my lords,” the old chief answered, smiling now, and the smile ran along the faces of the others like a flame touching dry grass. “If ye do this thing, we will be satisfied indeed.”

“It shall be done,” I said. “We three—Incubu, Bougwan, and Macumazahn—have said it, and it shall be done. Dost thou hear, Infadoos?”

“I hear, my lord,” Infadoos replied, awe mixed with practical urgency. “But it is a wonderful thing ye promise—to put out the moon, the mother of the world, when she is full.”

“Yet shall we do it.”

“It is well. To-day, two hours after sunset, Twala will send for my lords to witness the girls dance. One hour after the dance begins the girl whom Twala thinks the fairest shall be killed by Scragga, the king’s son, as a sacrifice to the Silent Ones who sit and keep watch by the mountains yonder.” He pointed towards the three strange peaks where Solomon’s road was said to end, the stone watchers that seemed to drink moonlight without returning any. “Then let my lords darken the moon and save the maiden’s life, and the people will believe indeed.”

“Ay,” said the old chief, still smiling, “the people will believe indeed.”

“Two miles from Loo,” Infadoos went on, “there is a hill curved like a new moon, a stronghold, where my regiment and three other regiments which these chiefs command are stationed. This morning we will make a plan whereby two or three other regiments may be moved there also. Then, if in truth my lords can darken the moon, in the darkness I will take my lords by the hand and lead them out of Loo to that place, where ye shall be safe. Thence we can make war upon Twala.”

“It is good,” I said. “Now leave us to sleep awhile and to make ready our magic.”

Infadoos saluted and departed with the chiefs, their footfalls sliding back into the night until the kraal seemed empty again, though we knew it was packed with lives and listening.

When they were gone, Ignosi looked at us with the calm hunger of a man who has lived too long on promises and now sees meat set before him.

“My friends,” he said, “can ye do this wonderful thing, or were ye speaking empty words to the captains?”

“We believe we can do it,” I answered. “Umbopa—Ignosi, I mean.”

“It is strange,” he said slowly. “Had ye not been Englishmen, I would not have believed it. But I have learned that English ‘gentlemen’ tell no lies. If we live through this matter, be sure I will repay you.”

“Ignosi,” Sir Henry said, “promise me one thing.”

“I will promise, Incubu, even before I hear it,” Ignosi answered, and there was warmth in his smile, like the first heat from a fire that has been coaxed to life. “What is it?”

“That if ever you come to be king of this people, you will do away with the smelling-out of wizards such as we saw last night; and that the killing of men without trial shall no longer take place in the land.”

Ignosi stood silent for a moment after I had translated. The lamp hissed softly; outside, some early bird tried a note and stopped, as though reconsidering whether the world was safe.

“The ways of black people are not as the ways of white men, Incubu,” he said at last. “Nor do we value life so highly. Yet I will promise. If it be in my power to hold them back, the witch-finders shall hunt no more, nor shall any man die the death without trial or judgment.”

“That’s a bargain,” Sir Henry said. “Now let us get some rest.”

We slept like men dropped into darkness—sleep not as comfort, but as collapse. Ignosi woke us about eleven. We washed, ate heartily, and for a little while walked outside, examining the Kukuana huts and watching the women move about their work. They went with a sure, economical grace: water carried, grain handled, fire tended. There is a kind of quiet civilisation in such routines, and I could not help thinking how the cruelty of Twala and Gagool sat upon this people like smoke on good food—spoiling what might have been wholesome.

“I hope that eclipse will come off,” Sir Henry said at length.

“If it does not,” I answered, “it will soon be all up with us; for as sure as we are living men some of those chiefs will tell the whole story to the king. Then there will be another sort of eclipse—one I am certain we shall not like.”

We returned to the hut, ate dinner, and endured the long remainder of the day, receiving visits of ceremony and curiosity. Faces peered in, voices offered compliments, questions came like the tapping of spoons on bowls—light, polite, insistent. All the while the thought of the moon sat in my mind like a stone in a pouch.

At last the sun set. For a couple of hours we had such quiet as anxious men can manage. Then, about half-past eight, a messenger came from Twala to bid us to the great annual dance of girls.

We hastily put on the chain-shirts the king had sent us—cold, metallic things, each link like a little promise of survival—and took our rifles and ammunition, keeping them as close as a cook keeps salt when strangers are near the pot. Then we walked out boldly enough, though inside me fear moved and shifted, restless as a trapped animal.

The great space before the king’s kraal looked altogether different from the previous night. Where there had been grim, serried ranks of warriors, there were now companies of girls—lightly clothed, crowned with wreaths of flowers, each holding a palm leaf in one hand and a white arum lily in the other. The air was sweet with bruised greenery and blossoms warming under bodies and lamp-smoke; beneath it, faint but unmistakable, lay the tang of human closeness and trampled earth. Their singing rose and fell in clean lines that seemed to rinse the place of last night’s screams, though no song can truly wash blood from memory.

In the centre sat Twala the king, with old Gagool crouched at his feet like a poison root. Infadoos was there, too, and Scragga, and twelve guards. About a score of chiefs stood nearby, among them most of those who had visited us in the night.

Twala greeted us with showy cordiality, though his single eye fixed itself on Ignosi with a hatred so sharp it seemed to cut the air.

“Welcome, white men from the Stars,” he said. “This is another sight than that which your eyes gazed on by the light of last night’s moon, but it is not so good a sight. Girls are pleasant, and were it not for such as these, we should none of us be here this day; but men are better. Kisses and tender words are sweet, but the sound of clashing spears and the smell of men’s blood are sweeter far! Would ye have wives from among our people, white men? Choose the fairest, and ye shall have them—as many as ye will.”

Good’s ears pricked at that like a dog’s at the rattle of a pan, and I saw the trouble beginning in his eyes. I answered quickly, as an older hand answers a boy reaching too near the fire.

“Thanks to thee, O king, but we white men wed only with white women like ourselves. Your maidens are fair, but they are not for us.”

Twala laughed. “It is well. In our land there is a proverb: ‘Women’s eyes are always bright, whatever the colour,’ and another: ‘Love her who is present, for she who is absent is false.’ But perhaps these things are not so in the Stars. In a land where men are white all things are possible. So be it. The girls will not go begging. Welcome again; and welcome, too, thou black one. If Gagool had won her way, thou wouldst have been stiff and cold by now. It is lucky for thee that thou too camest from the Stars; ha! ha!”

“I can kill thee before thou killest me, O king,” Ignosi answered calmly, “and thou shalt be stiff before my limbs cease to bend.”

Twala started, anger flaring. “Thou speakest boldly, boy. Presume not too far.”

“He may well be bold in whose lips are truth,” Ignosi said, his voice even. “The truth is a sharp spear which flies home and misses not. It is a message from the Stars, O king.”

Twala scowled, his one eye gleaming, but he said no more.

“Let the dance begin,” he cried.

The girls sprang forward in companies, singing a sweet song and waving palms and lilies. They moved in patterns—whirling, meeting, feinting in mimic battle, retreating and advancing in ordered confusion that was delightful to see. In the soft moonlight they seemed almost unreal, like white smoke drifting across dark ground. Their feet slapped and whispered on the packed earth; their voices braided together, and for a time the place belonged to sound rather than to cruelty.

At last they paused, and a beautiful young woman stepped out and danced alone, pirouetting with a grace and vigour that would have shamed most trained performers. When she retired, another took her place, then another and another, but none equalled the first.

When the chosen girls had danced, Twala lifted his hand.

“Which deem ye the fairest, white men?” he asked.

“The first,” I said, unthinking.

Instantly I regretted it, remembering Infadoos’s warning. The word had left my mouth like a seed thrown—and now I saw what crop it would bring.

“Then is my mind as your minds, and my eyes as your eyes,” Twala said. “She is the fairest! And a sorry thing for her, for she must die!”

“Ay, must die!” piped Gagool, her quick eyes flashing towards the girl.

The poor creature stood some ten yards off, still ignorant, nervously picking a flower from her wreath to pieces—petal by petal, as a child crumbles bread not knowing famine is coming.

“Why, O king?” I asked, forcing my voice to steadiness. “The girl has danced well and pleased us; she is fair too. It would be hard to reward her with death.”

“It is our custom,” Twala said, and pointed towards the peaks, black against the sky. “The figures who sit in stone yonder must have their due. If I fail to put the fairest girl to death to-day, misfortune will fall on me and my house. Thus runs the prophecy: ‘If the king offer not a sacrifice of a fair girl on the day of the dance of maidens to the Old Ones who sit and watch on the mountains, then shall he fall, and his house.’ Look ye: my brother who reigned before me offered not the sacrifice because of the tears of the woman, and he fell, and his house, and I reign in his stead. It is finished; she must die!”

He turned to the guards. “Bring her hither. Scragga, make sharp thy spear.”

Two men stepped forward. The girl understood at last. She screamed—a sound like a bird’s sudden terror—and turned to run, but strong hands caught her and dragged her, weeping and struggling, into the open before us.

“What is thy name, girl?” Gagool asked, her voice thin with mockery. “What! Wilt thou not answer? Shall the king’s son do his work at once?”

At the hint Scragga advanced, more evil than ever, lifting his great spear. I saw Good’s hand creep towards his revolver. The faint glint of metal caught the girl’s eye through tears; it steadied her as a sip of strong drink steadies a fainting person. She stopped struggling and stood shuddering, hands clenched together as though she might squeeze life back into herself by force.

“See,” Scragga cried, delighted, “she shrinks from the sight of my little plaything even before she has tasted it,” and he tapped the broad blade.

“If ever I get the chance,” I heard Good mutter, “you shall pay for that, you young hound.”

“Now that thou art quiet,” Gagool went on, “give us thy name, my dear. Come, speak out, and fear not.”

“Oh, mother,” the girl said in trembling accents, “my name is Foulata, of the house of Suko. Oh, mother, why must I die? I have done no wrong!”

“Be comforted,” Gagool said, with the cruelty of an old cook telling a child the knife will not hurt. “Thou must die as a sacrifice to the Old Ones, but it is better to sleep in the night than to toil in the daytime; it is better to die than to live, and thou shalt die by the royal hand of the king’s own son.”

Foulata wrung her hands and cried aloud, grief pouring from her in a rush of images—sunrise she would not see, stars she would not count, dew-heavy flowers she would not gather, the laughing of waters she would not hear; her father’s hut, her mother’s kiss, the sick lamb she would never tend; the lover’s arm that would never circle her, the children she would never bear. Her voice carried so much of the small domestic life of a people—the very things tyrants treat as nothing—that it struck me harder than any spear-point. Around us some faces softened; even among the guards there were eyes that flickered with pity. But Twala sat unmoved, and Gagool’s mockery never slipped.

Good gave a fierce snort, and made as though to step forward. Foulata saw it with a woman’s quickness and, with a sudden movement, flung herself before him and clasped his legs.

“Oh, white father from the Stars!” she cried. “Throw over me the mantle of thy protection; let me creep into the shadow of thy strength, that I may be saved. Oh, keep me from these cruel men and from the mercies of Gagool!”

“All right, my hearty,” Good blurted in nervous Saxon. “I’ll look after you. Come, get up—there’s a good girl,” and he stooped and caught her hand.

Twala motioned to his son. Scragga advanced again, spear lifted.

“Now’s your time,” Sir Henry whispered to me. “What are you waiting for?”

“I am waiting for that eclipse,” I whispered back, my eyes fixed on the moon as though I could force it to obey by staring. “I’ve watched it half an hour and never saw it look healthier.”

“You must risk it now,” Sir Henry said. “Twala is losing patience.”

He was right. The moment for talk was gone; only theatre would serve us—big enough to stop a spear in mid-air.

I stepped forward with as much dignity as a frightened man can manufacture, placing myself between the prostrate girl and Scragga’s poised blade.

“King,” I said, “it shall not be. We will not endure this thing. Let the girl go in safety.”

Twala sprang to his feet, wrath and astonishment twisting his features. Around us came a murmur from the chiefs and the serried ranks of maidens, who had closed in slowly, drawn by the scent of impending death as people are drawn to a cooking fire.

“Shall not be!” Twala roared. “Thou white dog that yappest at the lion in his cave—shall not be! Art thou mad? Be careful, lest this chicken’s fate overtake thee, and those with thee. Back, I say. Scragga, kill her! Ho, guards! seize these men!”

At his cry armed men ran swiftly from behind a hut where they had clearly been placed in readiness. Sir Henry, Good, and Ignosi ranged alongside me and lifted their rifles.

“Stop!” I shouted, and though my heart had dropped somewhere near my boots I made my voice ring. “Stop! We, the white men from the Stars, say it shall not be. Come but one pace nearer, and we will put out the moon like a wind-blown lamp, as we who dwell in her House can do, and plunge the land in darkness. Dare to disobey, and ye shall taste of our magic.”

The effect was immediate. The guards halted. Scragga stood frozen, his spear still lifted.

“Hear him! Hear him!” piped Gagool. “Hear the liar who says he will put out the moon like a lamp. Let him do it, and the girl shall be spared. Yes—let him do it, or die by the girl, he and those with him.”

I glanced up again, a sick, helpless look—and there, to my intense joy, on the very edge of the bright orb lay a faint rim of shadow. A smoky stain began to gather, as if some unseen hand had smudged the moon with ash.

Never shall I forget that instant: relief so strong it was almost pain, like warm broth poured into a belly that thought it would starve.
