I lifted my hand solemnly towards the sky. Sir Henry and Good did the same, playing their parts at once. I quoted a line or two from the Ingoldsby Legends in the most impressive voice I could summon. Sir Henry followed with a verse from the Old Testament, and—of all things—something about Balbus building a wall in Latin. Good, taking the cue with sailorly enthusiasm, addressed the Queen of Night with a rolling thunder of classical bad language.

Slowly the penumbra crept onward, the shadow of a shadow sliding over the moon’s bright face. As it spread I heard deep gasps rising from the multitude—fear made audible, like a sudden wind through dry reeds.

“Look, O king!” I cried. “Look, Gagool! Look, chiefs and people and women, and see if the white men from the Stars keep their word, or if they be but empty liars!

“The moon grows black before your eyes. Soon there will be darkness—darkness in the hour of the full moon. Ye have asked for a sign; it is given. Grow dark, O Moon! Withdraw thy light, thou pure and holy one; bring the proud heart of usurping murderers to the dust, and eat up the world with shadows.”

A groan of terror burst from the onlookers. Some stood as if turned to stone; others dropped to their knees and cried aloud. Twala, for all his blood-lust, turned pale beneath his dusky skin. Only Gagool held her nerve.

“It will pass,” she shrilled. “I have often seen the like before. No man can put out the moon. Lose not heart—sit still—the shadow will pass.”

“Wait,” I answered, hardly able to keep the triumph from my voice, “and ye shall see.”

The darkness thickened on the moon like soot gathering on a pot hung too long over a smoky fire. The songs had died; even the night insects seemed to listen. In that strange hush, Good’s curses rolled on—vast, inventive, unending—while above us the sky performed our promised miracle, and the world held its breath as the light began to fail.

Luckily for us, Infadoos and the elder chiefs carried the map of Loo in their bodies as surely as a cook carries the memory of a hearth: every alley, every cattle-gap, every narrow run between hut-walls where the smell of last night’s ash still clung. In the unnatural dusk men’s voices had turned low and startled, as if even speech might break. We slipped along side-ways, unchallenged, while above us the sky held its breath.

For more than an hour we went on so, feet scuffing in dust that felt suddenly cold. Then the eclipse began to loosen. The bitten edge of the moon showed again—first a thin, pale rim, like the bright blade of a knife drawn from a sheath. A silver streak burst out, and with it a ruddy glow, as though some unseen fire had been coaxed to life behind a shutter. The stars, those watchful pin-pricks, began to fade. In five minutes there was enough light to see our own hands and the sheen of spear-points.

We found ourselves clear of Loo and nearing a flat-topped hill, a great table of rock and grass, horseshoe-shaped, its sides strewn with boulders like pots knocked from a shelf and left where they fell. It was not high—no more than a couple of hundred feet—but it rose with a stubborn, compact steepness that made the lungs work and the thighs burn. On the summit, the table-land offered wide camping-ground; it had been used like a sanatorium for regiments returned from fever-country, a place to sleep and sweat the sickness out. Tonight it was something else. As we climbed in returning moonlight, we saw more than one regiment’s fires; there were several camps, their rings and ranks spread across the grass.

When we gained the top, we walked into a human tumult: men roused from sleep, shivering, huddled in knots, fear moving through them like wind through dry mealie leaves. They had seen the moon go out. They did not know whether the world had been swallowed or merely punished. We pushed through without a word, and came to a hut near the centre.

There, to our astonishment, stood two men waiting, bent under loads—our few goods and chattels. In the rush of flight we had left them behind as one leaves a pot on the fire when the roof catches. Infadoos nodded toward the bundles as calmly as if he had merely ordered extra water for a stew.

“I sent for them,” he said. “And also for these.”

He lifted, with a flourish, Good’s long-lost trousers.

Good gave a little cry that was half delight, half relief, the sound of a man reunited with an old certainty. He pounced on them, and, without shame or ceremony, began to put them on at once.

“Surely my lord will not hide his beautiful white legs!” Infadoos exclaimed, sincerely regretful, as though a prized dish had been taken off the table before the guests had tasted it.

But Good persisted. It was, I believe, the last time the Kukuanas ever had a fair chance of admiring those celebrated limbs. Good is modest to the marrow. Henceforward they had to satisfy their aesthetic hungers with his single whisker, his transparent eye, and the extraordinary trick of his movable teeth, which gave them more wonder than any song.

Infadoos’s face still held a sort of fond amazement over the trousers when he turned to business. He told us he had ordered the regiments to muster at daybreak. The chiefs had agreed: the rebellion must be explained while men’s minds were still soft with fear of the darkened moon, and Ignosi must be shown plainly—named, placed, claimed—as rightful heir.

So, when the sun was up, the troops—some twenty thousand, the flower of the Kukuana army—were drawn together on an open space. We went out with Infadoos and Ignosi. The men stood in three sides of a dense square, shields overlapping in disciplined blocks, the fourth side left open to us. The sight was magnificent: dark bodies, iron glints, the steady breathing of many thousands making a low, living hush. Around us pressed chiefs and captains; silence was proclaimed.

Then Infadoos spoke.

Like most Kukuanas of high rank, he was a born orator. His words came with a cadence that made even plain facts sound like a chant remembered from grandfathers. He told them of Ignosi’s father—how he had been murdered by Twala, and how the wife and child had been driven out to starve. He reminded them what they all knew in their bones: that Twala’s rule was a famine of mercy, a long gnawing hunger in the heart of the land. He pointed to the previous night’s horrors, when noble men had been dragged forth under the name of evil-doers and done to death with wicked haste.

Then he turned their fear into a tool.

The white lords from the Stars, he said, had looked down and seen the country’s trouble, and had come—at great personal inconvenience, which pleased them mightily as proof of greatness—to relieve it. They had taken Ignosi, the true king, by the hand and led him over the mountains. They had seen Twala’s wickedness, and to save the girl Foulata and steady the wavering, they had, by high magic, put out the moon and slain Scragga, the young fiend. And they would stand by the Kukuanas to overthrow Twala and set Ignosi upon the seat of his fathers.

A murmur rose—approval, awe, the shifting sound of a people choosing a story to live inside.

Ignosi stepped forward. He reiterated what his uncle had said, but in his own manner—less flowing, more like the ring of iron on stone. He ended with power:

“O chiefs, captains, soldiers, and people, ye have heard my words. Now must ye make choice between me and him who sits upon my throne, the uncle who killed his brother and hunted his brother’s child forth to die in the cold and the night. That I am indeed the king these”—he pointed to the chiefs—“can tell you, for they have seen the snake about my middle. If I were not the king, would these white men be on my side with all their magic? Tremble, chiefs, captains, soldiers, and people! Is not the darkness they have brought upon the land to confound Twala and cover our flight, darkness even in the hour of the full moon, yet before your eyes?”

“It is,” answered the soldiers.

“I am the king; I say to you, I am the king,” he went on, drawing himself up and lifting his broad-bladed battle-axe till it flashed. “If there be any man among you who says that it is not so, let him stand forth and I will fight him now, and his blood shall be a red token that I tell you true. Let him stand forth, I say.”

He shook the axe; sunlight ran along it like water.

Nobody accepted this heroic invitation to be slaughtered for argument’s sake, so Ignosi went on.

He promised rewards—oxen, wives, precedence of regiments. And he promised something rarer than cattle: an end to fear used as law. When he sat on the seat of his fathers, bloodshed would cease in the land. No longer would men cry for justice and find slaughter. No longer would the witch-finder hunt a man out so that he might be slain without cause. The “eating up” of kraals would cease; each would sleep secure in his own hut; justice would walk blindfold through the land.

“Have ye chosen, chiefs, captains, soldiers, and people?”

“We have chosen, O king,” they answered, and their voices held that relieved steadiness men have when they finally stop wavering.

Ignosi pointed beyond the hill to Loo. Twala’s messengers, he said, were even then running east and west and north and south to gather an army to kill them all. To-morrow, or the next day, Twala would come in force. Then Ignosi would see who was truly his man, who feared not to die; and such a one would not be forgotten when spoil was divided. He sent them to their huts to make ready for war.

There was a pause, like the moment before a drum is struck. Then a chief lifted his hand and out rolled the royal salute: “Koom.” It was the sound of acceptance, as final as a seal. The battalions marched away in order.

Half an hour later we held a council of war with all the commanders. From our height we could see Loo like a disturbed anthill: troops mustering, runners darting out in every direction to summon more. We had about twenty thousand men—seven of the best regiments. Twala, Infadoos calculated, had thirty to thirty-five thousand already in Loo, with perhaps another five thousand by midday on the morrow. Some might desert to us, but one could not season a pot with salt not yet fetched.

Active preparations were clear enough. Armed bodies patrolled the hill’s foot in slow circles, a tightening ring. Yet Infadoos and the chiefs judged there would be no assault that day. Twala would use the daylight to steady his men’s minds, to scrape away the moral terror left by the moon’s darkening. The onslaught would come tomorrow. They were right.

So we set to work to strengthen the position. Nearly every man laboured; the day, though long in the sky, felt far too short in the hands. The paths up the hill were blocked with masses of stone; every approach was made as hard as time allowed. Boulders were piled where they could be loosed and rolled down on climbing ranks. Stations were allotted to regiments, orders passed, messengers ran like ants between knots of authority. The hill that had nursed regiments back from fever now braced itself to break an army’s teeth.

Just before sundown we rested, sweating and dusty, and saw a small company approaching from Loo. One man held a palm leaf as sign of a herald. Ignosi, Infadoos, a couple of chiefs, and we went down to meet him at the foot of the mountain.

He was a gallant-looking fellow, wrapped in the regulation leopard-skin cloak. His voice carried with the assurance of one who believes the world belongs to his master.

“Greeting!” he cried. “The king’s greeting to those who make unholy war against the king; the lion’s greeting to the jackals that snarl around his heels.”

“Speak,” I said.

“These are the king’s words. Surrender to the king’s mercy ere a worse thing befall you. Already the shoulder has been torn from the black bull, and the king drives him bleeding about the camp.”

The phrase turned my stomach. There are cruelties that announce war more plainly than any drum.

“What are Twala’s terms?” I asked, partly from curiosity and partly because sometimes even foul offers reveal the measure of the man.

The herald swelled, like a singer reciting a long list of patrons.

“His terms are merciful, worthy of a great king. These are the words of Twala, the one-eyed, the mighty, the husband of a thousand wives, lord of the Kukuanas, keeper of the Great Road, beloved of the Strange Ones who sit in silence at the mountains yonder, Calf of the Black Cow, Elephant whose tread shakes the earth, Terror of the evil-doer, Ostrich whose feet devour the desert, huge One, black One, wise One, king from generation to generation! These are the words of Twala: ‘I will have mercy and be satisfied with a little blood. One in every ten shall die, the rest shall go free; but the white man Incubu, who slew Scragga my son, and the black man his servant, who pretends to my throne, and Infadoos my brother, who brews rebellion against me, these shall die by torture as an offering to the Silent Ones.’ Such are the merciful words of Twala.”

We spoke together quickly. Then I answered him loudly so that those above might hear and carry it like smoke into the camp:

“Go back, thou dog, to Twala who sent thee, and say that we—Ignosi, veritable king of the Kukuanas; Incubu, Bougwan, and Macumazahn, the wise ones from the Stars who make dark the moon; Infadoos of the royal house; and the chiefs, captains, and people here gathered—make answer and say: ‘We will not surrender. Before the sun has gone down twice, Twala’s corpse shall stiffen at Twala’s gate, and Ignosi, whose father Twala slew, shall reign in his stead.’ Now go, ere we whip thee away, and beware how thou dost lift a hand against such as we are.”

The herald laughed, loudly and contemptuously, like a man snapping a twig.

“Ye frighten not men with such swelling words,” he cried. “Show yourselves as bold to-morrow, O ye who darken the moon. Be bold, fight, and be merry, before the crows pick your bones till they are whiter than your faces. Farewell; perhaps we may meet in the fight. Fly not to the Stars, but wait for me, I pray, white men.”

With that he turned back toward Loo, his palm leaf bobbing like a pennant.

Almost at once the sun sank. Night came down, and with it work. Exhausted as we were, preparations went on by moonlight—stones shifted, posts reinforced, sentries placed, messages carried in whispers so as not to rouse panic. The camp’s soundscape changed: fewer voices, more footfalls, the scrape of rock on rock, the occasional metallic click of a spear-head, and the steady night wind combing the grass. In those sounds men tried to find courage. There is a kind of health in quiet; the hill’s natural hush held us together better than any shouting could have done.

At last, about an hour after midnight, all that could be done was done. The camp sank into silence, broken only by the challenge of a sentry now and then—two words flung out and answered, like the call and response of nocturnal birds.

Sir Henry and I, with Ignosi and a chief, went down and made a round of the pickets. As we walked, spears gleamed suddenly from unexpected shadows and vanished again when we spoke the password. No one slept at his post.

We climbed back, picking our way through thousands of sleeping warriors. Many were taking their last earthly rest. Moonlight flickered along spear-shafts and played over faces, turning them solemn, almost carved. The chilly wind tugged at tall plumes until they looked like hearse-feathers. Men lay in confusion, arms flung out, legs bent at odd angles, shields tucked close as if even in sleep they expected the shove of an enemy’s body.

“How many of these do you suppose will be alive at this time to-morrow?” Sir Henry asked.

I could only shake my head. I looked again at the sleepers, and to my worn, excited mind it seemed Death had already marked them. It is a strange thing, the way the mind reaches for patterns when the future becomes a blade: it singled out men as if it could see the ones sealed to slaughter, and pity came on me like a flood. Tonight these thousands slept their healthy sleep. Tomorrow, they and many others—ourselves, perhaps—would be stiff in the cold. Wives would wake as widows. Children would become fatherless. The world would not pause for them.

Yet above, the old moon shone on serenely, untroubled as a domestic lamp kept burning while the house argues. The wind moved through grass the way it must have moved ages before men named it, and will move ages after our names are forgotten. And still—though names vanish—the breath a man breathes does not entirely leave the earth. The sound of words spoken once does not wholly die; it becomes part of the great, unownable murmur that lives in places. I felt, standing there in that lunar hush, that the universe keeps our voices the way a valley keeps echoes: changed, thinned, mingled, but not nothing. Not sheeted ghosts, but elements of lives—stubborn remnants that blend and change and change again.

All these reflections crowded in—for as I grow older, I regret to say, a detestable habit of thinking seems to be getting hold of me—while I stared at those grim, fantastic lines.

“Curtis,” I said, “I am in a condition of pitiable fear.”

Sir Henry stroked his yellow beard and laughed softly.

“I have heard you make that sort of remark before, Quatermain.”

“Well, I mean it now. I very much doubt if one of us will be alive to-morrow night. We shall be attacked in overwhelming force, and it is quite a chance if we can hold this place.”

“We’ll give a good account of some of them,” he answered. “Look here, Quatermain, this business is nasty, and one with which, properly speaking, we ought not to be mixed up. But we are in for it, so we must make the best of our job. Speaking personally, I’d rather be killed fighting than any other way, and now that there seems little chance of finding my poor brother, it makes the idea easier to me. Fortune favours the brave, and we may succeed. Anyway, the battle will be awful, and having a reputation to keep up, we shall need to be in the thick of it.”

He said the last in a mournful voice, but his eye held a gleam that contradicted him. I have long suspected Sir Henry Curtis actually likes fighting.

We slept a couple of hours.

Just about dawn Infadoos woke us. Great activity, he said, was to be seen in Loo; skirmishers were driving in our outposts. We rose and dressed for the fray, each of us putting on his chain armour shirt—never had iron felt so welcome on my skin.

Sir Henry went the whole length and dressed like a native warrior.

“When you are in Kukuanaland, do as the Kukuanas do,” he remarked, drawing the shining steel over his broad breast; it fitted him like a glove.

At his request Infadoos had provided full war gear. He fastened round his throat the leopard-skin cloak of a commanding officer. He bound on his brow the plume of black ostrich feathers worn only by generals. Around his middle he tied a magnificent moocha of white ox-tails. Sandals, a leglet of goat’s hair, a heavy battle-axe with a rhinoceros-horn handle, a round iron shield covered with white ox-hide, and the regulation number of tollas completed him—save that he added his revolver.

Savage it was, no doubt; but I seldom saw a finer sight. The barbaric finery seemed designed to announce what his body already declared: strength. When Ignosi arrived presently in similar dress, I found myself thinking I had never seen two such splendid men.

As for Good and myself, the mail did not suit us nearly so well. Good insisted on keeping his trousers; and a stout, short gentleman with an eye-glass, one half of his face shaved, arrayed in a mail shirt tucked carefully into a seedy pair of corduroys, looks more remarkable than imposing. My own chain shirt was too big, so I wore it over all my clothes; it bulged in an ungainly fashion. I discarded my trousers, however, keeping only my veldtschoons, meaning to fight bare-legged to run the lighter if retreat became necessary. The mail coat, a spear, a shield I hardly knew how to use, a couple of tollas, a revolver, and a huge plume pinned into the top of my shooting hat to give a bloodthirsty finish—this was my modest equipment.

Of course we had our rifles too, but ammunition was scarce; and in a charge they would be useless. We arranged they should be carried behind us by bearers.

When we were equipped we swallowed food hastily—dry, hurried mouthfuls, eaten not for pleasure but to keep the limbs steady. Even so, the smell of it—smoke and grain—seemed to anchor the mind for a moment in the ordinary world, as though somewhere, in some hut, a pot still simmered as it always had, indifferent to kings.

Then we went out to see how matters stood.

On the table-land there was a little brown-stone koppie that served as head-quarters and conning tower. There we found Infadoos among his own regiment, the Greys—the finest in the Kukuana army, three thousand five hundred strong now—held in reserve. The men lay on the grass in companies, watching.

Below, the king’s forces crept out of Loo in long ant-like columns. There seemed no end to them—three columns, each, by our reckoning, eleven or twelve thousand men. As soon as they were clear of the town they formed up. Then one body marched to the right, one to the left, and the third came on slowly toward us.

“Ah,” said Infadoos, quietly, “they are going to attack us on three sides at once.”

It was serious news. Our hill-top position, a mile and a half in circumference, was too wide for comfort; we needed concentration, yet could not dictate the shape of the storm. So we made the best of it. Orders went out to the regiments to prepare to receive the separate onslaughts, and the great waiting began, the air full of distant footfalls and the thin, metallic whisper of arms being readied—sounds that, once heard, are hard to forget, as though the land itself has a language for war that no man can truly translate.

The three enemy columns came on as if time itself belonged to them—no running, no shouting yet, only that steady, measured creep of feet and shields which, heard from above, is like rain beginning far off and deciding to become a storm.

We had our men posted in a bent line on the hillside, the hollow of it facing Loo, the two horns reaching down toward the plain. Below, the centre column halted at the root of a long green tongue of grass that licked up into the hill’s curve, waiting while the other two divisions slid wide to right and left to fold us in. Even before spear met shield you could taste the day: sun-warmed stone, the metallic tang of sweat, and, drifting up from thousands of bodies, that faint animal smell of hide and hair rubbed hard with fat. The air carried sound with a cruel fidelity. Every clink of a spear-butt on rock, every low-voiced order, arrived at our ears as clean as a bellstroke. It was a world without engines; no iron wheels, no pounding pistons—only men, breath, and the old instruments of killing. There was a strange health in that honesty of noise, even as it promised slaughter.

“Oh, for a gatling!” Good groaned, staring down at the packed ranks. “I would clear that plain in twenty minutes.”

“We haven’t got one,” Sir Henry said, without taking his eyes off the manoeuvre. Then, with that cold cheerfulness he could put on like a coat, he turned on me. “Try a shot, Quatermain. See if you can drop that tall fellow who seems to be commanding. Two to one you miss him—and an even sovereign, honestly paid if ever we get out, that you don’t put your bullet within five yards.”

The wager pricked me like a thorn. I loaded the express with solid ball and lay down behind a rock. The officer—tall, confident, stepping ten yards forward with only an orderly—stood plain enough, his head and shoulders sharp against the grass. The rifle was sighted only to three hundred and fifty yards; I took him halfway down the neck for the drop, breathed once, and pressed.

Smoke jumped, cleared—and there he was, still upright, untouched. His orderly, three paces to the left, had folded as though a rope had been cut. The commander spun and began to run back.

“Bravo, Quatermain!” Good sang out. “You’ve frightened him.”

To miss is one thing; to miss with witnesses is another. My pride, that poor hungry creature, reared up and demanded feeding. I swung the second barrel after the running man and fired.

He flung up his arms and went down on his face.

Our men, watching from behind the spear-hedge, broke into wild cheering at the white man’s “magic,” and the regiment that had followed the fallen leader wavered and fell back in disorder. Sir Henry and Good began shooting in earnest; Good worked his Winchester like a cook turning a spit—quick, methodical, never pausing to admire the crackle—while I took what shots I could. Six or eight men went down before they were out of range, and then the fight ceased to be something a rifle could manage.

From far right came a deep roar; from far left another answered it. The outer divisions had struck our horns.

The mass below opened, advanced, and began to trot up the green tongue, singing as they ran—a thick, chest-born song that rolled like wind in a cave. We fired steadily, Ignosi firing now and then too, but our bullets were pebbles thrown at a breaking sea. The pickets among the lower rocks were swallowed. Then the climb began to tell; the attackers slowed to save breath, and the sound of their breathing rose with the sound of their feet.

We had three lines: one halfway down, one fifty yards behind it, and the third on the plateau’s edge. The enemy came on, shields forward, spears high, shouting their cry—“Twala! Twala! Chiele! Chiele!”—and our men answered, “Ignosi! Ignosi! Chiele! Chiele!”

Now the tollas flashed, those cruel throwing-knives slipping through sunlight like fish in a stream. Then the awful yell, and the closing shock.

What sound is a battle at spear-range? Not one sound, but a weave: the hollow drum of shield on shield, the wet crack of wood meeting bone, the hiss of breath drawn through teeth, the brief high cry when iron finds the soft place. The hillside became a living thing, heaving and swaying. Men fell as leaves do when the first hard wind of autumn arrives—not one here and there, but in gusts.

Our first line buckled under sheer weight and pressed back into the second. The second line fought like a wall fighting a flood. For a time it held; then it too was driven back, and within twenty minutes our third line was engaged along the plateau edge.

By then the assailants were blown and bleeding; their rush had spent itself on rock and spear. They had lost heavily, and that last hedge of points was too dense to break. The two bodies of men rocked back and forth in fierce, uncertain ebb, and the issue hung like a knife balanced on its tip.

Sir Henry’s eyes were alight. Without a word he vaulted down into the thick of it, battle-axe in hand, and Good went after him.

I stayed where I was, where I could see and hear and be useless in the way I am best at. The soldiers caught sight of Sir Henry’s great form plunging among them, and a roar went up—“Nanzia Incubu! Nanzia Unkungunklovo!”—Here is the Elephant! Here is the great Elephant! And then again, “Chiele! Chiele!”

From that moment the end of that thrust was certain. Inch by inch our men, heartened by the sight, drove the attackers back down the slope until they reeled into their reserves in something like confusion. A messenger came—left horn repulsed the assault. I found myself breathing again, foolishly thinking the worst was over.

Then, across the plain to our right, we saw our own men streaming back toward us—broken, pursued—enemy swarms behind them. The right defence had failed.

Ignosi took in the whole picture in one glance and snapped out an order. The Greys around us extended, shields interlocking in a long grey rib. Another command, caught up and flung along by captains’ voices—and to my intense disgust, I was swept forward with the rest into a full onslaught.

I have never pretended to love close fighting. I got as much of Ignosi’s great frame between myself and the world as I could and went on, small legs carrying me to be killed as if I had asked for it.

We plunged through our own fugitives; as we passed they began to re-form behind us, shame and fury stiffening their spines. Then the lines met.

After that, memory becomes sound and fragments: the dreadful rolling thunder of shields colliding; the smell of trampled grass crushed into green bitterness; the hot, copper reek of blood; the grit of dust between my teeth. A huge man burst out of the press at me, eyes starting, spear red already, and for one clear second I knew with perfect simplicity that if I stood upright I would die.

So I did the only clever thing I can remember doing in that charge. I flung myself flat in front of him at the last instant. He could not stop; momentum took him, and he pitched headlong over me. Before he could scramble up, I was on my feet and put a revolver shot into him from behind.

Not long after, someone struck me down. The world blinked out.

When I came to, I was back at the koppie. Good was bending over me, holding a gourd to my mouth. The water tasted of smoke and skin and heaven.

“How do you feel, old fellow?” he asked, and I heard in his voice the thin edge of fear he tried to hide.

I sat up, shook my head once as if to settle the brains. “Pretty well, thank you.”

“Thank Heaven! When I saw them carry you in, I felt quite sick; I thought you were done for.”

“Not this time. A rap on the head, I fancy. How has it ended?”

“Repulsed at every point—for a while. The loss is dreadful. We have two thousand killed and wounded, and they must have lost three. Look—there’s a sight.”

He pointed, and I saw long lines of men advancing by fours. In the centre of each group, borne shoulder-high, was a hide tray—one of the kind the Kukuanas always carried in quantity, with loops at the corners for hands. On them lay the wounded, endless as if the hill itself were producing them.

As the trays arrived, the medicine men—ten to a regiment—fell upon them with swift hands. They examined, pressed, peered, spoke in quick, flat syllables that seemed made for decisions. If a man might live, he was carried away and tended with all the care possible amid such ruin: binding with strips of hide, packing wounds with chewed herbs, splashing water, murmuring old phrases that sounded like lullabies from a language older than any of us.

But if the wound was hopeless, what happened turned my stomach colder than fear ever had. Under pretence of one more examination, a doctor’s knife would flash; an artery opened; and within a minute or two the man’s life ran quietly out. Often the sufferers were already beyond feeling; often the nick was so fast they scarcely seemed to notice. Mercy, perhaps—the cleanest mercy war allows—but it was ghastly all the same. The broad Kukuanas’ spears left such gaping tears that the body had no chance to knit itself back together.

We hurried away from that strip of red work, glad to escape the sight, and found Sir Henry on the far side of the koppie, still gripping his battle-axe. Ignosi stood with him, Infadoos and a few chiefs gathered close, their heads bent like stalks in a wind of urgent talk.

“Thank Heaven, here you are, Quatermain!” Sir Henry said when he saw me. “I can’t quite make out what Ignosi wants. Though we’ve beaten them off, Twala is receiving reinforcements and seems ready to invest us—to starve us out.”

“That’s awkward,” I said.

“Yes—especially as Infadoos says the water’s given out.”

“My lord, that is so,” Infadoos said. His voice was calm, but his eyes went, almost unwillingly, toward the spring. “The water cannot supply so great a multitude. It fails rapidly. Before night we shall all be thirsty. Listen, Macumazahn. Thou art wise. Thou hast seen many wars in the lands from whence thou camest—if indeed they make wars in the Stars. Tell us what we shall do.

“Twala has brought fresh men to fill the places of those who have fallen. Yet Twala has learnt his lesson; the hawk did not think to find the heron ready; but our beak has pierced his breast. He fears to strike again. We too are wounded, and he will wait for us to die; he will wind himself round us like a snake round a buck, and fight the fight of ‘sit down.’”

“I hear thee,” I answered.

“So,” he went on, rising and pointing toward the dense, watchful mass of the enemy, “we have no water here, and but little food. We must choose: to languish like a starving lion in his den, or to strive to break away towards the north, or”—his hand cut down like an axe—“to launch ourselves straight at Twala’s throat.

“Incubu, the great warrior—for to-day he fought like a buffalo in a net, and Twala’s soldiers went down before his axe like young corn before the hail; with these eyes I saw it—Incubu says, ‘Charge.’ But the Elephant is ever prone to charge. Now what says Macumazahn, the wily old fox, who has seen much, and loves to bite his enemy from behind? The last word is in Ignosi the king. But let us hear thy voice, and the voice too of him of the transparent eye.”

“What sayest thou, Ignosi?” I asked.

“Nay, my father,” Ignosi replied. It still struck me oddly to hear that title from a man who now stood in full war-gear, every inch a king, the old servant’s humility burned away by destiny. “Do thou speak, and let me, who am but a child in wisdom beside thee, hearken.”

I took a moment, hasty counsel with Good and Sir Henry—though it was plain enough. A garrison without water is a pot left on the coals: it will boil dry whether you watch it or not. And the longer men sit listening to thirst, the smaller their courage grows; the ears begin to magnify every enemy-shout, every spear-rattle, until even silence becomes a threat.

So I spoke briefly. Trapped as we were, with the spring failing, the best chance was to attack. Not later, but at once—before wounds stiffened, before the sight of Twala’s numbers melted the heart like fat near a fire. Delay, I warned, would invite treachery: captains might make peace, desert, or sell us outright.

Among the Kukuanas my words were received with a respect I have never earned elsewhere. But the decision was Ignosi’s; and as he stood thinking, the soundscape of war pressed in—the moan of the wounded beyond the koppie, the distant murmur of Twala’s host like a sea that never sleeps, the sharp, ritual calls of captains counting men. Even the language of commands mattered: a king’s syllables carried power because they were the king’s, and the wrong mouth speaking them could turn a regiment like a rudder.

At last Ignosi spoke.

“Incubu, Macumazahn, and Bougwan, brave white men and my friends; Infadoos, my uncle, and chiefs—my heart is fixed. I will strike at Twala this day and set my fortunes on the blow, ay, and my life—my life and your lives also.

“Listen. Ye see how the hill curves round like the half-moon, and how the plain runs like a green tongue towards us within the curve?”

“We see,” I said.

“It is now mid-day,” he went on. “The men eat and rest after toil. When the sun has turned and travelled a little way towards darkness, let thy regiment, my uncle, advance with one other down to the green tongue. When Twala sees it he will hurl his force at it to crush it. But the place is narrow; the regiments can come against thee one at a time only—so may they be destroyed one by one. The eyes of all Twala’s army shall be fixed upon a struggle the like of which has not been seen by living man.

“With thee shall go Incubu my friend, that when Twala sees his battle-axe flashing in the first rank of the Greys his heart may grow faint. I will come with the second regiment, that which follows, so that if ye are destroyed—as it might happen—there may yet be a king left to fight for. With me shall come Macumazahn.”

“It is well, O king,” Infadoos said, as calmly as if he were agreeing to take seed to a distant field, not to have his regiment ground to nothing.

“And whilst the eyes of Twala’s soldiers are thus fixed,” Ignosi continued, “one-third of the men who are left alive to us—about six thousand—shall creep along the right horn of the hill and fall upon Twala’s left flank. One-third shall creep along the left horn and fall upon Twala’s right flank. When I see the horns are ready to toss Twala, then will I, with the men who remain to me, charge home in his face. If fortune goes with us, the day is ours, and before Night drives her black oxen from the mountains to the mountains we shall sit in peace at Loo.

“Now let us eat and make ready. Infadoos, prepare that the plan be carried out without fail. And—stay—let my white father Bougwan go with the right horn, that his shining eye may give courage to the captains.”

What followed was done with astonishing speed. In little more than an hour rations were served out and devoured. There was no ceremony about it—no feasting, no fire-smoke perfumes, no slow comfort of a hearth—only the harsh, necessary business of putting strength back into bodies. I saw men squat in the dust and eat as if each mouthful were a command: strips of dried meat softened with saliva, handfuls of meal, a sip of precious water measured like gold. The noises of eating—teeth tearing, throats working, the short sigh a man gives when food hits an empty place—mingled with the clatter of shields being checked and the murmured repetitions of Ignosi’s plan as captains carried it down the line in their own dialects, each word a thread in a net that would either catch a king or strangle us.

Divisions formed. Leaders were instructed. Eighteen thousand men stood ready to move, leaving a guard with the wounded.

Good came up to Sir Henry and me. He looked oddly neat for a man going into that work, moustache stiff, face pale under the grime.

“Good-bye, you fellows,” he said. “I’m off with the right wing, according to orders. Came to shake hands—in case we shouldn’t meet again.”

We shook hands in silence, with as much feeling as Englishmen permit themselves to show without shame.

“It is a queer business,” Sir Henry said, and his deep voice trembled a little. “I confess I never expect to see to-morrow’s sun. So far as I can make out, the Greys, with whom I’m to go, are to fight till they’re wiped out to let the wings slip round and outflank Twala. Well—so be it. At any rate, it will be a man’s death. Good-bye, old fellow. God bless you! I hope you’ll pull through and live to collar the diamonds; but if you do, take my advice and don’t have anything more to do with Pretenders!”

In another second Good had wrung us both by the hand and gone. Infadoos came and led Sir Henry to the front of the Greys, where the narrow green tongue waited like a throat. With many misgivings, and the taste of dust and half-chewed ration still in my mouth, I went with Ignosi to my place in the second attacking regiment, and listened to the hill’s two horns filling with men who moved as quietly as wind through grass, readying themselves to speak—at the proper moment—in steel and in blood.

Within a few minutes the regiments appointed for the flanking march had slipped away, their feet falling with a practised softness along the lee of the rising ground, so that even the sharp-eared scouts of Twala might mistake their going for the ordinary hushes and stirrings of a camp. The pause that followed had in it a strange domestic quiet, as though the whole army, for half an hour, had drawn the same careful breath a cook draws while waiting for a stubborn pot to come to the boil—knowing that the smallest disturbance might spoil the work.

Only after that interval did the Greys and their supporting regiment, the Buffaloes—the chest—bestir themselves. Both were almost fresh: the Greys had been held back in the morning and had lost little save in that successful sweep when I had charged with them and paid for it by being stunned; and the Buffaloes, third line of defence on the left, had scarcely been forced into the press at all. They stood now as full as granaries after harvest, and just as grimly purposeful.

Old Infadoos, wary as an elder who has counted seasons and knows how spirits must be tended before hard labour, used the delay to speak to the Greys. His words came in their poetical fashion—praise, promise, and the old songs of duty—telling them what honour it was to stand in the forefront, telling them, too, that the great white warrior from the Stars would fight in their ranks, and that cattle and promotion would be given to those who lived to taste tomorrow.

I looked along the long black lines—waving plumes, faces cut hard as dried hides—and my throat tightened. Most of them were men past forty, veterans with the patience of old fields that have borne crop after crop, and within the space of an hour the greater part must lie down in the dust and never rise. It was not cruelty for cruelty’s sake; it was that cold, wise recklessness by which a general saves the body by sacrificing the ribs. The Greys were being sent like a well-fed ox to the slaughter-yard so the herd might pass the winter. They understood it. Not a face flinched. There they stood—about to quit the light of day—and looked upon it as if it were simply another task set by custom and king. I confess my own mind was far less composed, and my envy of their steadiness had in it something bitter, like chewing a root one has not learned to like.

“Behold your king!” Infadoos ended, pointing at Ignosi. “Go fight and fall for him, as is the duty of brave men; cursed and shameful for ever be the name of him who shrinks from death for his king, or who turns his back to the foe. Do your homage to the sacred Snake, and then follow on, that Incubu and I may show you a road to the heart of Twala’s host.”

A pause—then the army answered, not in speech, but in sound that moved through bone. From the serried phalanxes rose a murmur like the far whisper of sea on a night without wind: six thousand spear-handles tapping gently against shield rims, one to one, as if a vast household were beating time before a song. The whisper swelled and deepened into a rolling roar that struck the mountain faces and came back, wave upon wave, until the air itself seemed thick with it. Then it ebbed away by degrees, and in the sudden emptiness the royal salute crashed out.

Ignosi lifted his battle-axe in acknowledgement, and the Greys began to file off in triple line—about a thousand fighting men to each, not counting officers. When the last companies had marched some five hundred yards, Ignosi took his place at the head of the Buffaloes, also drawn in threefold order, and gave the word. Off we went. I, as may be imagined, put up the most sincere prayers that I might come out of it with my skin untorn. I have been in queer corners; never in one quite so full of the certainty that my body might be turned from a vessel for breath into mere meat.

When we reached the edge of the plateau the Greys were already half-way down the slope toward the green tongue of land that thrust itself into the mountain bend like the frog of a horse’s foot into the shoe. Beyond, on the plain, Twala’s camp boiled with movement. Regiment after regiment started forward at that long swinging trot of theirs, eager to seize the root of the tongue before our force could spill out into the open of Loo.

The tongue ran some four hundred yards deep. Even at its root, its widest, it was scarcely six hundred and fifty paces across; at the tip, not ninety. The Greys, descending and narrowing into column, reached the place where the strip broadened again, spread back into their triple line, and halted—dead, silent.

We—the Buffaloes—moved down behind them and took our place in reserve, a hundred yards back and a little higher. From there we could see Twala’s whole host, reinforced since the morning and still, for all their losses, not less than forty thousand. They came on swiftly—then hesitated at the root when they understood the throat of the pass: only one regiment could go in at a time, and there, seventy yards from the mouth, unassailable except from the front because of the boulder-strewn walls to either side, stood the Greys—famous, dreaded—ready to hold the way as a man might bar a doorway with his own body.

They stopped. There was no hunger in them for this meal.

Presently a tall general in the usual nodding ostrich plumes came forward with chiefs and orderlies—Twala, I thought. He gave an order. The first regiment raised a shout and charged up at the Greys. The Greys did not stir or answer until the attackers were within forty yards and a volley of tollas rattled among their ranks like stones flung on a roof.

Then, with a bound and a roar, the Greys sprang forward. The meeting was instant and terrible. The roll of shields struck our ears like thunder; in the clear air, with no smoke of firearms to veil it, the plain flickered with spear-points catching light. The mass swayed and stabbed—humanity kneading itself into slaughter—until suddenly the attacking line thinned, and with a slow, long heave the Greys passed over it, as a great wave passes over a hidden ridge. That regiment was destroyed. But the Greys had only two lines left: a third of their number lay down and did not rise.

They closed up shoulder to shoulder and halted again in silence. My heart lifted to catch sight of Sir Henry’s yellow beard moving to and fro as he set men in place. He lived.

We moved forward onto ground carpeted with bodies—dead, dying, wounded—stained red, thick as fallen fruit under a tree after a storm. Ignosi sent the order down that none of the enemy’s wounded were to be killed. So far as I saw, it was obeyed. The cries and groans, if one listened, were like a village after a fire—so many voices that the mind, for sheer self-defence, refuses to translate them into sense.

A second regiment, marked by white plumes, kilts, and shields, advanced against the remaining two thousand Greys. Again the Greys waited in that dreadful stillness until the foe was within forty yards, then hurled themselves in. Again the clash came—awful, heavy, unrelenting.

This time the issue hung longer. The attackers were young men, fighting with a fury that was like green wood flaring hot. By weight and rage they seemed to drive the veterans back. Hundreds fell every minute. Above the shouting and the groans, and woven through the music of clashing spear on shield, ran that thin hissing undertone—“S’gee, s’gee”—the small triumph each victor breathed as his assegai went through flesh and out again.

But discipline, steady as a hand that never forgets the true measure of salt, began to tell. A veteran is worth two young ones when the press is close and breath is short. Just when we thought the Greys must be finished and were readying ourselves to take their place, Sir Henry’s deep voice rang through the din. I saw his battle-axe circling high above his plumes. The Greys ceased to give. They held, rock-still, and the young regiment broke against them like surf, recoiling, returning, recoiling again. Then the Greys began to move—forward.

Ignosi stood beside me, teeth grinding with excitement. “Ah, these are men indeed; they will conquer again. See, it is done!”

Suddenly the white-plumed regiment broke away in flying groups, their head-dresses streaming like torn cloth in the wind. The Greys were victors—yet no longer a regiment. Of the triple line that had entered three thousand strong forty minutes before, there remained perhaps six hundred, blood-spattered and swaying with fatigue. They cheered, waved their spears, and instead of falling back upon us, ran forward a hundred yards after the fugitives, took a rising knoll, and formed again—this time a threefold ring round its base. On the top of the mound I saw Sir Henry, apparently unhurt, and Infadoos with him.

Then Twala’s regiments rolled down upon that doomed ring, and the fight closed in again.

I am, if truth be told, no hero by inclination. I have always disliked fighting and have preserved my own blood, when possible, by the judicious use of my heels. Yet at that moment something in me—perhaps the long tension, perhaps the music of the spears, perhaps the sheer sight of men making a wall with their bodies—caught fire. Verses and savage scraps of old tales leapt up in my head. My horror thawed into a desire to kill. I looked at the Buffaloes behind us—hands twitching, lips apart, faces hungry—and wondered, absurdly, whether my own expression had begun to resemble theirs, as if a man’s features can be rewritten by the language of a crowd.

Ignosi, for all his grinding teeth, seemed the calmest among us, like a chief who can still taste what is in the pot while others only smell smoke.

“Are we to stand here till we put out roots, Umbopa—Ignosi, I mean—while Twala swallows our brothers yonder?” I cried.

“Nay, Macumazahn,” he answered. “See—now is the ripe moment: let us pluck it.”

As he spoke a fresh enemy regiment rushed past the ring on the mound, wheeled, and attacked it from our side.

Ignosi lifted his battle-axe. The signal went. Screaming the wild war-cry, the Buffaloes charged—like the rush of the sea when the tide turns and nothing can argue it back.

What followed immediately I cannot set in order. I remember an advance that was irregular yet somehow kept its shape, as though the ground itself were moving; a sudden forming up of the regiment we struck; then an awful shock—dull roar—spears flashing, seen through a red mist that seemed to rise from the world and hang before the eyes.

When my mind cleared I found myself inside the remnant of the Greys near the top of the mound, just behind Sir Henry. How I came there I did not know then. Sir Henry afterwards told me the first furious rush of the Buffaloes carried me almost to his feet, and when we were pressed back he dashed out, seized me, and dragged me into the shelter of the ring.

The fighting that followed—who can truly describe it? Again and again the multitudes surged against our circle, which shrank as men fell; again and again we beat them back. Each time a comrade dropped, another stepped into his place without thought, as naturally as a hand moves to pinch the next bit of mealie-cake when the first is gone.

It was a splendid horror to see Twala’s battalions come on over barriers of their dead. Some lifted corpses before them to take our spear-thrusts—then left their own bodies to swell the rising heaps. Infadoos was everywhere, cool as if on parade, shouting orders, taunts, and even jests, tending courage as a household tends a shared fire. Sir Henry was more gallant still. A spear had shorn away his ostrich plumes, and his long yellow hair streamed behind him. Hands, axe, and armour were red. None could live before his stroke. Time after time I saw his axe sweep down as a warrior ventured close; and with each blow he shouted “O-hoy! O-hoy!” like some old northern forefather, and the blow went through shield and spear, through head-dress, hair, and skull—until men of their own will ceased to come near the great white umtagati, the wizard who killed and did not miss.

Then a cry rose: “Twala, y’ Twala!” Out of the press sprang the gigantic one-eyed king himself, armed with battle-axe and shield, clothed in chain armour. He was like a bull that has broken its pen, maddened and magnificent.

“Where art thou, Incubu, thou white man, who slewest Scragga my son—see if thou canst slay me!” he shouted, and flung a tolla straight at Sir Henry. Sir Henry saw it, caught it on his shield; it pierced through and stuck wedged in the iron plate behind the hide.

With a cry Twala sprang forward and struck Sir Henry’s shield so terrible a blow that the shock brought Sir Henry—strong as he is—down upon his knees.

But the matter went no further. In that very instant a shout rose from the regiments pressing us—something like dismay. I looked up and saw why.

To right and left the plain was suddenly alive with charging plumes. Our outflanking squadrons had come. Their approach had been masked by the lay of the ground and by the greater sound of the struggle itself; Twala’s men had listened only to the loudest language—the clang and roaring at the mound—and had not heard the quieter sentence being spoken on their flanks until it was too late. Believing the horns still hidden in reserve on the crescent hill, they were caught before they could form properly, and the outflanking impis leapt on them like greyhounds.

Within five minutes the fate of the battle was decided. Taken on both sides and shaken by the slaughter the Greys and Buffaloes had worked among them, Twala’s regiments broke. The whole plain between us and Loo scattered into running groups retreating as best they might. The hosts that had surrounded us seemed to melt away like mist when sun takes hold of it; suddenly we stood there almost alone, like a rock from which the sea has retreated.

But what a shore it left behind.

Around us lay the dead and dying in heaped masses. Of the gallant Greys there remained only ninety-five men on their feet. More than three thousand four hundred had fallen in that one regiment alone, most never to rise.

Infadoos, binding a wound on his arm with the calm precision of one who has dressed many cuts in many seasons, surveyed what was left to him. “Men,” he said quietly, “ye have kept up the reputation of your regiment, and this day’s fighting will be well spoken of by your children’s children.”

Then he turned and shook Sir Henry Curtis by the hand. “Thou art a great captain, Incubu,” he said simply. “I have lived a long life among warriors and have known many a brave one, yet have I never seen a man like unto thee.”

At that moment the Buffaloes began to march past our position on the road to Loo, and a message came from Ignosi asking Infadoos, Sir Henry, and myself to join him. We told the remaining ninety of the Greys to gather the wounded, and went on. Ignosi meant to press to Loo and complete the victory by capturing Twala if possible.

We had not gone far when we saw Good sitting on an ant-heap about a hundred paces away. A Kukuana lay close beside him, apparently dead.

“He must be wounded,” Sir Henry said anxiously.

As he spoke, the “dead” man sprang up, knocked Good head over heels off the ant-heap, and began to spear him. We rushed forward. The brawny warrior drove thrust after thrust at the prostrate Good, who jerked all his limbs up at each prod like a puppet yanked by strings. Seeing us, the fellow gave one final, vicious dig and shouted, “Take that, wizard!” and bolted.

Good did not move. We thought him finished. But when we came close, he was pale and faint yet wore a serene smile, and his eyeglass was still fixed in his eye, absurdly neat amid the chaos.

“Capital armour this,” he murmured, as our faces bent over him. “How sold that beggar must have been,” and then he fainted.

He had, it seemed, been seriously wounded in the leg by a tolla during the pursuit, but his chain armour had saved him from the spear, which did little more than bruise him. It was a merciful escape. Since nothing could be done at once, we placed him on one of the wicker shields used as stretchers and had him carried along.

Before the nearest gate of Loo we found one of our regiments watching it by Ignosi’s orders. Others guarded the remaining exits. The officer in command saluted Ignosi as king and reported that Twala’s army had taken refuge within the town and that Twala himself had escaped there too; but they were demoralised and would likely surrender.

Ignosi took counsel with us, then sent heralds to each gate. They were to order the defenders to open and, on Ignosi’s royal word, promise life and forgiveness to every soldier who laid down arms; but if they did not submit before nightfall he would burn the town and all within its gates. The message worked. Half an hour later, amid the shouts and cheers of the Buffaloes, the bridge dropped across the fosse and the gates beyond were flung wide.

Taking precautions against treachery, we marched into the town. Along the roadways stood thousands of dejected warriors, heads drooping, shields and spears at their feet. Officers at their front saluted Ignosi as king as he passed. We went straight on to Twala’s kraal. When we reached the great open space where, a day or two before, we had watched the review and the witch-hunt, we found it deserted.

Not quite deserted.

There, on the far side, in front of his hut, sat Twala himself with only one attendant—Gagool.

It was a melancholy thing to see him thus: battle-axe and shield beside him, chin resting on his mailed breast, a king reduced to a man alone with an old crone. For all his crimes, a pang of compassion went through me at the sight of him fallen from his high estate. Not one soldier remained of the armies that had roared his name; not one courtier from the hundreds who had crawled about his feet; not even a wife. Poor savage—he was tasting the lesson Fate gives so many: that the eyes of mankind turn blind toward the discredited, and that the defenceless fallen find few friends and little mercy. In his case, indeed, he had earned none.

We filed through the kraal gate and crossed the space toward him. At fifty yards the regiment halted, and with only a small guard we advanced. Gagool reviled us bitterly in that shrill, rasping tongue of hers, flinging words like hot ash.

As we drew near, Twala lifted his plumed head at last and fixed his one eye—flashing with fury as bright as the great diamond on his forehead—upon Ignosi.

“Hail, O king!” he said with bitter mockery. “Thou who hast eaten of my bread, and now by the aid of the white man’s magic hast seduced my regiments and defeated mine army, hail! What fate hast thou in store for me, O king?”

“The fate thou gavest to my father, whose throne thou hast sat on these many years!” Ignosi answered sternly.

When the last spear had fallen and the shouting ebbed into that stunned, after-storm hush, they carried Sir Henry and Good into Twala’s great hut as though they were sacks of meal—too precious to leave outside, too limp to help themselves. I followed, my own feet dragging as if the soles had been traded for lead. Weariness has its own taste: coppery on the tongue, sour in the throat, and sharp at the back of the nose where pain begins to bloom. Mine came with familiar companions—the old lion-wound waking like an ill-tempered dog, and a hammering skull from the morning’s blow that had sent me into darkness.

We were a wretched trio, and we knew it; yet the one comfort that could still be chewed and swallowed was this: misery is a proof of breath. Thousands that had risen with the sun full-chested and laughing now lay stiff on the plain, and would not taste anything again—neither broth nor smoke nor the clean, cold air.

Foulata was there, moving with that swift domestic certainty that turns a battlefield’s aftermath into a sick-room. Since we had helped tear her from Twala’s grip, she had made herself our handmaiden—most especially Good’s—and now she set to work on us with the grave authority of a woman who understands what bodies need when men’s pride has spent itself.

The chain shirts came off with difficulty; each link had done its duty, and each had left its mark. Underneath, the flesh was beaten purple and black as bruised fruit. Steel had kept the points out, but it could not stop the blunt cruelty of impact; Sir Henry and Good were scored and blotched everywhere, and I was not spared.

Foulata brought a poultice—green leaves pounded to a wet paste on some flat stone, the way an old country cook grinds herbs for a stew, pressing until the fibres give up their bitterness and their scent. The smell rose at once: aromatic and sharp, like crushed wild mint mixed with something resinous. She spread it on with warm fingers, firm but gentle, and the relief was immediate, the skin cooling as though a shaded stream had been laid across fire.

Bruises, though, were only a nuisance beside the true dangers. Good’s “beautiful white leg”—a phrase he would have preened at if he’d been capable of vanity just then—had a clean hole right through the fleshy part, and he had bled freely. Sir Henry had other hurts, but the worst was the deep cut along the jaw where Twala’s battle-axe had found him. It would make eating an awkward business and laughter—if any man could have laughed that night—quite impossible.

Good, to his credit, was a decent surgeon even when half undone. When his little medicine-box was brought, he worked by the trembling glow of the Kukuana lamp—primitive oil and wick, throwing a smell of warm fat into the hut. He cleaned the wounds with care, his hands steady in a way his face was not. First he stitched Sir Henry, drawing skin together with quick, practiced motions; then, with the stubbornness of sailors, he set to his own leg and did the same. Afterwards he smeared both cuts with antiseptic ointment from a small pot—pungent, medicinal, a foreign smell amid native smoke—and we bound the places up with what remained of one of our pocket-handkerchiefs.

Foulata, meanwhile, set a pot to the fire and made broth strong enough to stand a spoon in—meat simmered down until it gave up its marrow and salt, a dark, sustaining liquid that didn’t ask for chewing. We were too tired to eat, so we drank. The warmth went into us like a small promise, and when we could no longer keep our eyes open we dropped onto the piles of karrosses—heavy fur rugs that held the day’s heat and smelt of animal life and woodsmoke.

Fate, with its dry sense of humour, arranged Sir Henry on Twala’s own couch, wrapped in Twala’s own particular karross—the very bed of the man he had slain. He slept there, if the word can be used for what we did. After such a day sleep comes in fragments, and each fragment carries you back into the teeth of what you’ve done.

All night the air was thick with sound—sound more than anything, as if the land itself would not let the ear rest. From every direction came the wailing of women: keening cries that rose, broke, and rose again, each one shaped by a different throat yet part of one immense grief. It rolled over the huts like wind in long grass. In that acoustic tide I felt, for the first time fully, the horror of ambition paid in human breath. Over twelve thousand men—nearly a fifth of their army—had been destroyed. Numbers mean little until you lie in the dark and hear what they turn into: voices searching for voices that will never answer.

Toward midnight the crying thinned, not from comfort but from exhaustion, until silence came in uneasy stretches. Then, every few minutes, a long piercing howl cut through—one single note, drawn out like a blade across bone. It came from a hut behind ours. Later I learned it was Gagool, “keening” over Twala, her grief (or her theatre) as thin and sharp as her body.

I slept at last, but in jolts. Dreams returned me to the mountain-top where a warrior I had sent to his last account charged again and again; then I stood once more within that glorious ring of Greys holding the mound against the rush of Twala’s regiments; then Twala’s plumed head rolled past my feet, bloody, gnashing, eyes glaring with a life that should have been gone. Each time I woke, the real sounds—the dying echoes of mourning, the soft shift of hides, the lamp’s faint hiss—seemed no less haunted.

When dawn came we looked at one another like men who had been boiled and poured back into their skins. Good was worse. Fever had him hard; his skin burned, his eyes were too bright, and soon, to my alarm, he began to spit blood—likely from some internal hurt gained when a Kukuana had tried with desperate force to drive a spear through the mail. Sir Henry, in spite of the cut on his face, seemed comparatively fresh, though he was stiff and sore enough to move like an old man.

At about eight, Infadoos came in. The tough old warrior looked little the worse for battle; he told us he had been up all night. He greeted us warmly, grieved to see Good’s state, and shook hands with an earnestness that made his wrist-bangles click. Yet I noticed a change: when he addressed Sir Henry there was reverence in it, as though he spoke to something not quite human. And, as we learned later, throughout Kukuanaland Sir Henry had become a creature of story—no man, they said, could have fought as he fought and then, at the end of such a day, slain Twala—king, and believed strongest warrior in the land—shearing through his bull-neck at a stroke. That blow became a proverb; any feat of extraordinary strength was thereafter called “Incubu’s blow.”

Infadoos told us the regiments had submitted to Ignosi, and messages of submission were coming in from chiefs in the outlying country. With Twala dead by Sir Henry’s hand and Scragga already gone, there was no legitimate rival left alive to stir trouble. I remarked that Ignosi had swum to power through blood. Infadoos only shrugged, the way an elder shrugs at rain.

“Yes,” he said, “but the Kukuana people can only be kept cool by letting their blood flow sometimes. Many are killed, indeed, but the women are left, and others must soon grow up to take the places of the fallen. After this the land would be quiet for a while.”

Later that morning Ignosi himself came, and on his brow the royal diadem was bound. He advanced with a new weight in his step—kingly dignity laid over the memory of the tall Zulu who had appeared in Durban months before asking to enter our service. The wheel of fortune had turned; a man’s name had changed his posture.

“Hail, O king!” I said, rising.

“Yes, Macumazahn. King at last, by the might of your three right hands,” he answered readily.

All, he said, was going well. In two weeks he meant to hold a great feast to show himself to the people. The word “feast” made my mind run, absurdly, to pots and smoke and the peaceable labour of feeding mouths—how strange that a land could be scoured by spears one day and measured by cattle and beer the next, as though history could be stirred into the same calabash as porridge.

I asked what he meant to do with Gagool.

“She is the evil genius of the land,” he said, and there was iron in his voice. “I shall kill her, and all the witch-doctors with her. She has lived so long that none can remember when she was not very old; she trains the witch-hunters and makes the land wicked in the sight of the heavens above.”

“Yet she knows much,” I replied. “It is easier to destroy knowledge, Ignosi, than to gather it.”

He paused, thoughtful. “That is so. She—and she only—knows the secret of the ‘Three Witches’ yonder, whither the great road runs, where the kings are buried, and the Silent Ones sit.”

“Yes,” I said, “and the diamonds are. Forget not thy promise, Ignosi. Thou must lead us to the mines, even if thou hast to spare Gagool alive to show the way.”

“I will not forget, Macumazahn,” he answered. “I will think on what thou sayest.”

After he left, I went to Good. He was wholly delirious now; the fever had seized him and would not let go, complicated by that hidden injury. For four or five days his condition was critical, and I believe with all my heart that without Foulata he would have died.

Women are women, the world over, whatever their colour; but it was still a curious thing to watch this dusky beauty turn Twala’s hut into a place of tending. Her movements were quiet, economical, always attentive to the sound-world around the sickbed. She understood, instinctively, that noise is a kind of violence to a fevered mind. She kept voices low, steps soft, the lamp set where it would not flare and startle. At her insistence, by the king’s order, the huts for three hundred yards behind Twala’s chief hut were cleared—so that no chatter, no laughter, no clatter of domestic life would drift in and jar the sufferer’s ears. In that wide hush you could hear the small truths: the lamp’s faint crackle, the fly’s hungry buzz, Good’s rasping breath.

For the first night or two I tried to help; Sir Henry did as well when he could move. But Foulata bore our interference with impatience and at last drove us away, saying our coming and going made Good restless. She was right. She had the manner of a trained nurse without ever having seen a hospital: swift with water, careful with cloths, unceasing in her watch.

His only medicine, beyond what Good’s box could offer, was a native cooling drink she prepared: milk warmed and then tempered, infused with the juice from the bulb of a tulip-like plant. She mashed the bulb until it bled its pale bitterness, then stirred it through the milk, letting it steep until the scent changed—floral at first, then medicinal, as if the plant’s cold heart had entered the liquid. She fed it to him a little at a time, as one feeds broth to a child, and she kept the flies from his lips and eyes with endless patience.

Night after night I saw the scene by lamplight: Good’s face drawn and emaciated, eyes shining too large in his skull, his mouth running nonsense by the yard; and Foulata sitting on the ground beside him, back against the hut wall, her shapely arms making a sheltering circle of attention. Her face was weary with vigil, yet lit by a look of infinite compassion—or something nearer and more dangerous than compassion.

For two days we thought he must die. Sir Henry and I moved about with heavy hearts. Only Foulata would not accept it.

“He will live,” she said, and her certainty had the blunt force of an elder’s proverb.

On the fifth night of the illness, I went across as usual to see how he fared before taking a few hours of sleep myself. I entered carefully. The lamp on the floor made a small island of light. Good lay quite still.

So it had come at last, I thought. A sob rose in me, bitter and involuntary.

“Hush—h—h!” came a whisper from the darkness behind Good’s head.

I crept closer and saw: he was not dead. He was sleeping—sleeping soundly, the first true sleep of days—and Foulata’s narrow fingers were clasped tight in his poor white hand. The crisis had passed. He would live.

He slept like that for eighteen hours. And I scarcely like to write it, for fear I should not be believed, but through the whole of that time Foulata did not move. She feared that if she shifted, if she drew away her hand, he would wake and fall back into fever. What she suffered—cramp twisting her limbs, hunger gnawing, exhaustion settling into bone—no one can measure. When at last he woke, she had to be carried away; her legs were so stiff she could not stand.

Once the turn was taken, Good’s recovery was rapid and complete. When he was nearly well, Sir Henry told him what he owed Foulata; and at the part about the eighteen hours, the honest sailor’s eyes filled with tears. He rose at once and went straight to the hut where Foulata was preparing the midday meal—our quarters being back to normal now—taking me along to interpret in case his meaning stumbled.

The smell in the hut was comforting after days of sickness: smoke clinging to the thatch, fat warming, something starchy steaming in a pot. Foulata worked as though feeding were a kind of prayer, her hands sure, her attention divided between fire and food with the ease of long practice.

“Tell her,” said Good, “that I owe her my life, and that I will never forget her kindness to my dying day.”

I interpreted. Under her dark skin she seemed to blush. Turning to him with one of those swift, graceful motions that always reminded me of a wild bird’s flight, she answered softly, glancing at him from beneath her lashes with those large brown eyes:

“Nay, my lord; my lord forgets. Did he not save my life, and am I not my lord’s handmaiden?”

It will be observed that she had entirely forgotten the share Sir Henry and I had taken in saving her from Twala. But that is the way of women. My dear wife was much the same. I withdrew from the little scene sad at heart, for I did not like Miss Foulata’s soft glances. I knew too well the fatal amorous propensities of sailors in general and of Good in particular. There are two things, I have found, which cannot be prevented: you cannot keep a Zulu from fighting, and you cannot keep a sailor from falling in love upon the slightest provocation.

A few days after, Ignosi held his great indaba, and was formally recognised as king by the indunas of Kukuanaland. It was an imposing spectacle, with a grand review of troops. The remaining fragments of the Greys were paraded and thanked before the whole army for their splendid stand. Ignosi gave each man a large present of cattle and promoted them all to be officers in the new corps of Greys then being formed. An order went out across the land that while we honoured the country with our presence, the three of us were to be greeted with the royal salute and treated with the same ceremony and respect accorded by custom to the king. Publicly, too, the power of life and death was conferred upon us.

Ignosi reaffirmed, before his people, the promises he had made: no man’s blood should be shed without trial, and witch-hunting would cease. Words, of course, are only sound until they are made into habit; and in any country the dominant tongue can turn justice into a slogan as easily as it can turn cruelty into law. Still, spoken aloud in that open place, with the crowd’s listening silence pressing on the syllables, the promises had a weight.

When the ceremony ended we waited on Ignosi and told him we were now anxious to pursue the mystery of the mines to which Solomon’s Road ran. Had he discovered anything?

“My friends,” he said, “I have discovered this. It is there that the three great figures sit, who here are called the Silent Ones, and to whom Twala would have offered the girl Foulata as a sacrifice. It is there, too, in a great cave deep in the mountain, that the kings of the land are buried; there ye shall find Twala’s body sitting with those who went before him. There also is a deep pit, which, at some time, long-dead men dug out—mayhap for the stones ye speak of, such as I have heard men in Natal tell of at Kimberley. There, too, in the Place of Death is a secret chamber, known to none but the king and Gagool. But Twala, who knew it, is dead, and I know it not, nor know I what is in it.

“Yet there is a legend in the land that once, many generations gone, a white man crossed the mountains, and was led by a woman to the secret chamber and shown the wealth hidden in it. But before he could take it she betrayed him, and he was driven by the king of that day back to the mountains, and since then no man has entered the place.”

“The story is surely true,” I said, “for on the mountains we found the white man.”

“Yes,” he answered. “We found him. And now I have promised you that if ye can come to that chamber, and the stones are there—”

“The gem upon thy forehead proves that they are there,” I put in, pointing to the great diamond I had taken from Twala’s dead brows.

“Mayhap,” he said. “If they are there, ye shall have as many as ye can take hence—if indeed ye would leave me, my brothers.”

“First we must find the chamber,” I said.

“There is but one who can show it to thee,” Ignosi replied. “Gagool.”

“And if she will not?”

“Then she must die,” he said sternly. “I have saved her alive but for this. Stay—she shall choose.”

He called a messenger and ordered Gagool brought before him. In a few minutes she was dragged in by two guards, cursing them as she came. When Ignosi told the guards to leave her, she collapsed at once—no more than a withered bundle of rags with two bright, wicked eyes gleaming out of it like a snake’s.

“What will ye with me, Ignosi?” she piped. “Ye dare not touch me. If ye touch me I will slay you as ye sit. Beware of my magic.”

“Thy magic could not save Twala, old she-wolf, and it cannot hurt me,” Ignosi answered. “Listen. I will this of thee: that thou reveal to us the chamber where are the shining stones.”

“Ha! ha!” she piped. “None know its secret but I, and I will never tell thee. The white devils shall go hence empty-handed.”

“Thou shalt tell me. I will make thee tell me.”

“How, O king? Thou art great, but can thy power wring the truth from a woman?”

“It is difficult,” he said, “yet will I do so.”

“How, O king?”

“Nay, thus: if thou tellest not thou shalt slowly die.”

“Die!” she shrieked, terror and fury twisting her voice into something that seemed too loud for her small body. “Ye dare not touch me—man, ye know not who I am. How old think ye am I? I knew your fathers, and your fathers’ fathers’ fathers. When the country was young I was here; when the country grows old I shall still be here. I cannot die unless I be killed by chance, for none dare slay me.”

“Yet will I slay thee,” Ignosi said. “See, Gagool, mother of evil—thou art so old that thou canst no longer love thy life. What can life be to such a hag as thou, who hast no shape, nor form, nor hair, nor teeth—hast naught save wickedness and evil eyes? It will be mercy to make an end of thee.”

“Thou fool!” she shrieked. “Deemest thou that life is sweet only to the young? It is not so, and naught thou knowest of the heart of man to think it. To the young, death is sometimes welcome, for the young can feel. They love and suffer, and it wrings them to see their beloved pass to the land of shadows. But the old feel not, they love not, and—ha! ha!—they laugh to see another go out into the dark. Ha! ha! They laugh to see the evil that is done under the stars. All they love is life—the warm, warm sun and the sweet, sweet air. They are afraid of the cold, afraid of the cold and the dark—ha! ha! ha!”

She writhed on the ground in ghastly merriment, and the sound of it—thin laughter scraping along the ear—made the skin crawl more than any spear-point could have done. There are voices that carry old power; not always good power, but the kind that survives because people cannot forget it.

“Cease thine evil talk and answer me,” Ignosi said angrily. “Wilt thou show the place where the stones are, or wilt thou not? If thou wilt not thou diest, even now.”

He seized a spear and held it over her.

“I will not show it,” she hissed. “Thou darest not kill me—darest not! He who slays me will be accursed for ever.”

Slowly Ignosi brought the spear down until its point pricked the prostrate heap of rags. With a wild yell Gagool sprang up, then fell again and rolled upon the floor.

“Nay!” she screamed. “I will show thee. Only let me live—let me sit in the sun and have a bit of meat to suck, and I will show thee.”

“It is well,” Ignosi said. “I thought that I should find a way to reason with thee. To-morrow shalt thou go with Infadoos and my white brothers to the place. Beware how thou failest; for if thou showest it not, then thou shalt slowly die. I have spoken.”

“I will not fail, Ignosi,” she said, and her eyes glinted as though she tasted some private relish. “I always keep my word—ha! ha! ha! Once before a woman showed the chamber to a white man, and behold—evil befell him. Her name was Gagool also. Perchance I was that woman.”

“Thou liest,” I said. “That was ten generations gone.”

“Mayhap, mayhap,” she answered. “When one lives long one forgets. Perhaps it was my mother’s mother who told me; surely her name was Gagool also. But mark: ye will find in the place where the bright things are a bag of hide full of stones. The man filled that bag, but he never took it away. Evil befell him, I say—evil befell him! Perhaps it was my mother’s mother who told me.

“It will be a merry journey—we can see the bodies of those who died in the battle as we go. Their eyes will be gone by now, and their ribs will be hollow. Ha! ha! ha!”
