Darkness had already thickened on the third day after our late turmoil when we took shelter in a cluster of huts crouched at the feet of the “Three Witches”—that sharp triangle of peaks to which Solomon’s Great Road runs as to an altar. We were a strange household for such a hearth: Sir Henry Curtis, Good, and myself; Foulata moving quietly among us with the grave attentiveness of one born to make small kindnesses feel like law; Infadoos with his guard; and Gagool, bundled in a litter, the old witch’s voice rasping from within it all day—muttering, spitting curses, and now and then breaking out into that cracked laughter that turned the air colder than night.

Before dawn had properly loosened its grip, the mountains took their full shape. Three peaks, three points of one upheaved mass: one to our right, one to our left, and one straight ahead like a judgement. In the early sunlight their snow lay twisted and wreath-like, bright as salt piled on dark boards; below the snow-line the heaths purpled the slopes, and the moors climbed upward in wild, stubborn patches. Straight before us, the Great Road shone pale as a ribbon of bone, running uphill for five miles to the foot of the centre peak—and there it ended, as abruptly as a sentence cut off.

We set out with a haste that was almost hunger. For months, for years in some sense, the idea of those mines had been the daily bread of our imaginations: the old Portuguese Dom who had died miserably three centuries gone; the ill-starred descendant who had followed the same lure to his ruin; and—so we feared—George Curtis, Sir Henry’s brother, swallowed by the same bright promise. The Road beneath our boots felt too clean, too deliberate; the morning itself seemed to listen. Even the soundscape altered as we climbed: the hush of high ground, the faint scratch of wind in heath, the far thin calls of birds that carried too clearly in the rare air. It was a world with no industrial roar to blunt the nerves—no clatter of carts, no iron pounding—only the steady drum of our own blood and footfalls, and the occasional creak of leather. That clarity of sound, that acoustic purity, ought to have been peace. Instead it sharpened every misgiving.

We walked so fast in our excitement that the bearers of Gagool’s hammock lagged; at last her piping voice shot out from the litter like a thorn.

“Walk more slowly, white men,” she called, thrusting her shrivelled face between the grass curtains. Her eyes shone as if lit from behind. “Why will ye run to meet the evil that shall befall you, ye seekers after treasure?”

And she laughed—high, cracked, delighted. The laughter did what a sudden sour smell can do to appetite: it turned our eagerness queasy. For a little while the road felt less like promise and more like a channel dug for doom.

Still we went on.

An hour and a half brought us to a sight that made even Good fall silent. Before us yawned a vast circular pit with sloping sides—three hundred feet deep or more, and half a mile round—its edges softened with grass and bush, but its emptiness still raw enough to make the stomach drop. The Great Road split and went around it, as though even stonework had learned to step aside.

“Can’t you guess what this is?” I asked.

They stared, shook their heads.

“Kimberley,” I said. “You’ve never seen the diamond diggings. This is the same story written in another hand. Look there—blue clay still showing in the sides. And down there—those worn flat slabs? Washing-tables, I’d swear it. This is Solomon’s diamond mine.”

The pit had the same patient brutality as any great excavation: the sense of countless hands, countless days, a human will that had bitten into the earth and not let go. Yet here, with heather growing where men had once clawed and cursed, it wore an ancient calm. A breeze slid along the rim and down into the hollow, and its sound changed as it fell—softened, swallowed—like speech disappearing into a language nobody spoke any more.

We followed the road that skirted the gulf, drawn toward three towering shapes visible beyond. As we neared, they resolved into Colossi seated upon huge pedestals of dark rock, carved with rude emblems of the old worship of fertility. Forty paces apart they sat, facing down the road that ran sixty miles across plain to Loo, staring forever across distance as if distance were their native element.

It was only close at hand that their full majesty struck like a physical blow. Two male figures, draped, their faces terrible—one to our right with a devil’s cast that made the very stone seem to sneer; one to our left serene, but with a serenity that chilled more than rage. Between them the female figure sat nude, beautiful in a severe way, though weather had eaten at the features; from either side of her head rose the points of a crescent, like horns of a half-moon.

Sir Henry, looking up, spoke low. “That calm,” he said, “is the calm of cruelty that does not need to strain itself.”

Contemplation brought curiosity on like thirst. Who had carved these? Who had driven the road, dug the pit, made this place where stone obeyed a mind long dead?

Because my head is full of old texts and their stubborn names, it came to me suddenly—Solomon, who went astray after strange gods. “Ashtoreth,” I said, and named also Chemosh and Milcom. “Perhaps these are the three.”

Sir Henry’s scholar’s mind took the suggestion and turned it in his hands. “There may be something in it. Ashtoreth of the Hebrews—Astarte of the Phoenicians. Horned. Crescent. Traders in Solomon’s time. A Phoenician overseer, perhaps, left his gods here in stone. Who can say?”

Who indeed? Names are nets, and the past slips through them. Even when you catch a syllable—Ashtoreth, Astarte—the living feel of what those sounds meant to those who spoke them is gone, as untranslatable as the taste of a vanished fruit. The statues sat on, indifferent to our guesses, their silence a kind of dominance: whatever they were called, they remained what they were, and our language did not move them.

Infadoos came up then, saluted the “Silent Ones” with his spear, and asked whether we would enter the “Place of Death” at once, or wait till mid-day to eat.

We were like men who have smelled a feast and cannot bear delay. It was not later than eleven, and our curiosity burned hotter than any hunger. We would go in immediately. Still, I urged that we take food, in case the cave held us.

Gagool’s litter was brought up. She was hauled out, and stood leaning on her stick, grinning at us as though our faces were dishes set before her. At my request Foulata packed biltong—dried game flesh, hard and dark, salted with sun and smoke—into a reed basket with a hinged cover, and added two gourds of water. She did it with the calm precision of someone who knows the value of provisions, whose people have always understood that sustenance is memory made portable: smoke and salt keeping not only meat, but stories, from spoiling.

Straight ahead, about fifty paces behind the Colossi, rose a sheer wall of rock—eighty feet and more—sloping upward into the base of the snow-wreathed peak that soared three thousand feet above us. Gagool hobbled toward it, casting one evil grin back over her shoulder, and we followed to a narrow portal, solidly arched, like the mouth of a mine.

There she waited, gourd-lamp in hand, her face pinched and gleaming.

“Now, white men from the Stars,” she piped. “Great warriors—Incubu, Bougwan, and Macumazahn the wise—are ye ready? Behold, I am here to do the bidding of my lord the king, and to show you the store of bright stones. Ha! ha! ha!”

“We are ready,” I said, though my mouth felt dry.

“Good, good! Make strong your hearts to bear what ye shall see. Comest thou too, Infadoos, thou who didst betray thy master?”

Infadoos’ brow darkened. “Nay, I come not. It is not for me to enter there. But thou, Gagool, curb thy tongue. Beware how thou dealest with my lords. At thy hands will I require them. If a hair of them be hurt—be’st thou fifty times a witch—thou shalt die.”

“I hear, Infadoos,” she answered, unmoved. “Thou didst ever love big words. When thou wast a babe, I remember, thou didst threaten thine own mother. That was but the other day. Fear not, fear not. I live only to do the bidding of the king. I have done the bidding of many kings—till in the end they did mine. Ha! ha! I go to look upon their faces once more—and Twala’s also! Come on, come on—here is the lamp.”

She produced a large gourd full of oil, fitted with a rush wick. The flame she coaxed from it was small, and the smell of the oil—fatty, plant-sweet, faintly rancid—puffed into the cool air with every movement.

“Art thou coming, Foulata?” Good asked in his villainous Kukuana, improved by much practice under her tuition.

“I fear, my lord,” she said, and her eyes flicked to the black opening.

“Then give me the basket.”

“Nay, my lord,” she answered, gathering herself as though to a duty, “whither thou goest there I go also.”

I could have groaned aloud at that—her presence might become a cruel complication—yet there was no arguing with the kind of loyalty that grows in silence.

Without more, Gagool plunged into the passage. It was wide enough for two abreast and swallowed the lamplight greedily. We followed her voice, thin and eager ahead, our own boots scraping stone. The acoustics changed at once: the passage took our sounds and returned them altered—tightened, multiplied—until our breathing seemed to belong to more than our lungs.

A sudden rush of wings burst past, a leathery brushing against faces.

“Hullo! what’s that?” Good cried. “Somebody hit me in the face!”

“Bats,” I said. “On you go.”

After some fifty paces the blackness thinned. A faint light seeped into the passage, not from before but from above, as though the mountain had been pierced by narrow throats that admitted day grudgingly. Another minute, and we stepped into a chamber that made speech feel profane.

Imagine the nave of the greatest cathedral you ever stood in—windowless, yet dimly lit from high shafts in the roof—and then make it wider, loftier, more indifferent to human measure. The arched ceiling soared a hundred feet above us. The air was cold and clean, with the mineral taste that rises from wet stone. And down the length of that vast natural hall ran rows of gigantic pillars that at first glance seemed carved from ice, but were in truth white spar—stalactites grown into columns with a slow patience that mocked empires.

Some were twenty feet thick at the base and sprang up with a delicate power to the distant roof. Others were unfinished: on the floor lay what looked like broken Grecian columns, while high above, the point of a huge icicle hung dimly, waiting. And the work of making went on even as we watched. From far overhead came the tiniest sound—so small it would be lost in any modern din, in any city’s clangour—yet here it rang clear as a pin dropped in a bowl.

A drop fell: a minute splash.

On some columns the drops came only once in two or three minutes. We stood listening, held by the rhythm. It was a music older than language, a metronome for centuries. I could not help thinking how the health of the mind is shaped by such soundscapes—by the presence of quiet enough to hear what is usually drowned. If our own world learned again to make room for these small natural syllables, perhaps fewer hearts would have to be shouted into numbness.

On one pillar we found a crude carving: a mummy-like figure, and by its head what appeared to be an Egyptian god. Doubtless some ancient labourer’s idle attempt at immortality, scratched at the habitual height—about five feet from the floor. Yet the column upon which it was cut was now only eight feet high, and still growing. Nearly three thousand years must have passed, and still, as we stood there, a drop fell and the stone drank it. A foot to a thousand years, perhaps—an inch and a fraction to a century. Time here was not a river but a drip.

Elsewhere the stalagmites had taken strange forms where the drip wandered: one enormous mass like a pulpit, its outer surface fretted as finely as lace; others resembling beasts half-dreamt. Along the cave walls spread fanlike ivory tracings like frost-leaves on glass. Smaller caves opened out of the main aisle like chapels from a cathedral—some large, some unbelievably tiny, one no bigger than a doll’s house, yet complete with its own miniature icicles, its own faint drip, as though nature refused to alter her grammar for any scale.

I wanted desperately to study it all—to find how the light was admitted, whether those shafts were natural or cut by ancient hands; to read in stone whatever remained of the place’s old use. But Gagool cared nothing for beauty that could not be stolen. She hurried on, her stick tapping, her lamp bobbing, her impatience souring the air.

We followed her to the far end of the great cave, where another doorway waited—square-topped, like the entrances of Egyptian temples.

“Are ye prepared to enter the Place of Death, white men?” she asked, and her pleasure in the question was obvious.

“Lead on, Macduff,” said Good, with a solemnity that sat oddly on his face.

“This is getting rather ghastly,” Sir Henry muttered, peering into the dark beyond. Then, with a forced lightness, “Come on, Quatermain—seniores priores. We mustn’t keep the old lady waiting.”

He stepped aside and left me the front, which I did not bless him for in my heart.

Tap, tap, went Gagool’s stick as she trotted down the passage, chuckling. A presentiment, irrational yet heavy, clung to me. The air seemed to thicken. Even the sound of our steps altered again, muffled, as if the rock itself were holding its breath.

After about twenty paces the passage widened into a gloomy room—some forty feet long, thirty broad, and thirty high—hollowed by hand from the mountain in some ancient time. It was dimmer than the great spar-hall behind. At first glance I made out only a massive stone table running down the room, a colossal white figure at its head, and life-sized white figures seated around.

Then I saw a brown thing seated in the centre of the table.

My eyes adjusted in one sickening second, and I knew what I was looking at. My legs acted on their own, and I was turning tail—out, out of that place—before my mind had finished the thought.

Sir Henry caught me by the collar and held fast.

I am not, as a rule, a nervous man. I have lived long enough to see superstitions for the folly they often are. Yet I confess freely that what lay before us struck through reason and landed somewhere older in the blood. Had Curtis not held me, I believe I should have fled back through the stalactite cave and out into daylight, and all the diamonds in Kimberley would not have bribed me to return.

But he held me. And then his own eyes grew used to the light. His grip loosened. He let me go, and began to mop sweat from his forehead.

Good swore—weakly, as though language had failed him. Foulata flung her arms round his neck and shrieked. Only Gagool laughed, long and loud, as if she were tasting our fear.

At the far end of the table, grasping a great white spear in skeleton fingers, sat Death.

Not an idea of death, not a symbol scratched on stone, but a colossal human skeleton—fifteen feet high or more—leaning forward as though about to rise, one bony hand braced on the table, the other holding the spear lifted high to strike. Its skull thrust toward us, jaws slightly open as if speech hovered there, and the hollow eye-places fixed upon us with a stare that made the room feel occupied by something more than carved spar.

“Great heavens,” I managed at last. “What can it be?”

“And what are those things?” Good asked, pointing with a shaking hand to the white company seated around.

“And what on earth is that thing?” Sir Henry demanded, pointing at the brown object on the table.

“Hee! hee! hee!” Gagool cackled. “To those who enter the Hall of the Dead, evil comes. Hee! hee! hee! ha! ha!”

Then, with a spiteful intimacy, she caught Sir Henry’s coat in her skinny fingers. “Come, Incubu, brave in battle—come and see him thou slewest.”

She led him toward the table. We followed, because some part of us needed to know, as one needs to look at a wound.

She stopped and pointed.

Sir Henry looked—and recoiled with an exclamation, for there, stark and naked, sat the gaunt corpse of Twala, the last king of the Kukuanas. The head Curtis’ battle-axe had shorn from the body rested upon its knees. The vertebrae jutted a full inch above the shrunken flesh of the neck, making the whole thing look horribly like some black double of a grotesque doll, propped for mockery.

And over the corpse was gathered a thin glassy film that made it more appalling, as though the body had been lacquered.

We did not understand at first. Then we heard it—the steady sound, unmistakable in that unnatural quiet:

Drip. Drop. Drip.

From the roof water fell directly onto the severed neck, ran down over the corpse, and slipped away through a tiny hole in the stone table.

The film was not varnish.

Twala’s body was being turned into stone.

A glance at the white figures around the table confirmed it. They had been men—kings—once. Now they were stalactites in the shape of men, iced over in spar, preserved under the drip, their faces dimly visible through the translucent shroud. The Kukuanas, from time immemorial, had kept their royal dead not in earth but in this cold patience of mineral water; they petrified them.

Whether there was a system beyond setting them beneath the falling drops for years upon years I never learned. But there they sat—twenty-seven of them, the last being Ignosi’s father—fixed at their ghastly banquet, wrapped in the slow glass of the mountain, with Death himself for host.

Nothing I have ever seen was more awe-inspiring: that long line of departed royalties, the table like a judgment-slab, the drip’s tiny voice counting centuries, and the colossal skeleton presiding in a posture of perpetual rising. The practice, by the number alone, could not have run much more than four and a quarter centuries—allowing an average reign of fifteen years, and even then not every king could have been brought here. But the White Death at the head of the board was older by far. He was hewn from a single stalactite, and, as a work of art, conceived with a genius that made the mind recoil while it admired. Good, who has an eye for such things, declared the anatomy perfect down to the smallest bones.

I could not help thinking that this terrible figure—ancient, deliberate—might have been the fancy of the same hand that designed the three Colossi outside, and that its presence had suggested to the Kukuanas the very notion of placing their kings here beneath its awful presidency. Or perhaps it was set to frighten away marauders from whatever lay beyond. I do not know. Names and explanations felt feeble in that chamber, as if the dominant lexicon of our own world—its tidy categories, its confident labels—were only another kind of power trying to master what it could never fully translate.

All I can do is tell what we saw.

Such was the White Death, and such were the White Dead.

While we still stood catching our breath in that cold, dreadful hall—where the air smelt of old stone, stale damp, and the sour-sweet taint of ages—Gagool had been busy in her own fashion. The ancient thing, quick as a spider when she chose, had clambered up on to the great table and hobbled along to where Twala lay under the steady drip that fell like a slow, patient cook’s ladle, basting him for some obscene feast. She bent and pressed her withered lips to his dead mouth, as if greeting him at a threshold, then tottered back again, pausing to mutter to one or other of the shrouded kings, like a housewife speaking to elders seated in the dusk of a kraal. At last she squatted beneath the pale stalactite they called the White Death, and began, as well as I could make out, to pray—words swallowed by the vault, by the drip, by the hush.

The cavern held its own sound the way a kitchen holds its own smell. Every little noise—our boots shifting on grit, the faint creak of leather, the lamp’s minute suck of air—felt too loud, an insult to that oppressive silence. I found myself whispering without meaning to, as though any higher voice would wake the dead.

“Now, Gagool,” I said, “lead us to the chamber.”

She slid down from the table and leered up at me. “My lords are not afraid?”

“Lead on.”

“Good, my lords.” She shuffled round behind the White Death. “Here is the chamber. Let my lords light the lamp, and enter.” She set the gourd of oil down and leaned against the rock as if it were her own hearth-wall.

I struck one of our remaining matches and lit the rush wick. The lamp breathed out its thin, animal warmth and a smell of oil—comforting in any other place, like a pantry lamp before dawn, but here it only made the blackness feel thicker. I held the light up and looked for a doorway.

There was nothing—only solid rock.

Gagool’s grin widened. “The way is there, my lords. Ha! ha! ha!”

“Do not jest with us,” I said, and my own voice sounded small.

“I jest not, my lords. See!”

As she pointed, the stone before us began to rise—slowly, smoothly—lifting as though the mountain itself had decided to take a breath. A great slab, door-wide, ten feet high and monstrously thick, drew upward and vanished into the rock above, no doubt into some prepared hollow. There was no scraping, no grinding. The mechanism—counterweights, I guessed, like a simple balance—worked with a terrible patience, and the quiet of it was worse than any clamor. Somewhere, perhaps at a hidden notch, a lever had been nudged; but Gagool kept her hands away, careful as a thief around another man’s larder.

When the mass had gone, a dark opening gaped where it had stood.

My heart began to tremble in my chest as if it were trying to beat its way out. For years—through dust, thirst, fear, and blood—we had marched after a rumor, after an old tale clinging to a dead man’s bones. And now the mouth of that tale was open before us.

“Enter, white men from the Stars,” said Gagool, and she stepped into the doorway with the lamp’s light licking her bent shoulders. “But first hear your servant, Gagool the old. The bright stones that ye will see were dug from the pit over which the Silent Ones are set, and stored here—I know not by whom, for it was done longer ago than even I remember.

“But once has this place been entered since the time that those who hid the stones fled in haste, leaving them behind. The report went down among the people from age to age; but none knew where the chamber was, nor the secret of the door. Then it happened that a white man came over the mountains—perchance he too came ‘from the Stars’—and the king of that day received him. He it is who sits yonder.” She pointed to the fifth king at the table.

“And it came to pass that he and a woman of the country journeyed to this place, and by chance the woman learnt the secret—a thousand years might ye search, but ye should never find it. The white man entered with the woman and found the stones, and he filled with stones the skin of a small goat which she had to hold food. And as he went he took up one more stone, a large one, and held it in his hand.”

She stopped there, and we hung upon her like hungry men scenting stew from another hut.

“Well,” I said, “what happened to Da Silvestra?”

At the name her eyes snapped. “How knowest thou the dead man’s name?” she hissed. Then she spat the story on without waiting for my answer. “None can tell what happened; but the white man was frightened. He flung down the goat-skin and fled with only the one stone in his hand. The king took it, and it is the stone which thou, Macumazahn, didst take from Twala’s brow.”

“Have none entered here since?” I asked, peering into the black passage beyond her.

“None, my lords. Only the secret has been kept, and every king has opened it, though he has not entered. There is a saying: those who enter will die within a moon, even as the white man died in the cave upon the mountain where ye found him. Therefore the kings do not enter. Ha! ha! mine are true words.”

Her gaze held mine, and in that moment a chill went through me as if the drip of the White Death had found my spine. How did she know—how could she speak of names, of fear, of the old Portuguese’s last trembling?

“Enter,” she croaked. “If I speak truth, the goat-skin with stones lies on the floor. And if there is truth that it is death to enter—ye will learn afterwards. Ha! ha! ha!”

She hobbled in with the light. I hesitated.

“Oh, confound it,” said Good. “I’m not going to be frightened by that old devil,” and he plunged after her. Foulata followed him, though the girl’s shoulders shook and her breath came short, as if each step were into colder water. Sir Henry and I went after.

A few yards down the passage, hewn clean out of living rock, Gagool waited. “See, my lords,” she said, holding the lamp low so its flame made long shadows on the walls, “those who stored the treasure fled in haste, and would have guarded against any who should find the secret—but had not time.”

She pointed to large square blocks of stone set across the passage to the height of two courses, as if men had begun to wall it up and then been called away mid-task. Along the sides lay more blocks ready. Most curious of all, there was a heap of mortar, and beside it two trowels—so familiar in shape that for an instant my mind leapt, absurdly, to bricklayers whistling at their work in some modern town, their songs swallowed now by machines and streets. Here, in this older world, the tools had waited in silence for centuries, like a language paused on a half-spoken word.

Foulata faltered then. She said she felt faint and could go no farther, and her voice sounded thin in that narrow stone throat. We sat her down on the unfinished wall, and set the basket of provisions by her—a homely thing, that basket, smelling of dried meat and gourds, as out of place here as a cooking pot in a tomb. We left her there to recover, the passage swallowing our footsteps.

Fifteen paces farther we came upon an elaborately painted wooden door, standing wide open. Whoever had last stood there had not shut it—or had not had time.

Across the threshold lay a skin bag of goat-hide, bulging and heavy, as if it were full of pebbles. The lamp’s light caught it, and the passage seemed suddenly to breathe.

“Hee! hee! white men,” sniggered Gagool. “What did I tell you? The white man fled in haste and dropped the woman’s bag. Look within also and ye will find a water-gourd amongst the stones.”

Good stooped and lifted it. The bag gave a rich, hard jingle—like dry seeds in a gourd, only sharper, more metallic. His whisper was almost reverent. “By Jove! I believe it’s full of diamonds.”

Sir Henry, impatient as a man with hunger in him, snatched the lamp from Gagool. “Go on,” he said shortly, and stepped through.

We followed, forgetting the bag for the moment, and found ourselves in King Solomon’s treasure chamber.

It was a small room, hewn from rock, perhaps ten feet square, the ceiling arched. At first the light showed only stone—dry, pale, close—then the chamber filled in like a picture in dawn. Along one side, stacked one upon another up to the arch, were elephant tusks—fine, creamy ends in ranks, hundreds of them. The air carried a faint, dusty sweetness from that old ivory, like dried bone warmed by sun and time. There was enough there to make a man rich without another ounce of gold.

Opposite the tusks were wooden boxes, painted red, like oversized ammunition cases. Their paint had dulled to a rusty skin.

“There are the diamonds,” I said. “Bring the light.”

Sir Henry held the lamp close to the top box. The lid, rotten with age, had been broken in—no doubt by Da Silvestra. I pushed my hand through the hole and drew it out full—not of diamonds, but of gold coins, oddly shaped, stamped with characters that looked to me like Hebrew.

“Ah,” I said, and my mouth tasted suddenly dry. “We shan’t go back empty-handed. There must be a couple of thousand in each box, and there are eighteen boxes. Wages for workmen, perhaps—money for merchants—Solomon’s payments locked away like grain in a sealed store.”

“Well,” said Good, eyeing the room, “I think that is the lot. I don’t see any diamonds, unless the old Portuguese put them all into his bag.”

Gagool’s head turned, and in the lamplight her face seemed carved from old leather. “Let my lords look yonder where it is darkest, if they would find the stones. There is a nook, and three stone chests in the nook, two sealed and one open.”

Before I translated this to Sir Henry I could not help asking her how she knew it all, if no one had entered here since the white man long ago.

“Ah, Macumazahn, the watcher by night,” she mocked, “ye who dwell in the stars—do ye not know that some live long, and that some have eyes which can see through rock? Ha! ha! ha!”

“Look in that corner, Curtis,” I said.

Sir Henry moved, and then stopped so abruptly that the lamp’s flame wavered. “Hullo, you fellows,” he cried. “Here’s a recess. Great heavens!”

We hurried to him. The nook was shaped like a shallow bay in the wall. Against it stood three stone chests, each about two feet square. Two had lids fitted; the third lay open, its lid resting against its side.

“See!” Sir Henry said again, hoarse now, holding the lamp over it.

For a second we could make nothing out because of the glare—an odd, cold sheen that struck the eyes. Then sight adjusted, and we saw the chest was three-parts full of uncut diamonds, big rough stones piled like pale lumps of sugar, like coarse salt crystals in a butcher’s tub, only with a deeper, inner fire. I stooped and took up a few. They had that peculiar “soapy” feel—like wet pebbles rubbed with fat—and for all their glitter they felt strangely common in the hand, like something you might toss into a stew-pot if you did not know better.

I dropped them with a gasp.

“We are the richest men in the whole world,” I said. “Monte Christo was a fool to us.”

“We shall flood the market with diamonds,” said Good, half-laughing, half-staring.

“Got to get them there first,” Sir Henry answered grimly.

We stood with pale faces, the lamp between us and the open chest below, and it struck me how we looked—like men gathered round a forbidden dish, conspirators not against law but against fate. Behind us Gagool cackled, flitting about in the shadows like a bat.

“There are the bright stones ye love, white men,” she hissed, “as many as ye will. Take them, run them through your fingers—eat of them—hee! hee! drink of them—ha! ha!”

The ridiculous image of swallowing diamonds—of trying to chew them like biltong, of choking on their hard glitter—caught my mind so violently that I began to laugh. Not a sane laugh either, but a wild burst that tore out of me. The others joined in without knowing why, as if the chamber itself had tickled some nerve of madness. Our laughter rang off the stone and came back thin and wrong, a mockery of any human joy—no birds, no wind, no living sound to soften it.

There we stood, shrieking with laughter over treasure that had waited centuries, hoarded by dead hands for dead kings. We—mere wanderers—had come at last, and the ivory and gold and gems lay before us as if a pantry door had been opened.

Then, just as suddenly, the fit passed. The air seemed to thicken again. The lamp hissed softly. The diamonds lay silent.

“Open the other chests,” croaked Gagool. “There are surely more. Take your fill, white lords. Ha! ha! take your fill.”

We set to work. We broke the seals—wax, faded, still clinging like old resin—and hauled at the heavy lids. The second chest was full to the brim as well; no frightened Da Silvestra had emptied that. The third chest was only a quarter full, but the stones were picked—none less than twenty carats, some as large as pigeon-eggs. Holding them up, we saw many were tinged yellow, “off-coloured” as they say at Kimberley.

What we did not see, then, was Gagool’s face as she slid away—her eyes fixed, her lips drawn back—creeping out of the chamber and down the passage towards the great rock door.

Then—sound.

Not our laughter. Not the drip. A cry, ringing up the stone throat, shocking as a sudden scream in a still forest.

It was Foulata’s voice.

“Oh, Bougwan! help! help! the stone falls!”

We ran. The lamp bounced in Sir Henry’s hand, throwing the passage into frantic streaks of light and dark. Another cry—closer—torn and raw:

“Help! help! she has stabbed me!”

We burst out to the place where we had left her.

The door of living rock was closing, sliding down with that same horrible smoothness. It was not three feet from the floor. Near it struggled Foulata and Gagool. Blood ran down Foulata’s leg to her knee, bright and shocking in the lamp’s yellow light, and the smell of it—hot iron—cut through the stale air. Yet the brave girl had both arms locked about the old witch, holding her back from the narrowing gap. Gagool fought like a wild cat, twisting and striking.

Foulata faltered. Gagool tore free.

The girl fell.

Gagool flung herself flat and began to wriggle like a snake beneath the dropping stone. For an instant she was half through—her skull and shoulders scraping the floor.

Then the door caught her.

She screamed—such a scream as I pray never to hear again—and the great weight came down, inch by inch, compressing her against the rock. The sound of it was not loud; it was worse: a drawn-out pressure, a wet, hideous crunch that made the stomach turn. Her shrieks rose and broke, and then, with a final sickening crack, the door shut.

We hurled ourselves against it. It did not move. The mountain might as well have been the sea.

It had all happened in four seconds.

We turned to Foulata.

She lay on the stone, her breath coming in little broken draughts. The lamp showed the wound—deep, fatal—and already her skin had begun to lose its warmth, as though the cavern were drawing it away.

“Ah, Bougwan, I die,” she gasped. “She crept out—Gagool—I did not see her, I was faint—and the door began to fall; then she came back and was looking up the path—I saw her come in through the slowly falling door, and caught her and held her, and she stabbed me, and I die, Bougwan!”

Good’s face crumpled as if his heart had been struck with a hammer. “Poor girl! poor girl!” he sobbed, and fell to kissing her, helpless as a child.

“Bougwan,” she whispered after a pause, “is Macumazahn there? It grows so dark, I cannot see.”

“Here I am, Foulata,” I said, kneeling.

“Be my tongue,” she breathed, “for Bougwan cannot understand me, and before I go into the darkness I would speak to him.”

“Say on,” I told her.

Her voice came in fragments, but the feeling in it was whole. “Say to my lord, Bougwan, that—I love him. And that I am glad to die because I know he cannot cumber his life with such as I am, for the sun may not mate with the darkness, nor the white with the black.

“Say that since I saw him, at times I have felt as though there were a bird in my bosom, which would one day fly hence and sing elsewhere. Even now—though I cannot lift my hand, and my brain grows cold—I do not feel as though my heart were dying; it is so full of love it could live ten thousand years and yet be young.

“Say that if I live again, mayhap I shall see him in the Stars, and that—I will search them all, though perchance there I should still be black and he would—still be white. Say—nay, Macumazahn, say no more, save that I love—Oh, hold me closer, Bougwan, I cannot feel thine arms—oh! oh!”

Her head slipped. The last breath went out of her like the last note of a song swallowed by distance.

“She is dead—she is dead,” Good muttered, rising, tears cutting bright tracks down his cheeks.

“You need not let that trouble you, old fellow,” Sir Henry said, and his voice had a strange harsh steadiness.

“Eh?” Good stared. “What do you mean?”

“I mean,” said Sir Henry, “that you will soon be in a position to join her. Man, don’t you see that we are buried alive?”

Until he spoke the words, the full horror had not entered me—grief had filled all the space in my mind. Now the truth slammed in like a door.

The rock had closed. The only mind that knew its secret—Gagool’s—was crushed to pulp beneath thirty tons of stone. There would be no lever for us, no hidden spring. The slab was five feet thick. You might as well try to open a cliff with your hands.

For some minutes we stood like stunned cattle in a slaughter-yard, with Foulata’s body cooling at our feet, and that unyielding rock before us. In the stillness I could hear our breathing—ragged, too loud—and the faint pulse in my own ears, as if the cave had become an immense shell pressed to my head. I understood then Gagool’s jeer about eating and drinking diamonds. She had meant us to die beside our glittering hoard, gnawing at stones while thirst chewed our throats.

“This will never do,” Sir Henry said hoarsely. “The lamp will soon go out. Let us see if we can’t find the spring that works the rock.”

We flung ourselves at the passage, frantic now, and felt along the stone—up, down, to either side—searching for any projection, any hollow, any secret catch. Our hands came away gritty and smeared with blood from where Foulata had lain. No knob. No spring. Nothing but smooth, unpitying rock.

“Depend on it,” I said at last, and my voice sounded far away to my own ears, “it does not work from the inside. If it did, Gagool would not have risked crawling under. It was the knowledge of that which drove her.”

Sir Henry gave a hard little laugh. “At all events, retribution was swift. Hers was almost as awful an end as ours is likely to be. We can do nothing with the door. Let us go back to the treasure room.”

We turned. The passage seemed longer now, as if despair had stretched it. As we passed the unfinished wall, I saw the basket of provisions where we had left it beside Foulata. I snatched it up—an instinct as old as hunger itself. The smell of dried meat rose from it, smoky and tough, and for a moment it was the only living scent in the whole dead place.

We went back to that accursed chamber that had promised richness and now offered only a slow end. Reverently we carried Foulata’s body in and laid her upon the floor beside the boxes of coin, where the gold lay packed as neatly as grain in a storehouse. Her dark skin in the lamplight looked like polished wood, and her stillness made all our frantic movement seem obscene.

We sat down with our backs against the three stone chests of diamonds—our treasure, our tomb.

“Let us divide the food,” said Sir Henry, “so as to make it last as long as possible.”

We did it carefully, like men portioning out flour in a famine. There was biltong—dry strips of game flesh, hard as leather, smelling of smoke and salt—there were two gourds of water, each not more than a quart. We reckoned it out: four infinitesimal meals apiece, enough perhaps to keep the body standing for two days.

“Now,” said Sir Henry grimly, “let us eat and drink, for to-morrow we die.”

We chewed the biltong. It drew saliva and then seemed to steal it, leaving the mouth drier than before. The taste was familiar—salt, smoke, the wildness of the veld clinging to it—and it struck me with a sharp, bitter irony that this last comfort should be something so plain and homely, while diamonds lay in heaps at our heels. We each took a sip of water, precious as a sacrament, and the coolness on my tongue was almost painful.

Then we rose and examined the chamber systematically, as if method could argue with fate. We sounded the walls, the floor, listening for any hollowness. The rock gave back only dull, dead answers. A treasure chamber is not built with back doors.

The lamp began to burn low. The smell of its dying oil thickened. Shadows pooled deeper in the corners, and the room’s silence grew heavier—not even the drip reached us clearly here, only a distant, intermittent whisper as though the mountain were breathing in sleep.

“Quatermain,” Sir Henry said, “what is the time—your watch goes?”

I drew it out and looked. Six o’clock. We had entered the cave at eleven.

“Infadoos will miss us,” I said, clinging to the thought as a man clings to a twig in flood. “If we do not return to-night, he will search for us in the morning.”

“He may search in vain,” Sir Henry answered. “He does not know the secret of the door, nor even where it is. No living person knew it yesterday, except Gagool. To-day no one knows it. Even if he found the door, he could not break it down. All the Kukuana army could not break through five feet of living rock. My friends, I see nothing for it but to bow ourselves to the will of the Almighty. The search for treasure has brought many to a bad end; we shall go to swell their number.”

The lamp dimmed further, then suddenly flared as if with a last stubborn pride, throwing the chamber into sharp relief. I saw everything at once: the ivory tusks stacked like a giant’s bones; the red-painted boxes; the goat-skin bag that jingled with stolen centuries; the diamonds glimmering with their cold, tasteless light; Foulata’s body laid out quiet among riches that could not buy her one more breath; and the three of us—wan-faced men—sitting against stone chests as against gravestones, waiting for thirst to do its work.

Then the flame sank, shivered, and went out.

And the dark, at last, was complete.

No tongue I have can compass the night that followed our sealing in that stone belly of the mountain. Weariness, like a blunt mercy, did press our eyelids down at whiles; yet for me sleep came in thin scraps, shying off as a wild thing shies from fire.

It was not only the thought of what waited us—thirst first, then the slow failing of the heart, then that last indignity of the body in darkness. It was the silence. Not the common quiet a man meets in a veld at midnight, where the grass whispers to itself and some far jackal threads his thin cry through the stars; not even the hush of a snowfield, where one’s own breath seems loud. This was silence made solid—so complete it had weight, so perfect it seemed to press on the ears like a hand.

We lay under a mountain’s white cap, thousands upon thousands of feet of rock and frozen air above us; and though the dawn might be kindling up there on the snow-wreaths, though a breeze might be combing the ridges into streaming banners, not the faintest syllable of it reached our living grave. A long passage and yards of stone shut us off even from the great chamber where the dead kings sat—dead folk who make no sound. We knew it, for we lay near poor Foulata, and death had already taken her voice.

In that oppressive stillness my mind, like a tongue worrying a sore tooth, kept returning to the same bitter jest: that about us, stacked in chests, lay wealth enough to load a nation’s coffers and buy ships and guns and whole cities’ worth of noise—yet we would have given it all, every glittering stone, for one rude mouthful of water from a gourd, for one crust, for one chance—only a chance—of crawling back to daylight. Soon, I thought, we should have been glad to exchange it for a swallow; later still, even for the favour of dying quickly. There is no jewel that can be chewed, no diamond that melts on the tongue.

At last Sir Henry’s voice came, low but startling in that stillness, as if speech were a blasphemy there.

“Good,” he said, “how many matches have you?”

“Eight, Curtis.”

“Strike one. Let us see the time.”

Good fumbled, the sulphur rasped, and the sudden flare near blinded us after so long a blackness. It was five o’clock by my watch. Somewhere far above our heads, in a world of air and colour, morning must be lifting her lids.

“We had better eat,” I said, for I have ever held that a man must keep his strength like a miser keeps coin, spending it slowly. “If we can swallow, we can think.”

“What’s the use of eating?” Good answered, his voice harsh and raw, as if his throat were lined with hot sand. “The sooner it’s done, the better.”

“While there is life there is hope,” said Sir Henry; and he spoke as he always did, quietly, as though hope were a thing you might lay a hand upon and hold steady.

So we chewed a little biltong—dry as old hide, yet how precious, how full of remembered smoke and salt when one’s mouth is parched—and we sipped our water, each swallow small, measured, as a careful cook measures spice when there is little left in the jar. Then time thickened again, slow and tasteless.

Presently Sir Henry bade us creep down the passage toward the stone door and hail, on the foolish chance that some sound might leak through. Good, with a sailor’s lungs, went, groping with one hand, and set up such yells as might have raised a mutiny on a deck. In that dead air they seemed to be devoured the moment they were born; for all the answer they got, he might as well have been a mosquito.

He came back thirstier for the effort and drank, and we ceased that folly, because even shouting spends water—if not from the skin, then from the throat.

Then came the worst part: the waiting. Our backs against chests of stones that could not buy us one draught, our hands empty, our mouths growing furred, our thoughts circling like trapped birds. In that helplessness my courage broke clean. I laid my head against Sir Henry’s shoulder—broad as a shield, warm with life—and I wept like a child, the tears burning as they ran. I heard Good on the other side gulping and cursing himself under his breath for it.

Sir Henry—God bless the man—forgot his own misery to nurse ours. He spoke softly, telling of men buried in collapsed workings, of sailors shut in holds, of miners who had gnawed leather and lived, of miracles that come like a loaf slid under a door. When those stories failed, he tried another kind of comfort: that death comes to all, that we were but taking the road a little sooner, that exhaustion is merciful (which it is not, for thirst has sharp teeth). And then, in that diffident way of his, as though asking pardon for speaking of such things, he said we should commend ourselves to a higher Power. I did so with all the force I had left, for when a man’s mouth is dry and the dark is thick, prayer seems less like a pious habit and more like a handhold.

Thus the hours wore, though in that blackness “day” and “night” were only names. When I struck another match it showed seven o’clock. We ate and drank again—each morsel chewed until it was almost memory, each drop made to last on the tongue—and as I swallowed, a small, sensible thought rose up in me like a spark in ash.

“How is it,” I said, “that the air stays fresh here? It is heavy, but it is not foul.”

Good started as if stung.

“Great heavens!” he cried. “Of course. If there were no air moving, we’d have been poisoned when first we came. It can’t come through that door—air-tight as a drum. It must come from somewhere. Come—let’s feel for it.”

You could have fed us on that mere notion of a draught. It was as if, after gnawing on leather, a man suddenly smells broth.

Down we went on hands and knees, crawling among tusks and chests and carved stone, pressing our cheeks and palms to cracks, searching for the faintest coolness. Once my hand touched something cold and smooth and I snatched it back with a shudder; it was Foulata’s face. For an hour we hunted, bruising heads on ivory and rock, until Sir Henry and I, sore and dulled, were ready to collapse. But Good kept at it with a stubborn kind of cheer.

“Better than sitting,” he muttered.

Presently he called us, his voice tight.

“Here! Come here.”

We scrambled to him.

“Quatermain—put your hand where mine is. Feel anything?”

I held my breath, laid my fingers down, and felt—yes—there was the faintest tickle of air, as though the mountain itself were drawing breath.

“I think—yes—I do,” I whispered.

“Now listen.”

He rose and stamped. The sound came back wrong—hollow, as if the stone were a lid.

With hands that shook, I struck a match—three left now, and each one a small life in itself. The flame showed we were in the far corner, tucked into an angle where sound and sight had cheated us before. There, cut clean in the rock floor, ran a seam; and set level with the stone was a ring—stone also, not iron—waiting like a handle on a buried pot.

We said nothing. Our hearts did all the speaking.

Good drew his knife—the back of it had a little hooked tool for picking stones from a horse’s hoof—and he scraped around the ring until he could lever it up. It moved obediently, as stone will after centuries if it has not rusted. He tugged. Nothing.

I tried, straining till my wrists hurt. Nothing.

Sir Henry tried, and though his shoulders bunched like a bull’s, the slab did not give.

Good scraped again around the seam where the air came, then wiped his brow with a hand that shook less now, because hope was a steadier thing than fear.

“Now, Curtis,” he said, “you’re as strong as two. Put your back into it. Stop.”

He took off his black silk handkerchief—neat to the last, even in a tomb—and ran it through the ring to make a hold.

“Quatermain, get round him. When I say, pull as if the devil’s behind you. Now.”

Sir Henry heaved. Good and I hauled with him, my arms locked about Sir Henry’s middle, my feet scraping for purchase on rock dust.

“Heave!” gasped Sir Henry. “It’s giving—”

I heard, horribly, the crack and creak of muscle in his back as if a rope were fraying. Then came a grating, a sudden rush of cold air up at our faces, and we all went over backwards as the flag-stone came free and toppled.

“Light a match,” Sir Henry said when we had scrambled clear, panting. “Careful.”

I did. And there, blessed as bread, was the first step of a stone stair dropping down into blackness.

“What now?” Good asked.

“Follow it,” said Sir Henry. “And trust Providence.”

“Stop,” he added, practical even in ecstasy. “Quatermain—fetch what biltong and water are left. We may need them.”

I crept back among the chests to our place. The jewels lay open-mouthed, winking faintly when my matchlight reached them, like cold fish-eyes. For twenty-eight hours those stones had meant nothing but mockery. Yet habit is a hard master, and I thought, If we do get out, it will be a strange thing to have come this far and carry nothing but hunger.

So I plunged my fist into the nearest chest and filled every pocket I had, not daintily but by handfuls, as one might cram meal into a sack before a drought. Then, remembering the third chest, I topped up with a few of the biggest—heavy, blunt lumps that sat in the palm like ice. By an afterthought I stuffed Foulata’s basket—now almost empty save for a gourd and a little biltong—with diamonds until it felt like carrying a pot of stones.

“I say!” I called. “Won’t you take some? I’ve filled my pockets and the basket.”

“Oh, come on, Quatermain—and hang the diamonds!” Sir Henry answered. “I hope I never see another.”

Good said nothing. He stood near where Foulata lay, and though I could not see his face, I knew he was taking leave of what was left of her. In that silence, grief had its own soundless weight.

If you, sitting by a cheerful hearth with supper on the table, could see the heaps we abandoned, you might think us mad. Yet I tell you truly: after a day and more of thirst and darkness, a man would not burden himself with jewels while crawling into unknown tunnels, save that old habit in me that hates leaving value behind when there is the least chance of carrying it away.

Sir Henry stood on the first step.

“Steady. I go first.”

“Mind your footing,” I said. “There may be a pit under us.”

“More like another chamber,” he answered, and began descending, counting under his breath.

“Fifteen,” he said, and stopped. “Bottom. Thank God. A passage, I think. Follow.”

Good went next, and I last with the basket. At the bottom I struck one of the two matches remaining. By that thin light we saw we stood in a narrow tunnel running right and left at right angles to the stair. The flame bit my fingers and died before more could be made out.

Then came the cruel choice: which way? One turn might lead to air; the other to nothing.

Good’s mind—sharp as a cook’s nose over a simmering pot—caught a detail.

“When you lit it,” he said, “the flame bent to the left. There’s a draught. Go against it. Air draws in, not out.”

We took his counsel. Feeling along the wall, testing the ground with each step, we left that accursed treasure chamber behind. If any living man ever enters it again—and I doubt it—he will find the open chests, the empty lamp, and the white bones of poor Foulata; and perhaps, if he listens, he will hear only that same thick silence we escaped.

For a quarter-hour we groped, then the passage turned sharp, or met another. We took it, then another, and another, until it seemed the mountain was nothing but corridors, like a great ant-hill of stone. We went on for hours, stumbling, turning, losing all sense of direction. We thought they must be old mine-workings, where men had chased the glittering ore like hunger through rock, driving galleries and adits wherever the vein tempted them. The very idea made my mouth ache: men digging for stones while their tongues dried in dust.

At length we halted, worn out, hope sickening within us. We ate the last strip of biltong—chewed it slowly, as a hungry man chews a memory of fat and smoke—and drank the last sup of water. Our throats felt like lime-kilns, baked and cracking. It seemed we had fled death in the treasure chamber only to meet him again in these blind tunnels.

As we stood, despair settling down like dust, I thought I heard something. Not imagined, not the beating of blood in the ear—something faint, far, yet real. I held up my hand and the others listened.

A murmur—soft, continuous.

No words can tell the blessedness of that sound after so long in absolute stillness, for sound itself felt like life returning.

“By heaven,” said Good, and his voice shook, “it’s running water.”

“Come on,” said Sir Henry.

We went toward it, the murmur guiding us as a scent guides a dog. I set the heavy basket down, my shoulders begging for mercy—then picked it up again. A foolish thought, yet stubborn: if we must die, let it be with riches at least. Hunger makes a man strange.

The sound grew clearer: a swirl, a rush. Yet how could there be running water in the belly of rock? Nearer—now it was loud in the quiet. Good, leading, said hoarsely, “I can smell it.”

“Gently,” Sir Henry warned. “We must be close.”

Splash! A cry—Good had vanished.

“Good!” we shouted, our hearts jumping into our throats. “Where are you?”

“All right,” came his voice, choky and strained. “I’ve got a rock. Strike a light—show me where you are.”

I lit our last match. The flame trembled, and in its sickly circle we saw dark water rushing at our feet. How wide the stream was we could not tell, but out in it Good’s shape clung to a projection.

“Stand clear!” he called. “I must swim for it.”

We heard him plunge, then the slap and drag of a fierce struggle. The current ran like a mill-race; you could hear its haste, feel its cold breath. A moment later his hand caught Sir Henry’s outstretched arm and we hauled him up, dripping and gasping.

“My word,” he choked, “that was touch and go. If I hadn’t caught that rock—and if I couldn’t swim—I’d have been done. No bottom I could feel. It runs like fury.”

We dared not creep along the bank in darkness for fear of stepping into it again. But thirst has its own courage. We knelt and drank our fill, that sweet cold water tasting like salvation, and washed our faces as best we could, mud and dust streaking away. The sound of it—splash, swallow, breath—was music after the tomb-quiet, and in that moment I understood how a man’s mind is fed by sound as much as by food. Silence had starved us.

Then, with Good unpleasantly wet in front, we turned back from that subterranean river—our African Styx, if you like—and retraced our steps until we found another tunnel branching right.

“We may as well take it,” Sir Henry said wearily. “All roads are alike here. We can only go on till we drop.”

On we stumbled for a long, long while, Sir Henry now leading, our feet dragging. Again I thought of abandoning the basket; again I did not. The diamonds knocked dully together with each step, a small, domestic sound almost—like dried beans shaken in a gourd—strangely homely in that place of death.

Suddenly Sir Henry stopped so sharp we bumped into him.

“Look,” he whispered. “Is my brain going—or is that light?”

We stared. Far ahead, no larger than a cottage window-pane, was a faint glimmer. So faint that in any ordinary place you might have missed it; but our eyes, trained by days of blackness, seized it greedily.

We pushed on. The patch grew. A minute more and a breath of real air, living air, came fanning our cheeks—air that tasted of earth and leaf-mould instead of stone.

The tunnel narrowed. Sir Henry went to his knees. Smaller still, until it was no bigger than a fox’s earth. And now, mind you, it was earth—soft, crumbly—no longer rock. A squeeze, a scrape, a struggle—Sir Henry wriggled out, then Good, then I, dragging Foulata’s heavy basket after me.

Above us—stars. Cool, vast, indifferent, and yet never did heaven look so friendly. The air filled our lungs like a draught of clear broth after famine.

Then something gave beneath us and we were rolling—over and over—through grass and bushes and soft wet soil, the slope taking us like a hand. The basket caught in something and jerked me to a stop. I sat up and shouted. An answering cry came from below where Sir Henry’s career had been checked by level ground. I scrambled down and found him shaken but unhurt. We found Good a little way off, jammed in a forked root, bruised and dazed, but soon swearing feebly as life returned.

We sat together there on the grass, and the revulsion of it was so great that I think we cried with joy, not ashamed. We had come out of that dungeon that had been so near to our grave. Some merciful Power must have guided our steps to that jackal hole—what else could it be?—at the tunnel’s end.

And yonder, on the mountain’s shoulder, the dawn we had never thought to see again was blushing rosy red, like fire under ash. Grey light stole down the slopes. We saw then that we were near the bottom of the vast pit before the cave entrance; and on its rim, dim against the paling sky, sat the three Colossi, solemn as judges.

Those countless passages we had wandered in the night must, in old times, have had their use in the working of the diamond mine. As for the river running in the mountain’s guts, Heaven only knows its source or end. I have no desire to learn.

As the light strengthened we looked at one another, and such scarecrows I hope never to see again: gaunt cheeks, hollow eyes, faces plastered with dust and mud, lips cracked, bruises darkening, blood dried in lines—fear written plain. Yet even then, absurdly, Good’s eye-glass still sat in his eye as if nothing on earth—not darkness, not drowning, not tumbling down a hill—could part him from it.

Fearing our limbs would stiffen, we rose and began, with slow painful steps, to climb the pit’s sloping side, dragging ourselves by roots and grasses through the blue clay. For more than an hour we toiled. And now the basket was no burden to be abandoned; it was a pledge of having endured, a proof in the hand that the mountain had not swallowed us for nothing. Nothing but death would have parted me from it.

At last we gained the great road on the side opposite the Colossi. A hundred yards away a fire burned before some huts. Shapes moved around it. Smoke drifted up with the familiar smell of wood and ash—how human a smell, how full of the memory of cooking pots and evening talk. We staggered toward it, supporting one another and stopping every few paces.

One of the figures rose, saw us, and fell flat, crying out in terror, as though ghosts had stepped from the earth.

“Infadoos! Infadoos!” we called. “It is we—thy friends!”

He got up, ran to us, stared wildly, shaking like a leaf.

“Oh, my lords, my lords,” he cried, and his voice broke, “it is indeed you—come back from the dead—come back from the dead!”

And the old warrior flung himself down and clasped Sir Henry’s knees, weeping aloud for joy.

Ten days after that morning which had very near been our last, we were back again in the huts at Loo. It is a strange thing to write, but we were not much the worse in body for what we had passed through. My beard, that had gone into the mountain black-stubbled, came out sprinkled as if dusted with ash, and I doubt it will ever quite lose that added grey. Good, too, carried a change no soap and razor could mend. Since Foulata’s death, something in him had fallen quiet—as though a cooking-fire had been quenched and the man sat still listening for the crackle that would not come again.

For my own part, speaking as an older hand who has seen what tangles men can make of their hearts, I will admit—coldly, perhaps—that her dying spared us trouble. She was no ordinary girl: there was a kind of proud grace in her, a delicacy of feeling that did not sit clumsily upon her as it often does where life is hard. Yet she herself had spoken the truth with a simplicity more bitter than any sermon: could the sun mate with darkness, or the white with the black? Some things do not translate; you can carry the words across, but the meaning breaks on the way.

We never again forced ourselves into Solomon’s treasure place. When we had slept away the worst of our exhaustion—forty-eight hours of it—we went down into the great pit where we had rolled like stones, hoping to find the hole by which we had crawled out of the mountain’s belly. Rain had fallen since then and wiped our spoor clean as a cook wipes a board before kneading. Besides, the walls of the pit were honeycombed with ant-bear tunnels and other holes, so that the cliff-face looked like a cheese riddled by mice. Which of those openings had been our deliverance? There was no telling.

On the day before we were to start back to Loo, a restlessness seized us, and we went once more through the cool, wet breath of the stalactite cave, even back into the Chamber of the Dead. The air there has a taste, thin and mineral, like water that has stood long in a stone jar. Passing beneath the spear of the White Death, we stood and looked at the mass of rock that had cut us off from life. Behind that rock lay the coffers—wealth piled up until it was no longer wealth but a kind of madness in stone; and there, crushed and flattened, were the remains of Gagool, that ancient malice which had clung to the land like a curse in an old song no one dares to sing aloud. And there, too—though we could not see it—was the tomb that had been the portal, and the girl’s bones, and all that unspent splendour waiting in the dark.

We examined the “rock” until our hands were sore with scraping, but we could find no seam, no join of the sliding door, no hint of the trick that moved it. It was mechanism, yes, but so plain, so massive, so perfectly concealed that it mocked us. The very age of it seemed to stare back—an age that could do such things without leaving any clue for another tongue, another mind, to read. At last we gave it up, half in anger, half in fear; for I will say honestly that if the stone had lifted of its own accord at that moment, I doubt we should have had the stomach to step over what was left of Gagool and go in again, even with the sure promise of diamonds as big as pigeons’ eggs.

And yet I could have wept at leaving it. The greatest treasure, most likely, that has ever been stored in one place since men first learned to covet, lay there beyond reach. Only dynamite would have eaten through five feet of living rock, and we had none. So we left it, and I dare say it will remain, for I have a feeling—call it fancy if you like—that those stones will never shine on any living woman’s throat. They, and Foulata’s bones, will keep cold company until the last silence.

Still, disappointment was ungrateful of us, for we had not come away empty-handed. By a lucky thought I had stuffed the broad pockets of my old shooting-coat and trousers with gems before we escaped, and filled Foulata’s basket as well, though the water-bottle took up part of its space. During our tumble down the pit’s side a good many stones shook loose and were lost—several of the largest, that I had foolishly jammed on top in my coat. But a great quantity remained. Ninety-three large diamonds we counted later, from over two hundred carats down to seventy, besides a multitude of smaller ones. Coat and basket together held enough to make us, if not American millionaires, at least men of very considerable means—and to leave each of us stones enough for the finest sets of jewels in Europe. It is odd to think of, when I remember the taste of that cave-air and the sound—no, the absence of sound—around those coffers. Wealth so vast, and yet the ears hear only dripping water and one’s own breath.

When we reached Loo we were welcomed by Ignosi with real warmth. We found him in good health, already busy as a man must be who has taken a throne by spear and must now keep it by law. He was consolidating his power, shifting captains, rebuilding regiments thinned in the struggle with Twala. He listened to our tale with eyes that held it as a pot holds broth, taking in every bit; but when we told him of Gagool’s end, his face darkened into thought.

“Come hither,” he called to a very old Induna who sat with others in a circle, just beyond earshot.

The ancient man rose slowly, like a tree that has been leaning into the wind for many seasons, came forward, saluted, and sat.

“Thou art aged,” said Ignosi.

“Ay, my lord the king! Thy father’s father and I were born on the same day.”

“Tell me. When thou wast little, didst thou know Gagaoola the witch doctress?”

“Ay, my lord the king!”

“How was she then—young, like thee?”

“Not so, my lord the king! She was even as she is now and as she was in the days of my great grandfather before me; old and dried, very ugly, and full of wickedness.”

“She is no more; she is dead.”

“So, O king! then is an ancient curse taken from the land.”

“Go!”

“Koom! I go, Black Puppy, who tore out the old dog’s throat. Koom!”

When the old man had gone, Ignosi looked at us gravely. “Ye see, my brothers,” he said, “this was a strange woman, and I rejoice that she is dead. She would have let you die in the dark place, and mayhap afterwards she had found a way to slay me, as she found a way to slay my father, and set up Twala, whom her black heart loved, in his place. Now go on with the tale; surely there never was its like!”

So I told it all—our escape, our climb, our fall, the blind groping that ended in air and day—because we had agreed amongst ourselves that I should speak for the three. And when I had done, I chose my time, as one chooses the moment to take bread from the fire before it burns.

“And now, Ignosi,” I said, “the time has come for us to bid thee farewell, and start to see our own land once more. Behold, Ignosi, thou camest with us a servant, and now we leave thee a mighty king. If thou art grateful to us, remember to do even as thou didst promise: to rule justly, to respect the law, and to put none to death without a cause. So shalt thou prosper. To-morrow, at break of day, Ignosi, thou wilt give us an escort who shall lead us across the mountains. Is it not so, O king?”

He covered his face with his hands for a while, and in that hush I became aware of the sounds outside the hut—the distant cough of cattle, the soft pad of feet on dust, a child’s thin laugh. Power sits in a kraal as it sits in a kitchen: among small noises and daily needs. At last he spoke.

“My heart is sore,” he said; “your words split my heart in twain. What have I done to you, Incubu, Macumazahn, and Bougwan, that ye should leave me desolate? Ye who stood by me in rebellion and in battle, will ye leave me in the day of peace and victory? What will ye—wives? Choose from among the maidens! A place to live in? Behold, the land is yours as far as ye can see. The white man’s houses? Ye shall teach my people how to build them. Cattle for beef and milk? Every married man shall bring you an ox or a cow. Wild game to hunt? Does not the elephant walk through my forests, and the river-horse sleep in the reeds? Would ye make war? My Impis wait your word. If there is anything more which I can give, that will I give you.”

“Nay, Ignosi,” I answered, “we want none of these things; we would seek our own place.”

Then he flamed, wounded pride turning quickly to accusation as a fire takes dry grass. “Now do I learn,” he said bitterly, “that ye love the bright stones more than me, your friend. Ye have the stones; now ye would go to Natal and across the moving black water and sell them, and be rich, as it is the desire of a white man’s heart to be. Cursed for your sake be the white stones, and cursed he who seeks them. Death shall it be to him who sets foot in the place of Death to find them. I have spoken. White men, ye can go.”

I put my hand upon his arm. He was trembling; the king and the lonely man were at war inside him.

“Ignosi,” I said, “tell us, when thou didst wander in Zululand, and among the white people of Natal, did not thine heart turn to the land thy mother told thee of, thy native place, where thou didst see the light, and play when thou wast little, the land where thy place was?”

“It was even so, Macumazahn.”

“In like manner, Ignosi, do our hearts turn to our land and to our own place.”

There was a silence, full as a pot held just below boiling. When he spoke again, his voice had changed, and with it the whole air in the hut.

“I do perceive that now as ever thy words are wise and full of reason, Macumazahn; that which flies in the air loves not to run along the ground; the white man loves not to live on the level of the black or to house among his kraals. Well, ye must go, and leave my heart sore, because ye will be as dead to me, since from where ye are no tidings can come to me.

“But listen, and let all your brothers know my words. No other white man shall cross the mountains, even if any man live to come so far. I will see no traders with their guns and gin. My people shall fight with the spear, and drink water, like their forefathers before them. I will have no praying-men to put a fear of death into men’s hearts, to stir them up against the law of the king, and make a path for the white folk who follow to run on. If a white man comes to my gates I will send him back; if a hundred come I will push them back; if armies come, I will make war on them with all my strength, and they shall not prevail against me. None shall ever seek for the shining stones: no, not an army, for if they come I will send a regiment and fill up the pit, and break down the white columns in the caves and choke them with rocks, so that none can reach even to that door of which ye speak, and whereof the way to move it is lost. But for you three, Incubu, Macumazahn, and Bougwan, the path is always open; for, behold, ye are dearer to me than aught that breathes.

“And ye would go. Infadoos, my uncle, and my Induna, shall take you by the hand and guide you with a regiment. There is, as I have learned, another way across the mountains that he shall show you. Farewell, my brothers, brave white men. See me no more, for I have no heart to bear it. Behold! I make a decree, and it shall be published from the mountains to the mountains; your names, Incubu, Macumazahn, and Bougwan, shall be hlonipa even as the names of dead kings, and he who speaks them shall die. So shall your memory be preserved in the land for ever.

“Go now, ere my eyes rain tears like a woman’s. At times as ye look back down the path of life, or when ye are old and gather yourselves together to crouch before the fire, because for you the sun has no more heat, ye will think of how we stood shoulder to shoulder, in that great battle which thy wise words planned, Macumazahn; of how thou wast the point of the horn that galled Twala’s flank, Bougwan; whilst thou stood in the ring of the Greys, Incubu, and men went down before thine axe like corn before a sickle; ay, and of how thou didst break that wild bull Twala’s strength, and bring his pride to dust. Fare ye well for ever, Incubu, Macumazahn, and Bougwan, my lords and my friends.”

He rose and looked at us a few seconds—long enough to fix our faces in his mind as one fixes a tune by humming it once through. Then he threw the corner of his karross over his head, hiding his face. We went out in silence.

At dawn next day we left Loo, escorted by our old friend Infadoos—heartsick at parting—and by the regiment of the Buffaloes. Though the light was barely up, the main street was lined with people. They gave us the royal salute as we passed at the regiment’s head; and the women blessed us for ridding the land of Twala, throwing flowers before our feet. The petals were crushed under sandals and boots and spear-butts, giving off that bruised green sweetness that lilies and other soft things have when they are broken—life’s brief fragrance trampled into the road.

One ludicrous thing happened, and I was glad of it, for laughter is a kind of food when the heart is tight. Just before we reached the edge of the town, a pretty young girl ran out with lilies in her hands and offered them to Good—somehow all the women liked Good; I believe his eye-glass and solitary whisker gave him a ridiculous glamour—and then she said she had a boon to ask.

“Speak on,” said Good.

“Let my lord show his servant his beautiful white legs, that his servant may look upon them, and remember them all her days, and tell of them to her children; his servant has travelled four days’ journey to see them, for the fame of them has gone throughout the land.”

“I’ll be hanged if I do!” cried Good, scandalised.

“Come, come, my dear fellow,” said Sir Henry, “you can’t refuse to oblige a lady.”

“I won’t,” Good insisted, stiff as a ramrod. “It is positively indecent.”

But in the end he yielded, red with vexation, and drew his trousers up to the knee. The women broke into notes of admiration so frank and joyous that the very air seemed to ring with it, and the girl looked as if she had been given a feast. In that absurd guise he had to walk until we were clear of the town. Good’s legs, I suspect, will never again enjoy such fame. The Kukuanas tired more or less of his “melting teeth” and even of his “transparent eye,” but of his legs—never.

As we travelled, Infadoos told us of another way across the mountains, north of Solomon’s Great Road: a place where one might climb down the cliff-wall that divides Kukuanaland from the desert, not far from where the huge shapes of Sheba’s Breasts rise like watchers. It seemed that a little over two years before, Kukuana hunters searching for ostriches—whose plumes they prize for their war head-dresses—had descended by this path and, led far from the mountains, were nearly undone by thirst. Seeing trees on the horizon, they had struggled toward them and found a wide oasis, fertile and well-watered. By way of this oasis, Infadoos said, we might return, escaping the worst rigours of the mountain pass. Some of those very hunters now came with us as guides, and they said that from the oasis other green spots could be seen far out in the waste.

As he spoke, I found myself thinking of Ignosi’s mother, wandering with her child—how any woman could survive that crossing. Perhaps, as with so many things in life, the story changes its meaning when told in another path’s language: she may have come this way, as Hagar went into the wilderness, and been found by hunters before thirst finished her. There is always some unrecorded kindness, some small domestic mercy—water carried, fire lit, a mouth fed—that saves a legend from ending as a death-song.

We travelled easily, and on the fourth night we found ourselves once more on the mountain crest. At our feet the desert rolled away in sandy billows, and we were, by Infadoos’ reckoning, some twenty-five miles north of Sheba’s Breasts. At dawn next day the guides led us to the lip of a fearful chasm down which we were to descend to the plain two thousand feet below. There we bade farewell to Infadoos, that true friend and tough old warrior. He wished us all good with great solemnity, and grief swelled in him until he near wept.

“Never, my lords,” he said, “shall mine old eyes see the like of you again. Ah! the way that Incubu cut his men down in the battle! Ah! for the sight of that stroke with which he swept off my brother Twala’s head! It was beautiful—beautiful! I may never hope to see such another, except perchance in happy dreams.”

We were sorry indeed to part from him. Good was so moved that he gave him, as a keepsake—what do you think?—an eye-glass. Later we discovered it was a spare. Infadoos was delighted, seeing at once how such a wonder would fatten his prestige. After several attempts, and much squinting and muttering, he managed to screw it into his own eye. Anything more absurd than that old warrior—leopard-skin cloak, black ostrich plumes, stern face—peering through a white man’s glass, I have never seen. The thing did not belong to his world, and yet he wore it as proudly as if it had grown there; a new word forced into an old mouth.

Then, after he had seen that our guides were properly laden with water and provisions, and after the Buffaloes had sent a thundering farewell salute crashing along the cliff, we wrung his hand and began the descent.

It was a hard business—hands scraping stone, muscles shaking, breath coming in hot pulls that tasted of dust and iron. But by evening we were at the bottom without accident, and made a small fire whose smoke rose straight up into the cold, still air beneath the beetling cliffs.

“Do you know,” Sir Henry said, as we sat watching the flames and looking up at that wall of rock, “I think there are worse places than Kukuanaland in the world, and I have known unhappier times than the last month or two, though I have never spent such queer ones. Eh, you fellows?”

“I almost wish I were back,” said Good, and sighed as if he could hear again the women’s admiration in Loo and, behind it, another voice that was gone.

As for myself, I reflected that all’s well that ends well; but in a long life of shaves I never had such shaves as those we had lately endured. The memory of that battle can still make me feel cold all over, and our time in the treasure chamber—there are things the tongue can name, but never properly convey, because the body remembers them first: the pressure of darkness, the thirst that turns the mouth to leather, the sound of one drop of water falling as if the whole world were reduced to that single note.

Next morning we set out across the desert. Our five guides carried a good supply of water, slung and stoppered, precious as blood. We marched until night and slept in the open, and marched again at dawn. The desert has its own acoustic tyranny: in the day, the hush is so wide it presses on the ears; at night, each small sound—foot on sand, the click of a pebble, the faint hiss of wind—seems magnified, as though the world had been emptied to make room for it. It is a relief, in its way, after the crowded noises of a town; and yet it can unman you, for silence, too, can be a kind of roar.

By noon on the third day we saw trees darkening the horizon—the oasis the hunters had promised.
